is the root system associated to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
.

In particular, from
Theorem

2.6.1
it follows that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(K)^{\circ}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
.

When
H
is isomorphic to one of
\PSL_{2}(25)
,
\PSL_{2}(37)
,
\PSL_{2}(39)
, it will follow from
Lemma

2.8.1
and
Lemma

2.8.2
that
B_{0}
is always contained in a maximal torus
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
.

Proposition 2.9.4
.

Suppose there are
B_{1},B_{2}\simeq B
contained in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, such that
B_{1}^{\prime},B_{2}^{\prime}\simeq B_{0}
are
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
, with
B_{i}=B_{i}^{\prime}\rtimes w_{i}
. In particular, we may assume
B_{1}^{\prime}=B_{2}^{\prime}=B_{1}\cap B_{2}=B_{0}\subset\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
. If for
i=1,2

B_{i}^{\prime}
is regular and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(w_{i})
is finite, then
B_{1},B_{2}
are
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
.

Proof.

Since
B_{0}
is regular,
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}^{\circ}(B_{0})=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
for some maximal torus
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\supset B_{0}
.

It is straightforward to prove that
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B_{0})\subseteq N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}^{\circ}(B_{0}))
: let
g\in N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B_{0})
,
b\in B_{0}
, and
c\in C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}^{\circ}(B_{0})
, then
b^{c^{g}}=(b^{g^{-1}})^{cg}=(b^{g^{-1}})^{g}=b,
that is
c^{g}\in C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}^{\circ}(B_{0})
.

This implies that
B_{1},B_{2}\subset N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B_{0})\subseteq N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
, therefore we can take
B
to lie in the normaliser of a maximal torus; using
Theorem

2.2.7
this means that
B=B_{0}\rtimes w
where
w
is the preimage in
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
of an element of
W(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
.

Let
\tau_{w}
be the endomorphism of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
induced by the conjugation action of
w\in N_{\boldsymbol{G}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
. Since
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(w)
is finite, by
Proposition

2.2.16

\tau
is a Steinberg endomorphism. Then, by
Theorem

2.2.17
, the map
t\mapsto\tau_{w}(t)t^{-1}=t^{w}t^{-1}
is surjective, i.e. for any
t_{1}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
there is
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
s.t.
t_{1}=t^{w}t^{-1}
. Rearranging the terms gives
wt_{1}=w^{t^{-1}}
, so the elements of
w\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
are all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\text{-conjugate}
; in particular,
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\text{-conjugate}
as for the cases we are considering there is a unique conjugacy class in
W(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
of elements of order
\lvert w\rvert
, so for both groups the element extending
B_{0}
lies in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}w
.

Therefore,
B
is unique up to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\text{-conjugacy}
.
∎

For the finite case, let
\mathrm{T}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}^{\sigma}
.

Lemma 2.9.5
.

There are at most
\lvert C_{\mathrm{T}}(w)\rvert
conjugacy classes of complements to
\mathrm{T}
in
Tw
.

Proof.

This time we use the map
\varphi_{w}\colon t\mapsto[t,w]
; clearly,
\varphi_{w}\colon\mathrm{T}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}
, and it is a homomorphism since for any
t,u\in\mathrm{T}
we have:

\displaystyle\varphi_{w}(tu)

\displaystyle=[tu,w]=

\displaystyle=u^{-1}t^{-1}w^{-1}tuw=

\displaystyle=u^{-1}(t^{-1}w^{-1}tw)u(u^{-1}w^{-1}uw)=

\displaystyle=[t,w]^{u^{-1}}[u,w]=

\displaystyle=[t,w][u,w]=\varphi_{w}(t)\varphi_{w}(u),

where the last step follows because
u,[t,w]\in\mathrm{T}
, which is abelian.

Observe now that given
s\in\mathrm{T}
, by rearranging
(tw)^{s}=uw
we obtain
t^{-1}u=[s,w]
, which means that
tw
and
uw
are
\mathrm{T}\text{-conjugate}
iff
t^{-1}u\in[\mathrm{T},w]=\im\varphi_{w}
.

Therefore,
\mathrm{T}w
splits into
\lvert\ker\varphi_{w}\rvert=\lvert C_{\mathrm{T}}(w)\rvert
conjugacy classes, not all of which have elements with the same order as
w
.
∎

Remark
.

While in general not all the classes have elements with the same order as
w
, they do for example if
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(w)=Z(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
, since multiplication by elements of the centre does not change the order.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
be an algebraic group with Steinberg endomorphism
\sigma
, and
H\leq\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
; we want to know whether the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugates}
of
H
contained in
\mathrm{G}
are
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugate}
or they split into multiple conjugacy classes.

Definition 2.9.6
.

Let
G
be an abstract group and
\sigma
be an automorphism of
G
. We say that
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
are
\sigma\text{-conjugate}
if there exists
x\in G
such that
g_{2}=x^{-1}g_{1}\sigma(x)
. It can be shown that
\sigma\text{-conjugacy}
is an equivalence relation, and its equivalence classes are called
\sigma\text{-conjugacy}
classes
of
G
.

Lemma 2.9.7

(
[
48
, Lemma 21.10]
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
be a linear algebraic group with Steinberg endomorphism
\sigma
, and let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{1}
be a closed connected normal
\sigma\text{-stable}
subgroup. Then the quotient map induces a natural bijection from
\sigma\text{-conjugacy}
classes of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
to
\sigma\text{-conjugacy}
classes of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}/\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{1}
.

Theorem 2.9.8
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
be a connected linear algebraic group with a Steinberg endomorphism
\sigma
acting transitively on a non-empty set
V
with a compatible
\sigma\text{-action}
, i.e.
\sigma(g.v)=\sigma(g).\sigma(v)
for all
g\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
,
v\in V
. Then:

(i)

\sigma
has fixed points on
V
, i.e.
V^{\sigma}\neq\emptyset
.

(ii)

If the stabilizer
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{x}
is closed for some
x\in V
, then for any
v\in V^{\sigma}
there is a natural 1-to-1 correspondence:

\Set{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}\text{-orbits on }V^{\sigma}}\leftrightarrow\Set{\sigma\text{-conjugacy classes in }\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{v}/\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{v}^{\circ}}.

Proof.

See for example
[
48
, Theorem 21.11]
.
∎

Corollary 2.9.9
.

Let
H
be a subgroup of
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
. If
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
is connected, then all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugates}
of
H
that lie in
\mathrm{G}
are conjugate in
\mathrm{G}
. If
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)\leq\mathrm{G}
, then the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugates}
of
H
that lie in
\mathrm{G}
split into a number of conjugacy classes equal to the number of conjugacy classes of elements of
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
.

Proof.

Let
H=\langle h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}\rangle
be a subgroup of
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
, and apply theorem
Theorem

2.9.8
by taking
V
to be
\Set{(h_{1}^{g},\ldots,h_{n}^{g})\colon g\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}
. By construction,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
acts transitively on
V
by conjugation, and since we have
H\leq\mathrm{G}
then
\sigma(h_{i})=h_{i}
, implying that
\sigma(h_{i}^{g})=h_{i}^{\sigma(g)}
for any
i=1,\ldots,n
and
g\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, i.e.
\sigma
is compatible with the action of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
on
V
.

We also have that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{(h_{1},\ldots,h_{n})}=C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
is closed since
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(h_{1})\cap\ldots\cap C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(h_{n})
is an intersection of centralisers of elements, which are closed sets, so by
Theorem

2.9.8
we have a 1-to-1 correspondence between the
\mathrm{G}\text{-orbits}
on
V^{\sigma}
, i.e. the number of conjugacy classes of
H
in
\mathrm{G}
, and the number of
\sigma\text{-conjugacy}
classes in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{(h_{1},\ldots,h_{n})}/\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{(h_{1},\ldots,h_{n})}^{\circ}=C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)/C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)^{\circ}
.

If
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
is connected, then
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)/C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)^{\circ}
is trivial so there is only one such conjugacy class.

If
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)\leq\mathrm{G}
, since
\sigma
fixes elements of
\mathrm{G}
then the
\sigma\text{-classes}
on
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
are the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
classes.
∎

Since we normally start by counting classes in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
and then use that knowledge to move down to the finite group
\mathrm{G}
, we need to know whether
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}
in the first place. In some cases this depends on the irrationalities in the characters of
H
, i.e. whether the ground field splits a certain polynomial.

Lemma 2.9.10
.

The field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
for odd
q
is a splitting field for the polynomial

(i)

X^{2}-X-1
;

(ii)

X^{2}-X-7
;

(iii)

X^{2}-X-9
;

(iv)

X^{3}-X^{2}-2X+1
;

if and only if

(i)

q\equiv 0,\pm 1\bmod 5
;

(ii)

q\equiv 0,\pm 1,\pm 4,\pm 5,\pm 6,\pm 7,\pm 9,\pm 13\bmod 29
;

(iii)

q\equiv 0,\pm 1,\pm 3,\pm 4,\pm 7,\pm 9,\pm 10,\pm 11,\pm 12,\pm 16\bmod 37
;

(iv)

q\equiv 0,\pm 1\bmod 7
.

Proof.

The first three cases can be shown by computing the roots of the corresponding polynomial:

(i)

(1\pm\sqrt{5})/2
;

(ii)

(1\pm\sqrt{29})/2
;

(iii)

(1\pm\sqrt{37})/2
.

The splitting field must contain
\sqrt{d}
,
d=5,29,37
, respectively. If
d
is 0 in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
then the polynomial splits as
q
is odd. The other congruences follow by using quadratic reciprocity, as
d
is a square in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
if and only if
q
is a square in
\mathbb{F}_{d}
, and the list of congruences can be computed explicitly.

For (iv), if
q\equiv 0\bmod 7
then
X^{3}-X^{2}-2X+1
factorises as
(X+2)^{3}
. Otherwise, observe that
-\zeta_{7}-\zeta_{7}^{-1}
is a root, with
\zeta_{7}
a 7th root of unity:

\displaystyle\left.X^{3}-X^{2}-2X+1\right|_{X=-\zeta_{7}-\zeta_{7}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=(-\zeta_{7}-\zeta_{7}^{-1})^{3}-(-\zeta_{7}-\zeta_{7}^{-1})^{2}-2(-\zeta_{7}-\zeta_{7}^{-1})+1

\displaystyle=-\zeta_{7}^{3}-3\zeta_{7}-3\zeta_{7}^{-1}-\zeta_{7}^{-3}-\zeta_{7}^{2}-\zeta_{7}^{-2}-2+2\zeta_{7}+2\zeta_{7}^{-1}+1

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=0}^{6}\zeta_{7}^{i}

\displaystyle=0.

Similarly, one can verify that the other roots are
-\zeta_{7}^{2}-\zeta_{7}^{-2}
and
-\zeta_{7}^{3}-\zeta_{7}^{-3}
.

If
q\equiv 1\bmod 7
, then
\mathbb{F}_{q}
admits 7th roots of unity, so the polynomial splits. Otherwise, consider
\mathbb{F}_{q^{a}}
with
q^{a}\equiv 1\bmod 7
, and observe that the roots are invariant under the map
x\mapsto x^{q}
, i.e. they lie in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, iff
q\equiv\pm 1\bmod 7
.
∎

Another key result that allows us to count conjugacy classes in an algebraic group is the following lifting lemma, proved by Larsen:

Theorem 2.9.11

(
[
29
, Theorem A.12]
)
.

If
H
is a finite group and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\cdot)
is a split group scheme, then the cardinality of the finite set
\hom(H,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(k))/\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(k)
is independent of the algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic not dividing
\lvert H\rvert
.

Here,
\hom(A,B)
denotes the the set of group homomorphism from
A
to
B
, and
\hom(A,B)/B
is the set of
B\text{-conjugacy}
classes of such homomorphisms.

This essentially tells us that to count the different conjugacy classes of embeddings of
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
in characteristic
p\notdivides\lvert H\rvert
, we can arbitrarily choose one such
p
, and the result automatically generalises.

Since we only use schemes indirectly through this lemma, we refer to
[
35
, §1-2]
for a detailed overview of the subject. In particular, we have that algebraic groups are group schemes, and we can think of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\cdot)
as the map associating the algebraically closed field
k
to the algebraic group
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(k)
, where
\mathrm{G}
is a root datum. As we study embeddings in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7,\mathrm{ad}}
, which are split reductive groups, we are able to use this result.

2.10
Maximal subgroups of exceptional groups of Lie type

In this section we will focus only on the groups of exceptional type, and present some well-known results about the classification of their maximal subgroups. When some groups belong to a known list we will often omit it, as it may be a rather long one, and refer to the original result for those interested.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
be a simple algebraic group of exceptional adjoint type over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
p
, and let
\aut\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
be the abstract group generated by inner, graph, diagonal, and field automorphisms of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. The maximal closed subgroups of positive dimension in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
are given by the following:

Theorem 2.10.1

(
[
43
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{1}
be a group satisfying
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{1}\leq\aut\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
be a proper closed connected subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
which is maximal among proper connected closed
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{X})\text{-invariant}
subgroups of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. Then one of the following holds:

(i)

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
is either parabolic or reductive of maximal rank.

(ii)

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7}
,
p\neq 2
, and
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}})=(2^{2}\times\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{4}).\sym_{3}
.

(iii)

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}
,
p\neq 2,3,5
, and
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}})=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{1}\times\sym_{5}
.

(iv)

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
belongs to a list of known cases.

The subgroups
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
in (ii), (iii), and (iv) exist, are unique up to conjugacy in
\aut\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and are maximal among closed connected
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}})\text{-invariant}
subgroups of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

For a given
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, let
\sigma
be a Steinberg endomorphism of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and set
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
; we denote by
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{G}/Z(\mathrm{G})
.

In particular, we are interested in the cases when
\mathrm{G}
is one of
\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
,
\mathrm{E}_{6}(q)
,
\!\prescript{2}{}{\mathrm{E}}_{6}(q)
,
\mathrm{E}_{7}(q)
, and
\mathrm{E}_{8}(q)
, as the maximal subgroups of the other possible
\mathrm{G}
have been completely classified.

Let
\mathscr{X}
be the set of the maximal positive-dimensional subgroups of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
given by
Theorem

2.10.1
; then we denote by
\mathscr{X}^{\sigma}
the set of maximal subgroups arising from positive-dimensional subgroups of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, which means:

(i)

\mathscr{X}^{\sigma}
is the set of fixed points
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}^{\sigma}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}\in\mathscr{X}
is
\sigma\text{-stable}
.

(ii)

If
Z(\mathrm{G})\neq 1
,
\mathscr{X}^{\sigma}
is the set of images of elements in (i) modulo the centre.

(iii)

If we are considering
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
,
\mathscr{X}^{\sigma}
is the set of
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})
, where
\mathrm{X}
lies in the set in (i) associated to
\mathrm{G}
.

Observe first that the maximal subgroups of
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
split into three categories:

(i)

M\cap\mathrm{G}/Z(\mathrm{G})
is a maximal subgroup of
\mathrm{G}/Z(\mathrm{G})
.

(ii)

M\cap\mathrm{G}/Z(\mathrm{G})
is not a maximal subgroup of
\mathrm{G}/Z(\mathrm{G})
.
M
is called a
novelty maximal subgroup
in this case.

(iii)

(\mathrm{G}/Z(\mathrm{G}))\leq M
.

In
Chapter

4
we will deal only with maximal subgroups of
\mathrm{G}/Z(\mathrm{G})
, so we can compute
M
by taking their normaliser in
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
.

The maximal subgroups of
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
are described by
[
40
, Theorem 2]
:

Theorem 2.10.2
.

Let
M
be a maximal subgroup of
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
, then one of the following holds:

(i)

M\in\mathscr{X}^{\sigma}
.

(ii)

M
has the same type as
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, possibly twisted. For example,
\mathrm{E}_{6}(p)
and
\!\prescript{2}{}{\mathrm{E}}_{6}(p^{2})
are subgroups of
\mathrm{E}_{6}(p^{2})
that belong to this category.

(iii)

\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{E}_{8}
,
p>5
, and
M=(\alt_{5}\times\alt_{6}).2^{2}
.

(iv)

M
is an exotic
r\text{-local}
subgroup, i.e. the normaliser of an elementary abelian
r\text{-group}
for
r\neq p
.

(v)

H=F^{*}(M)
is simple.

The subgroup in (iii) was discovered by Borovik in
[
10
]
, and is unique up to conjugacy; the subgroups in (iv) are known, and a list is given in
[
20
, Theorem 1]
.

The groups in (v) divide naturally in two classes, depending on whether the simple group
H
is a group of Lie type in characteristic
p
, or any other type of simple group; the two classes are called
generic
and
non-generic
subgroups, respectively.

For the generic subgroups, we have the following result:

Theorem 2.10.3
.

Let
H=H(q_{0})
be a simple group of Lie type in characteristic
p
as defined in
Theorem

2.10.2
(v). Assume that either of the following holds:

(i)

q>u(G)\cdot(2,p-1)
and
H(q_{0})
is one of
\mathrm{A}_{1}(q_{0})
,
\!\prescript{2}{}{B}_{2}(q_{0})
,
\!\prescript{2}{}{G}_{2}(q_{0})
.

(ii)

q>9
,
H(q_{0})
is not as in (i), and
H(q_{0})\neq\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\epsilon}(16)
.

Then
H
satisfies
Theorem

2.10.2
(i) or (ii).

Here,
u(G)
is as in the following table.

\mathrm{G}_{2}

\mathrm{F}_{4}

\mathrm{E}_{6}

\mathrm{E}_{7}

\mathrm{E}_{8}

u(G)

12

68

124

388

1312

It may be possible that there are no generic subgroups that do not satisfy
Theorem

2.10.2
(i) or (ii).

For the non-generic subgroups, the list of potential candidates is given in
[
41
, Theorem 1]
, where the problem of finding an explicit embedding of
H
in
\mathrm{G}
(and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
) up to conjugacy is still open for some cases.

The subgroups
H
are also categorised depending on whether they are primitive subgroups or not, see
Definition

1.0.1
. The list of candidate non-generic
H
is studied in
[
44
]
, where they are classified depending on whether they embed in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
as a strongly imprimitive subgroup, as a primitive subgroup, or they may admit embeddings of either type. The complete list is given in
[
44
, Tables 1.1-1.3]
.

In particular,
[
44
, Theorem 1]
gives a list of triples
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}},H,p)
such that
H
embeds in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
in characteristic
p
only as a strongly imprimitive subgroup. This has a strong implication for maximality in finite groups:

Theorem 2.10.4

(
[
44
, Theorem 8]
)
.

If
H
embeds in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
in characteristic
p
only as a strongly imprimitive subgroup, then
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
has no maximal subgroup with socle
H
.

Therefore, primitive subgroups are the only ones with a chance of being the socle of a maximal subgroups of
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
:

Lemma 2.10.5
.

If
H
embeds primitively in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, then either
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
is a maximal subgroup of
\mathrm{G}
or it is contained in a subgroup
M
such that one of
Theorem

2.10.2
(ii), (iii), (v), holds. If (v) holds, then
F^{*}(M)
admits a primitive embedding in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

This follows directly from the fact that
H
is a simple primitive group.

The complete list of cases studied in
[
44
]
is rather long, so we will list only those we can attempt with the technique hereby explained:

G

H

\mathrm{F}_{4}

\alt(5),\alt(6),\PSL_{2}(q)

q=7,8,13,17,25,27

\mathrm{E}_{6}

\alt(5),\alt(6),\PSL_{2}(q)

q=7,8,11,13,17,25,27

\mathrm{E}_{7}

\alt(5),\alt(6),\PSL_{2}(q)

q=7,8,11,13,17,19,25,27,29,37

\mathrm{E}_{8}

\alt(5),\alt(6),\PSL_{2}(q)

q=7,8,11,13,16,17,19,25,27,29,31,32,41,49,61

Observe that we have
\alt(5)\simeq\PSL_{2}(4)\simeq\PSL_{2}(5)
and
\alt(6)\simeq\PSL_{2}(9)
, so we can consider them of Lie type.

As we will see later on, unfortunately the technique we use fails on most of these, generally when
q
is “too small”, when doing the steps described in
Section

3.4.3
and
Section

3.4.4
.

We now list some results about these cases that have already been established. In particular,
Theorem

2.10.6
allows us to ignore the case
\alt(5)
altogether, as the group is always strongly imprimitive. We will also specify whether the number of conjugacy classes of embeddings is known.

2.10.1
Embedding of alternating groups

Using the notation that was previously defined in
Section

2.10
, we have the following results.

Theorem 2.10.6

(
[
22
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
be a simple group of type
\mathrm{F}_{4},\mathrm{E}_{6},\prescript{2}{}{\mathrm{E}_{6}},\mathrm{E}_{7}
, or
\mathrm{E}_{8}
. If
H\leq\mathrm{G}
with
F^{*}(H)\simeq\alt(n)
, for
n=5
or
n\geq 8
, then
H
lies inside a member of
\mathscr{X}^{\sigma}
.

Theorem 2.10.7

(
[
22
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
be a simple group of type
\mathrm{F}_{4},\mathrm{E}_{6},\prescript{2}{}{\mathrm{E}_{6}},\mathrm{E}_{7}
, or
\mathrm{E}_{8}
over a field of characteristic
p
, and let
H\leq\mathrm{G}
not lie inside a member of
\mathscr{X}^{\sigma}
.

(i)

if
F^{*}(H)\simeq\alt(6)
, then
(\mathrm{G},p)
is one of
(\mathrm{F}_{4},3)
,
(\mathrm{F}_{4},\geq 7)
,
(\mathrm{E}_{6},\geq 7)
,
(\mathrm{E}_{7},\geq 5)
,
(\mathrm{E}_{8},2)
,
(\mathrm{E}_{8},3)
,
(\mathrm{E}_{8},\geq 7)
;

(ii)

if
F^{*}(H)\simeq\alt(7)
, then
(\mathrm{G},p)
is one of
(\mathrm{E}_{7},5)
,
(\mathrm{E}_{8},3)
,
(\mathrm{E}_{8},5)
,
(\mathrm{E}_{8},7)
,
(\mathrm{E}_{8},\geq 11)
.

Note that in (i), the case
(\mathrm{F}_{4},3)
has been dealt with by the same author, who proved that it may be excluded from the list, although the result has not been published yet at the time of writing.

Therefore, for the alternating groups, only for
n=6,7
there is no complete answer yet. We will deal with the case of
\alt(6)<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
in characteristic 0 or coprime to
\lvert\alt_{6}\rvert
in
Chapter

5
.

2.10.2
Embedding of linear groups

These results will be useful to check that our code works; in particular, we can provide an independent confirmation of
Theorem

2.10.8
,
Theorem

2.10.9
, and the embedding of
\PSL_{2}(61)
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}(\mathbb{C})
of
Theorem

2.10.10
. The supplementary material includes the computations required in such cases.

Theorem 2.10.8

(
[
30
]
)
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic that does not divide
\lvert\PSL_{2}(41)\rvert
. There are three conjugacy classes of
\PSL_{2}(41)\text{-subgroups}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}(k)
, the subgroups give rise to six conjugacy classes of embeddings into
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}(k)
.

The groups
\SL_{2}(41)
and
\PGL_{2}(41)
are not embedded in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}(\mathbb{C})
.

Theorem 2.10.9

(
[
30
]
)
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic that does not divide
\lvert\PSL_{2}(49)\rvert
. There are two conjugacy classes of
\PSL_{2}(49)\text{-subgroups}
in
\mathrm{E}_{8}(k)
, the subgroups give rise to four conjugacy classes of embeddings into
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}(k)
.

The groups
\SL_{2}(49)
and
\PGL_{2}(49)
are not embedded in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}(\mathbb{C})
.

Theorem 2.10.10

(
[
16
,
38
,
18
,
19
,
52
]
)
.

Kostant’s conjecture holds, that is the group
\PSL_{2}(q)
embeds in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{C})
when
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}},q)
is one of
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{2},13)
,
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4},25)
,
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6},25)
,
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7},37)
,
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8},61)
.

For
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}_{2},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}
, there is a unique
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
\PSL_{2}(q)
. A similar result is only conjectured for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
, and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7}
.

Theorem 2.10.11

(
[
29
, Theorem 2.27]
)
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic that does not divide
\lvert\PSL_{2}(31)\rvert
. Then there are three conjugacy classes of
\PGL_{2}(31)\text{-subgroups}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}(k)
, the subgroups give rise to three conjugacy classes of embeddings into
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}(k)
.

This implies the following:

Corollary 2.10.12
.

Under the same hypothesis, there are at least three conjugacy classes of
\PSL_{2}(31)\text{-subgroups}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}(k)
.

In particular, it is not known whether there are other embeddings of
\PSL_{2}(31)
that do not extend to
\PGL_{2}(31)
.

2.11
The trilinear form for
\mathrm{E}_{6}

The contents of this section can be found in
[
46
]
and
[
3
]
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional vector space over a field
k
. Given
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, denote by
M_{m}(V)
the
k\text{-space}
of functions
V^{m}\rightarrow k
, i.e. the space of
m\text{-forms}
. Then
g\in\GL(V)
acts on
\alpha\in M_{m}(V)
via

(g.\alpha)(v_{1},\ldots,v_{m})=\alpha(v_{1}\cdot g,\ldots,v_{m}\cdot g),

where
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\in V
.

Definition 2.11.1
.

An element
g\in\GL(V)
is a
similarity
of
\alpha\in M_{m}(V)
if
g.\alpha=\lambda_{g}\alpha
for some
\lambda_{g}\in k^{\times}
. A similarity is an
isometry
if
\lambda_{g}=1
. It is clear that isometries of a given
\alpha\in M_{m}(V)
form a group, called the
isometry group
of
\alpha
.

Let
\alpha\in M_{m}(V)
be a symmetric
m\text{-linear}
form, and let
e=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
be a basis of
V
. Then
\alpha
is uniquely determined by
\alpha(e_{i_{1}},\ldots,e_{i_{m}})\eqqcolon\alpha_{i_{1}\ldots i_{m}}
, where
1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\leq n
, not necessarily distinct, so that

\alpha=\sum_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots i_{m}\leq n}a_{i_{1}\ldots i_{m}}e_{i_{1}}\ldots e_{i_{m}}.

(2.17)

Definition 2.11.2
.

Each summand of (
2.17
) is called a
monomial
of
\alpha
, thus
\alpha
is uniquely determined by its monomials.

For the rest of the section, we assume that the field
k
has characteristic
\neq 2,3
.

Definition 2.11.3
.

A triple
\mathcal{F}(T,P,f)
is a 3-form if

(i)

f
is a trilinear form on
V
.

(ii)

P:V\times V\rightarrow k
is linear in the first component and satisfies

\displaystyle P(x,ay)

\displaystyle=a^{2}P(x,y),

\displaystyle P(x,y+z)

\displaystyle=P(x,y)+P(x,z)+f(x,y,z),

for all
x,y,z\in V
,
a\in k
.

(iii)

T:V\rightarrow k
satisfies:

\displaystyle T(ax)

\displaystyle=a^{3}T(x),

\displaystyle T(x+y)

\displaystyle=T(x)+T(y)+P(x,y)+P(y,x),

for all
x,y\in V
,
a\in k
.

Lemma 2.11.4
.

If
\mathcal{F}(T,P,f)
is a 3-form, then
f
is symmetric.

Proof.

Using the definition of
T
and
P
we have that:

\displaystyle T(x+y+z)

\displaystyle=T(x)+T(y+z)+P(x,y+z)+P(y+z,x)

\displaystyle=T(x)+[T(y)+T(z)+P(y,z)+P(z,y)]+[P(x,y)+P(x,z)+

\displaystyle+f(x,y,z)]+[P(y,x)+P(z,x)],

where the brackets are simply to separate the computation of each summands.
Therefore

f(x,y,z)=T(x+y+z)-\sum_{a\in\left\{x,y,z\right\}}T(a)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\neq b\\
a,b\in\left\{x,y,z\right\}\end{subarray}}P(a,b)

(2.18)

is symmetric.
∎

Lemma 2.11.5
.

Since
\ch k\neq 2,3
, then
P(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}f(x,y,y)
and
T(x)=\frac{1}{6}f(x,x,x)
.

Therefore, if we define
P_{x}(y)\coloneqq P(x,y)
, then
P_{x}
is a quadratic form, whose associated bilinear form is
\frac{1}{2}f_{x}
, where
f_{x}\coloneqq f(x,-,-)
.

Proof.

The first equality follows from the definition of
P
, since

4P(x,y)=P(x,2y)=P(x,y)+P(x,y)+f(x,y,y),

so if
\ch k\neq 2
then by rearranging the terms we obtain the result.

The second equality follows from (
2.18
) and the definition of
T
. In particular, we have that
T(3x)=27T(x)
, and
T(2x)=2T(x)+2P(x,x)
, i.e.
P(x,x)=3T(x)
if
\ch k\neq 2
, so from (
2.18
) we have

\displaystyle f(x,x,x)

\displaystyle=T(3x)-3T(x)-6P(x,x)

\displaystyle=27T(x)-3T(x)-18T(x)=6T(x),

and the result follows since
\ch k\neq 2,3
.
∎

Definition 2.11.6
.

Let
v\in V
,
U\subseteq V
. We denote by
x\Delta
the radical of
f_{x}
, and
U\Delta\coloneqq\bigcap_{x\in U}x\Delta
. We also define
U\Theta\coloneqq\Set{v\in V}{P_{v}(u)=0\;\;\forall\,u\in U}
.

If
U\subseteq U\Delta
we call
U

singular
. If
U\subseteq U\Theta
we call
U

brilliant
.

Lemma 2.11.7
.

For any
U\subseteq V
,
U\Delta
and
U\Theta
are subspaces of
V
.

Proof.

This follows from the definition, since
f
is linear and
P
is linear in the first component.
∎

Lemma 2.11.8
.

Let
U\leq V
and
v\in V
, then
v\in U\Theta
iff
f(v,u,u^{\prime})=0
for all
u,u^{\prime}\in U
.

Proof.

This follows from the definition of
P
:

f(v,u,u^{\prime})=P(v,u+u^{\prime})-P(v,u)-P(v,u^{\prime}).

Let
v\in U\Theta
, then all the terms on the right hand side are zero since
u,u^{\prime},u+u^{\prime}\in U
.

Vice versa, if
f(v,u,u^{\prime})=0
for all
u,u^{\prime}\in U
then
P(v,u+u^{\prime})=P(v,u)+P(v,u^{\prime})
for all
u,u^{\prime}\in U
. In particular, for all
a\in k,u\in U
we have that
aP(v,u)=P(v,au)=a^{2}P(v,u)
, so
P_{v}(u)=0
for all
u\in U
, i.e.
v\in U\Theta
.
∎

Definition 2.11.9

(Dickson 3-form)
.

Let
V
be a
27\text{-dimensional}
vector space with basis
X=\Set{x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime},x_{ij}}{1\leq i,j\leq 6,i<j}
, with the convention
x_{ij}=-x_{ji}
. Let
f
be the symmetric trilinear form whose nonzero monomials are
x_{i}x_{j}^{\prime}x_{ij}
,
1\leq i\neq j\leq 6
and
x_{1^{d}2^{d}}x_{3^{d}4^{d}}x_{5^{d}6^{d}}
where
d\in C\subset\alt_{6}
.

C
is a set of coset representatives for
\alt_{\Delta}
in
\alt_{6}
, where
\alt_{\Delta}
denotes the stabiliser in
\alt_{6}
of the partition
\Delta=\left\{\left\{1,2\right\},\left\{3,4\right\},\left\{5,6\right\}\right\}
of
\left\{1,\ldots,6\right\}
. For a choice of
C
, the images of
(1,2;3,4;5,6)
are as follows:

12 34 56

13 24 65

14 23 56

15 26 43

16 25 34

12 35 64

13 26 54

14 25 63

15 24 36

16 23 45

12 36 45

13 25 46

14 26 35

15 23 64

16 24 53

For
(i,j;k,l;m,n)
as in the above table, the monomial
x_{ij}x_{kl}x_{mn}
has sign
+1
in
f
.

The
Dickson 3-form
is the 3-form
(T,P,f)
, where
f
is the trilinear form above, which we call the
\mathrm{E}_{6}\text{-form}
.

Remark
.

Since we are working with
\ch k\neq 2,3
,
P
and
T
are
P(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}f(x,y,y)
,
T(x)=\frac{1}{6}f(x,x,x)
, by
Lemma

2.11.5
.

Theorem 2.11.10

(
[
3
, Theorem 5.4]
)
.

The isometry group of the Dickson 3-form is the universal Chevalley group of type
\mathrm{E}_{6}
.

Definition 2.11.11
.

Using the notation of
Definition

2.11.9
, let

V_{9}:=\langle x_{16},x_{25},x_{34},x_{12},x_{56},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{5},x_{1},x_{6}^{\prime}\rangle,

and denote by
\mathcal{V}_{9}
the orbit of
V_{9}
under the action of
\mathrm{E}_{6}
.

A
3-decomposition
of
V
is a decomposition of
V
into
A_{1}\oplus A_{2}\oplus A_{3}
such that each of the
A_{i}
is a member of
\mathcal{V}_{9}
and
A_{i}\oplus A_{j}=A_{k}\Theta
for
i,j,k
distinct.

A property of 3-decompositions we will need is the following:

Lemma 2.11.12

(
[
4
, p.4]
)
.

The stabiliser in
\mathrm{E}_{6}
of a 3-decomposition is isomorphic to the product of a Cartan subgroup of
\mathrm{E}_{6}
with three copies of
\SL_{3}(k)
(i.e. a group of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}
) extended by
\sym_{3}
.

Recall that there is a canonical correspondence between bilinear forms on
V
and linear maps
V\otimes V\rightarrow k
:

(i)

Given
f
a bilinear form on
V
, the associated map is
u\otimes v\mapsto f(u,v)
.

(ii)

Given a linear map
F:V\otimes V\rightarrow k
, the associated bilinear form is
f(u,v)=F(u\otimes v)
.

The set of linear maps
V\otimes V\rightarrow k
is the dual space of
V\otimes V
, i.e.
(V\otimes V)^{*}
, which is canonically isomorphic to
V^{*}\otimes V^{*}
since we will only work with a finite-dimensional
V
, so bilinear forms can be thought as elements of
V^{*}\otimes V^{*}
.

The symmetric square of
V^{*}
is the quotient of
V^{*}\otimes V^{*}
by the ideal generated by elements of the form
x\otimes y-y\otimes x
,
x,y\in V^{*}
, so by following the maps described above, there is a canonical map between
\sym^{2}(V^{*})
and symmetric bilinear forms of
V
.

Remark
.

The above considerations extend naturally when
V
is a
kG\text{-module}
.

Lemma 2.11.13
.

Let
f
be a bilinear form on the
kG\text{-module}

V
, and
F\in(V\otimes V)^{*}
the associated linear map. Then
f
is
G\text{-invariant}
iff
F\in{(V\otimes V)^{*}}^{G}
.

Proof.

Let
f
be
G\text{-invariant}
, i.e.
f(u,v)=f(g.u,g.v)
for all
u,v\in V
and all
g\in G
. Then

\displaystyle g.F(u\otimes v)

\displaystyle=F(g.(u\otimes v))

\displaystyle=F(g.u\otimes g.v)

\displaystyle=f(g.u,g.v)

\displaystyle=f(u,v)

\displaystyle=F(u\otimes v).

Vice versa, let
F\in(V\otimes V)^{*}
be fixed by
G
, i.e.
g.F=F
for all
g\in G
, then

\displaystyle f(g.u,g.v)

\displaystyle=F(g.u\otimes g.v)

\displaystyle=F(g.(u\otimes v))

\displaystyle=g.F(u\otimes v)

\displaystyle=F(u\otimes v)

\displaystyle=f(u,v).

∎

Remark
.

In the same way, it follows from the canonical mappings that a symmetric bilinear form
f
on
V
is
G\text{-invariant}
iff the associated element in
\sym^{2}(V^{*})
is fixed by
G
.

In particular, if
\dim\sym^{2}(V^{*})=1
, then there is a unique symmetric bilinear form on
V
, up to scalars; if
V
is a
kG\text{-module}
and
\dim\sym^{2}(V^{*})^{G}=1
, then there is a unique
G\text{-invariant}
symmetric bilinear form on
V
.

The above considerations extend to
\sym^{n}(V^{*})
and symmetric
n\text{-linear}
forms on
V
. In particular, a symmetric trilinear form
f
on
V
corresponds to an element
F
of
\sym^{3}(V^{*})
, and
f
is
G\text{-invariant}
iff
F
is fixed by
G
.

Definition 2.11.14
.

Let
G
be a group over
k
,
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
be finite-dimensional
kG
-modules,
\Set{\alpha_{i,j}^{c}}_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{i,j}}
be a basis of
\hom_{kG}(M_{i},\sym^{2}(M_{j}^{*}))
, for
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
, where

\mathcal{C}_{i,j}=\left\{1,\ldots,\dim\hom_{kG}(M_{i},\sym^{2}(M_{j}^{*}))\right\},

and
\tau_{i}
be the canonical
kG\text{-isomorphism}
between
\sym^{2}(M_{i}^{*})
and the symmetric bilinear forms of
M_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
.

We define
f_{i,j}^{c}\colon M_{i}\times M_{j}\times M_{j}\rightarrow k
to be the
G\text{-invariant}
trilinear map defined by
(x,y,y^{\prime})\mapsto(\tau_{j}(\alpha^{c}_{i,j}(x)))(y,y^{\prime})
, for
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
.

We also define
\Set{f_{i}^{b}}_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
to be a basis of the set of
G\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear forms of
M_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
, where
\mathcal{B}_{i}=\left\{1,\ldots,\dim\sym^{3}(M_{i}^{*})\right\}
.

We assume that everything is chosen in such a way that all the
f_{i,j}^{c}
and
f_{i}^{b}
defined as above are nontrivial.

Note that in later chapters, we will often identify
f_{i,j}^{c}
with
\alpha_{i,j}^{c}
for simplicity.

Lemma 2.11.15
.

Let
G
be a group over
k
,
M=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
a finite-dimensional
kG\text{-module}
, and
f
a
G\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear form on
M
. Then

f=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}a_{i}^{b}f_{i}^{b}+\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{i,j}}a_{i,j}^{c}f_{i,j}^{c},

(2.19)

where
f_{i}^{b},f_{i,j}^{c},\mathcal{B}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i,j}
are as in
Definition

2.11.14
, and all
a_{i}^{b},a_{i,j}^{c}
lie in
k
.

Proof.

Let
x,y,z\in M
, with projections
x_{i},y_{i},z_{i}
over
M_{i}
. Then using the linearity of
f
we have

f(x,y,z)=\sum_{1\leq i,j,k\leq 2}f(x_{i},y_{j},z_{k})=\sum_{1\leq i,j,k\leq 2}\left.f\right|_{M_{i}\times M_{j}\times M_{k}}(x_{i},y_{j},z_{k}).

The conclusion is self-evident using
Definition

2.11.14
, since by symmetry we have four terms:
\left.f\right|_{M_{1}}
,
\left.f\right|_{M_{2}}
,
\left.f\right|_{M_{1}\times M_{2}\times M_{2}}
, and
\left.f\right|_{M_{2}\times M_{1}\times M_{1}}
. Each of the first two terms is a linear combinations of generators of the space of trilinear forms of the corresponding module, while each of the latter two terms is a linear combination of generators of
\hom_{kG}(M_{i},\sym^{2}(M_{j}^{*}))
for
i\neq j
, in the way described in
Definition

2.11.14
.
∎

Remark
.

Despite the unfriendly notation, the takeaway of the lemma is very intuitive: a
G\text{-invariant}
trilinear form on a module
M=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
, i.e. an element of the
G\text{-fixed}
point space of
\sym^{3}(M)
, is a linear combination of
\sym^{3}(M)
,
\hom_{kG}(M_{1},\sym^{2}(M_{2}^{*}))
,
\hom_{kG}(M_{2},\sym^{2}(M_{1}^{*}))
.

This can be generalised to decompositions with more than two direct summands, say
M=M\oplus N\oplus O
, but it needs additional maps like
\hom_{kG}(M,N^{*}\otimes O^{*})
in order to include the terms originating from
\left.f\right|_{M\times N\times O}(x_{M},y_{N},z_{O})
, where
x_{M},y_{N},z_{O}
denote the projection of
x,y,z
over
M,N,O
, respectively.

In later chapters, we will sometimes denote
\left.f\right|_{M\times N\times O}
as
f(M,N,O)
for simplicity.

Lemma 2.11.16
.

Let
U,V
be finite-dimensional
kG\text{-modules}
, and
f
a
G\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear form defined on
U\oplus V
. If
\dim\hom_{kG}(V,\sym^{2}(U^{*}))=0
, then
V\leq U\Theta
.

Proof.

Since
\left.f\right|_{V\times U\times U}
is a linear combination of the
\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\rvert
maps
f_{i,j}^{c}
described in
Definition

2.11.14
, if
\lvert\mathcal{C}\rvert=\dim\hom_{kG}(V,\sym^{2}(U^{*}))=0
then
\left.f\right|_{V\times U\times U}=0
, which means
V\leq U\Theta
by
Lemma

2.11.8
.
∎

Chapter 3
Algorithms

Sections

3.1
,
3.2
,
3.3
and
3.4
describe the constructions used in
Chapter

4
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a Lie algebra of dimension
d
and rank
l
over a finite field
k
of our choice. In
Section

3.1
we construct generators of the corresponding Chevalley group
\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{G}(k)
in its adjoint representation, described in
Definition

2.4.10
, as matrices in
\GL_{d}(k)
. We also construct generators of a maximal torus
T
and of its normaliser
N=N_{\mathrm{G}}(T)
. In
Section

3.2
we describe an easy way to check whether an element of
\GL_{d}(k)
lies in
\mathrm{G}
.

Let
\overline{k}
be the algebraic closure of
k
,
\overline{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
, and let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
be the subgroup of
\GL_{d}(\overline{k})
preserving
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
, in particular
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
for a Frobenius endomorphism
\sigma
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

Since the underlying vector space of
\mathcal{L}
is a
\mathrm{G}\text{-module}
under the natural action of
\mathrm{G}
in its adjoint representation, we will often denote
\mathcal{L}
by
L(\mathrm{G})
, the adjoint module of
\mathrm{G}
. The same holds for
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
and
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
. Whether we consider the Lie algebra structure or the module structure will be clear from the context. The minimal module of
\mathrm{G}
will be denoted by
M(\mathrm{G})
instead.

We then proceed to construct
s,u,t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
that satisfy a presentation for
\PSL_{2}(q)
, described in
Section

2.7
. In
Section

3.3
we construct a suitable
s\in N
and find
u\in T
satisfying the required relations, and in
Section

3.4
we describe how to find all the possible choices for
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. This is divided in several steps:

(i)

In
Section

3.4.1
we describe how to find a Chevalley basis
\varepsilon_{s}
of
\mathcal{L}
that diagonalises
s
.

(ii)

Using
\varepsilon_{s}
, in
Section

3.4.2
we construct an involution
e\in\mathrm{G}
that inverts
s
.

(iii)

In
Sections

3.4.3
and
3.4.4
we describe how to compute the possible
c\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
such that
t=ce
.

Observe that the last step is not always possible, depending on
q
and
k
. The embeddings we study in
Chapter

4
are some of the cases where no issues arise, and we are able to compute the possible choice of
t
.

Sections

3.5
and
3.6
describe how to construct the LDU decomposition of
M\in\SL_{n+1}(k)
, if
M
admits one, and use it to find the preimage of
M
under the standard representation
\rho:\mathrm{A}_{n}\rightarrow\SL_{n+1}(k)
. This is used in
Section

5.1
to construct a copy of
\alt_{6}<\mathrm{F}_{4}(k)\leq\GL_{52}(k)
, for a suitable field
k
.

Let
G
be a group and
V
a finite-dimensional
kG\text{-module}
. In
Section

3.7
we describe how to construct
G\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear forms defined over
V
, if any exists. This is used in
Section

5.3
, where we construct the
\mathrm{C}_{4}\text{-invariant}
form induced by the
\mathrm{E}_{6}\text{-invariant}
form described in
Definition

2.11.9
.

Let
G
be a finite group,
V
a finite-dimensional
kG\text{-module}
,
f
a
G\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear form on
V
, and
H
a subgroup of
G
. In
Section

3.8
we describe how we can write
\left.f\right|_{V}
in terms of
\left.f\right|_{V\!\downarrow_{H}}
. This is used in
Section

5.2
, where we use our knowledge of
\left.f\right|_{V\!\downarrow_{H}}
to study
\left.f\right|_{V}
.

We will point out when an algorithm or a step of it is already available in
Magma
via a built-in function; likewise, we will highlight whenever we use a built-in
Magma
function.

3.1
Construction of a maximal torus, its normaliser, and the corresponding root subgroups

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a Lie algebra of the desired type, with underlying root system
\Phi=\Phi(\mathcal{L})
, and let
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{d})
be a Chevalley basis of
\mathcal{L}
; we choose
\varepsilon
so that the structure constants are
N_{s,r}=\pm(p+1)
for each
r,s\in\Pi
,
\Pi
a base of
\Phi
, as described in
Section

2.4.3
.

Denote by
\mathrm{G}
the group of automorphisms of
\mathcal{L}
generated by
x_{\pm i}(1)
for
i=1,\ldots,l
, and let
T
and
N
be the groups defined in
Section

2.4.4
. We can construct generators for
\mathrm{G}
,
T
, and
N
by using their action on
\varepsilon
described by (
2.4
), (
2.7
), (
2.8
) respectively, in particular we construct
x_{\pm i}(1),h_{i}(\lambda),n_{i}
where
\lambda
is a primitive element of
k^{\times}
, and
\alpha_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,l
are a set of fundamental roots.

The supplementary material contains a function that constructs
x_{\pm i}(1),h_{i}(\lambda),n_{i}
for an arbitrary Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}
over a finite field
k
, provided that the given Chevalley basis
\varepsilon
is a list sorted as described in
Section

A.1
.

Verification 3.1.1
.

There are a few ways we can check that the matrices
x_{\pm i}(1),h_{i}(\lambda),n_{i}
we construct are correct.

If working with
Magma
, as mentioned earlier one could verify that this construction matches the built-in version.

Alternatively, one could verify that the group generated is isomorphic to the one we wanted by computing a composition series for it. In
Magma
this can be done with the
CompositionTree
function, whose algorithm is described in
[
6
]
.

Naturally, we can perform a membership test, explained in
Section

3.2
, to confirm that all the matrices we constructed are in
\mathrm{G}
; and it is easy but useful to verify that
n_{i}
normalises
h_{j}(\lambda)
for any choice of
i,j
, and that the matrices
h_{i}(\lambda)
commute with each other.

3.2
Membership test

Since we are working with large groups, stored by generating matrices, computations in the group can be quite difficult. Therefore, to check whether a matrix
g\in\GL_{d}(k)
is an element of
\mathrm{G}
we use the following result
[
30
, Computation 3.2]
:

Theorem 3.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=\langle a_{1},\ldots,a_{I}\rangle
be a Lie algebra, and let
g\in\GL_{\dim\mathcal{L}}(k)
. Then
g\in\langle x_{\pm i}(1)|1\leq i\leq I\rangle
iff
g^{-1}\mathrm{ad}(a_{i})g=\mathrm{ad}(a_{i}g)
for
1\leq i\leq I
.

Proof.

Define

\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\Set{a\in\mathcal{L}}{g^{-1}\mathrm{ad}(a)g=\mathrm{ad}(ag)}=\Set{a\in\mathcal{L}}{[a,bg^{-1}]g=[ag,b]\;\forall b\in\mathcal{L}}.

First, we show that
\mathcal{G}=\Set{a\in\mathcal{L}}{[b,a]g=[bg,ag]\;\forall b\in\mathcal{L}}
.

Let
a\in\mathcal{L}
be such that
[a,bg^{-1}]g=[ag,b]
for all
b\in\mathcal{L}
, and let
b\in\mathcal{L}
. Then

\displaystyle[b,a]g

\displaystyle=-[b,a]g

\displaystyle=-[a,(bg)g^{-1}]g

\displaystyle=-[ag,bg]

\displaystyle=[bg,ag].

Vice versa, let
a\in\mathcal{L}
be such that
[b,a]g=[bg,ag]
for all
b\in\mathcal{L}
, and let
b\in\mathcal{L}
. Then

\displaystyle[ag,b]

\displaystyle=-[b,ag]

\displaystyle=-[(bg^{-1})g,ag]

\displaystyle=-[bg^{-1},a]g

\displaystyle=[a,bg^{-1}]g.

If we show that
\mathcal{G}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}
, then
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{L}
because
a_{1},\ldots,a_{I}\in\mathcal{G}
are generators for
\mathcal{L}
. Therefore,
g
preserves the Lie product on
\mathcal{L}
and
g\in\aut\mathcal{L}
.

By
Definition

2.4.2
, we only need to show that
\mathcal{G}
is closed under Lie multiplication. Let
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
, then for any
b\in\mathcal{L}
we have

\displaystyle[b,[a,a^{\prime}]]g

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny J}}}{=}}[[a^{\prime},b],a]g+[[b,a],a^{\prime}]g

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny G}}}{=}}[[a^{\prime}g,bg]g^{-1},a]g+[[bg,ag]g^{-1},a^{\prime}]g

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny G}}}{=}}[[a^{\prime}g,bg],ag]+[[bg,ag],a^{\prime}g]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny J}}}{=}}[bg,[a,a^{\prime}]g]

therefore
[a,a^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{G}
. Here, J denotes using the Jacobi identity on
\mathcal{L}
, and G denotes using the fact that
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
.
∎

Therefore, our membership test is performed as follows:

(i)

Select a set
a_{1},\ldots,a_{I}
of generators for
\mathcal{L}
as a Lie algebra. A set of generators for
\mathcal{L}
as a vector space also works, but is in general larger thus requires more computations.

(ii)

Check whether
g^{-1}\mathrm{ad}(a_{i})g=\mathrm{ad}(a_{i}g)
for
i=1,\ldots,I
.

(iii)

g\in\mathrm{G}
if and only if equality holds for all
i
.

3.3
Constructing an element of
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
and its action on
\mathrm{T}

In all the embeddings we study, listed in
Theorem

1.0.2
, we end up looking for an element
u
lying in a maximal torus
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathrm{G}
, and an element
s\in N=N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
that acts on
u
in a certain way. We construct them by taking
s\in N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
first, for a given
\mathrm{T}
, then find a corresponding
u
.

In all cases except
\PSL_{2}(27)
, the
u
and
s
we mention in this section correspond to those of the presentation of the given
\PSL_{2}(q)
,
q
a power of
p
, so we will assume this is the case, and
s,u
are as described in
Section

2.7
.

Recall from
Theorem

2.2.7
that
N=\mathrm{T}\rtimes W
, and from
Section

2.4.4
that
N=\langle\mathrm{T},n_{i}|i=1,\ldots,l\rangle=\langle h_{i}(\lambda),n_{i}|i=1,\ldots,l\rangle
, so we know that a set of generators for
W
is given by the images of
n_{1},\ldots,n_{l}
via the map
N\rightarrow N/\mathrm{T}
. We will then take
s
to be a random element of
\widetilde{W}=\langle n_{i}|i=1,\ldots,l\rangle
of order
\frac{q-1}{2}
.

In all the cases we study, there is a unique conjugacy class in
W
of elements of the appropriate order, making this task quite easy, as we can find
s
by doing a random search in
\widetilde{W}
until we find an element of the correct order, and making sure that its image on
W
has that same order. In other cases, we would have to make sure it lies in the correct conjugacy class(es), which can be done on a case-by-case scenario: the trace could be a distinguishing factor, as is simply checking whether a suitable
u
exists for the given
s
.

Remark
.

Working with
Magma
, usually we can take
s
to be a preimage in
N=N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
of an element
w
of the Weyl group
W=N/\mathrm{T}
of the required order.

We use
s
to find
u
. Start by taking the maximal torus
\mathrm{T}
as constructed in
Section

3.1
, and compute its Sylow
p\text{-subgroup}

\mathrm{T}_{p}
by taking
\frac{\lvert k\rvert-1}{p}\text{-th}
powers of its generators
h_{i}(\lambda)
. Now the matrices
\widehat{h}_{i}\coloneqq h_{i}(\lambda)^{\frac{\lvert k\rvert-1}{p}}
have order
p
and commute with each other, so
\mathrm{T}_{p}\simeq p^{l}
is elementary abelian.

Construction 3.3.1
.

We can construct the matrix
\overline{s}\in\GL_{l}(p)
representing the action of
s
on
\mathrm{T}_{p}
in the following ways:

(i)

If we have
\widehat{h}_{i}
as matrices in
\GL_{d}(k)
, we can assume they are diagonal,
\widehat{h}_{i}=\mathrm{diag}(d_{i1},\ldots,d_{id})
. Then, for
1\leq i\leq l
, the entries of the
i\text{-th}
row of
\overline{s}
are given by the solution of the system of equation given by

\overline{s}_{i1}\log_{a}d_{1j}+\cdots+\overline{s}_{il}\log_{a}d_{lj}=\log_{a}((\widehat{h}_{i}^{s})_{jj}),

(3.1)

for
1\leq j\leq d
, where
a=\omega^{\frac{\lvert k\rvert-1}{p}}
,
\omega
a primitive element of
k
.

(ii)

If we have
\widehat{h}_{i}
as torus elements written in the form
\widehat{h}_{i}=h(a^{\delta_{1i}},\ldots,a^{\delta_{li}})
described in
Section

2.4.4
, where
\delta_{ij}=1
if
i=j
and 0 otherwise, and
a=\omega^{\frac{\lvert k\rvert-1}{p}}
, with
\omega
a primitive element of
k
, let
\widehat{h}_{i}^{s}=h(b_{i1},\ldots,b_{il})
for
i=1,\ldots,l
. Then the entries of the
i\text{-th}
row of
\overline{s}
are given by
\overline{s}_{ij}=\log_{a}(b_{ij})
,
j=1,\ldots,l
.

In both cases we compute
\overline{s}_{ij}
as elements of
\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}
, then we take the corresponding representative in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Proof.

For (i), we have that since the
\widehat{h}_{i}
are diagonal, and
s
normalises the torus they lie in, then
\widehat{h}^{s}
is also diagonal, and
\widehat{h}_{i}^{s}=\widehat{h}_{1}^{\overline{s}_{i1}}\ldots\widehat{h}_{l}^{\overline{s}_{il}}
for some
\overline{s}_{i1},\ldots,\overline{s}_{il}\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}
,
1\leq i\leq l
, as
\widehat{h}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{h}_{l}
is a basis of
\mathrm{T}_{p}
.

We can divide this relation into
l
equations, one for each entry of
\widehat{h}_{i}^{s}
, that is

(\widehat{h}^{s}_{i})_{jj}=(d_{1j})^{\overline{s}_{i1}}\ldots(d_{lj})^{\overline{s}_{il}},

(3.2)

for
j=1,\ldots,d
. Recall that
\widehat{h}_{i}
and
\widehat{h}_{i}^{s}
are both diagonal matrices in
\GL_{d}(k)
, so their diagonal entries are never 0 and we can compute discrete logarithms: let
\omega
be a primitive element of
k
, and let
a=\omega^{\frac{\lvert k\rvert-1}{p}}
, so that
a
has order
p
; then by applying
\log_{a}
to each side of (
3.2
) we obtain (
3.1
).

Since
\widehat{h}^{s}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
, the system of equations (
3.1
) has a solution and since
\widehat{h}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{h}_{l}
is a basis of
\mathrm{T}_{p}
, the solution is unique.

For (ii), the setting is different but the argument is similar. Let
\omega
be a primitive element of
k
and
a=\omega^{\frac{\lvert k\rvert-1}{p}}
, then
h_{1}=h(\omega,1,\ldots,1),\ldots,h_{l}=h(1,\ldots,1,\omega)
is a basis of
\mathrm{T}
(recall that multiplication is component-wise) and
\widehat{h}_{1}=h(a,1,\ldots,1),\ldots,h_{l}=h(1,\ldots,1,a)
, , is a basis of
\mathrm{T}_{p}
. Since
s
normalise
\mathrm{T}_{p}
, for
i=1,\ldots,l
we have

\widehat{h}_{i}^{s}=\widehat{h}_{1}^{\overline{s}_{i1}}\ldots\widehat{h}_{l}^{\overline{s}_{il}}=h(a^{\overline{s}_{i1}},\ldots,a^{\overline{s}_{il}}),

(3.3)

for some
\overline{s}_{i1},\ldots,\overline{s}_{il}\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}
. Therefore by writing
\widehat{h}_{i}^{s}=h(b_{i1},\ldots,b_{il})
we have
\overline{s}_{ij}=\log_{a}(b_{ij})
, for
j=1,\ldots,l
.
∎

Therefore
\overline{s}\in\GL_{l}(p)
is a matrix representing
s
.

Recall from
Theorem

2.7.1
that the action of
s
on
U\coloneqq\langle u^{s^{\delta}}\rangle
is described by the relation (iii) of (
2.11
).

(i)

Let
\overline{s}^{*}
be the rational canonical form of
\overline{s}
, and let
A\in\GL_{l}(p)
be such that
A\overline{s}A^{-1}=\overline{s}^{*}
. In
Magma
this can be computed with the
PrimaryRationalForm
function, implemented as described in
[
55
]
.

(ii)

Locate the block of
\overline{s}^{*}
whose minimal polynomial is
m(X)
, the minimal polynomial of
\omega^{2\delta}
.

(iii)

Take any non-zero vector
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{l}
acted on by said block, and let
z=(z_{i})_{i=1}^{l}\coloneqq vA
.

(iv)

Let
u\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{l}\widehat{h}_{i}^{z_{i}}\in\GL_{d}(k)
, which corresponds to the adjoint representation of the torus element
h(z_{1},\ldots,z_{l})
.

Remark
.

Observe that if
q=p
then
v
is a vector acted on by the block with minimal polynomial
m(X)=X-\omega^{2\delta}
, thus
z
is simply an eigenvector of
\overline{s}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\omega^{2\delta}
.

When studying
\PSL_{2}(25)
, we are forced to take
\delta=2
in (
2.11
), therefore we use (
2.13
) instead. This means that at step (ii) above we use
\mu(X)
, the minimal polynomial of
\omega^{2}
, instead of
m(X)
. After computing
u
, we have to confirm that the relations (iii) and (iv) of (
2.12
) are satisfied.

We also discuss here how we check whether
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
is finite, and if so how to compute it, by using the fact that
s
is a representative in
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
of some element
w\in W(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
.

Recall from
Definition

2.3.1
that
W
is generated by the reflections of
\Phi
, and the action of
W
as permutation group over
\Phi
corresponds to an action of
W
as an automorphism of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
, obtained by permuting the root subspaces
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\Phi
. There is a representation of degree
l
of
W
, called
reflection representation
, which describes the action of
W
on a Cartan subalgebra
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma 3.3.2
.

Let
w^{*}\in N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
be a preimage of
w\in W
. Then
w
does not have an eigenspace with eigenvalue 1 in the reflection representation of
W
iff
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(w)
is finite.

Proof.

This is immediate from the definition of reflection representation. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Cartan subalgebra of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
; if
v\in\mathcal{H}
then the subspace
\langle v\rangle
is fixed by
w
iff
\langle\mathrm{ad}v\rangle\leq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
centralises
w^{*}
, and
\langle\mathrm{ad}v\rangle
is a 1-dimensional (infinite) torus.
∎

Remark
.

In
Magma
, if we have
\mathrm{G}
as a
GroupOfLieType
, then we can obtain
W
as a permutation group using the
WeylGroup
function. Then,
ReflectionGroup
gives the reflection representation of
W
.

Remark
.

If we have
s\in N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
, but we do not know the reflection representation of the image of
s
in
W(\mathrm{G})
, then we can still deduce that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
is finite if the conjugation action of
s
on
\mathrm{T}
has no fixed points: if
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
was infinite, it would be a subtorus
{(\overline{k}^{\times})}^{j}\leq{(\overline{k}^{\times})}^{l}\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
for
1\leq j\leq l
, which then would have nontrivial intersection with
\mathrm{T}
.

Observe that the opposite is not true in general, as the existence of a (finite) set of fixed points
\mathrm{T}^{*}
on
\mathrm{T}
does not imply the existence of infinite set of fixed points on
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
containing
\mathrm{T}^{*}
.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Let
s\in N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
. If
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
is finite, it can be constructed by computing
C_{E}(s)
, where
E
ranges among the elementary abelian subgroups of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
for the primes dividing the order
o(s)
of
s
.

Proof.

Let
A
be a finite subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
, and let
A=A_{1}\times A_{2}
, where
\lvert A_{2}\rvert
is coprime to
o(s)
and the set of prime divisors of
\lvert A_{1}\rvert
is a subset of the prime divisors of
o(s)
. Then, by the Schur-Zassenhaus theorem, all complements of
A_{2}
in
\langle A_{2},s\rangle
are
A_{2}\text{-conjugates}
, hence
C_{A}(s)\leq A_{1}
. Therefore, we may assume
A=A_{1}
, and the result follows.
∎

For
j\geq 0
and for any prime
p
dividing
\lvert k\rvert-1
, define
\mathrm{T}_{p,j}\coloneqq\langle h_{i}(\lambda^{\frac{\lvert k\rvert-1}{p^{j}}}):i=1,\ldots,l\rangle
where
\lambda
is a primitive element of
k
, in particular
\mathrm{T}_{p,j}
is a homocyclic subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
with
l
cyclic factors of order
p^{j}
, and
\mathrm{T}_{p,0}
is the trivial group for all
p
.

The result of
Lemma

3.3.3
means that if
o(s)=\prod_{p\in P}^{n}p^{a_{p}}
for some set
P
of primes, then for each
p\in P
we can find the
p\text{-part}
of
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
by computing
C_{\mathrm{T}_{p,i}}(s)
for
i=0,1,\ldots
, and stopping when
C_{\mathrm{T}_{p,i}}(s)=C_{\mathrm{T}_{p,i-1}}(s)
.

Observe that if the process has not yet stopped when reaching
i
such that
p^{i}
does not divide
\lvert k\rvert-1
, then we need to use a field extension
k^{j}
instead, for some
j>1
. The process will always stop after a finite number of steps since by assumption
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
is finite.

Since we will need to compute
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\langle u,s\rangle)
, we also mention the following result.

Lemma 3.3.4
.

Let
B\leq\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
and let
B^{\prime}
be the derived subgroup of
B
. If
B^{\prime}
is supersoluble then
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)\leq N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
for a maximal torus
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathrm{G}
.

Proof.

Observe first that
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)\leq N_{\mathrm{G}}(B^{\prime})
; indeed, for all
x,y\in B
and all
g\in N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)
we have

[x,y]^{g}=[x^{g},y^{g}]\in[B,B]=B^{\prime},

so
g\in N_{\mathrm{G}}(B^{\prime})
.

Since
B^{\prime}
is supersoluble, then
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B^{\prime})\leq N_{\mathrm{G}}(T)
for a maximal torus
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathrm{G}
by
Lemma

2.8.1
.
∎

We will apply this result when
B=\langle u,s\rangle
and
B^{\prime}
lies in a maximal torus
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathrm{G}
; in such case we will have that
B^{\prime}
is supersoluble and lies in
\mathrm{T}
, so we can construct generators of
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
, and we may compute
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)
by taking
N_{N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})}(B)
.

3.4
Constructing the final involution
t

Given a Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}
over
k
of dimension
d
, the corresponding Chevalley groups
\mathrm{G}(k)
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
, and
u,s\in\mathrm{G}
as matrices in
\GL_{d}(k)
constructed as in
Section

3.3
, we now want to construct the possible
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
such that
(u,s,t)
satisfy the presentation for
\PSL_{2}(q)
given by
Theorem

2.7.1
.

Observe that the relations
t^{2}=(st)^{2}=1
imply that each possible
t
is an involution of the form
t=ce
, where
c
centralises
s
, and
e
is an involution that inverts
s
. We construct
e\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
first, then use the relation
(ut)^{3}=1
to narrow down our search for
c
.

If we find any suitable
c\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
and compute the corresponding
t
, then we only have to confirm that the relation (viii) of (
2.11
) is satisfied.

3.4.1
A Chevalley basis relative to a semisimple element

The aim of this section is to describe methods to obtain a Chevalley basis on which a given semisimple element
s
acts diagonally. This will be needed in
Section

3.4.2
, where we use a matrix that realises the change of basis to diagonalise
s
.

Remark
.

We preface this section by mentioning that if we are working with
Magma
and we know
s
as a word in
x_{\pm i}(t)
and
h_{i}(\lambda)
, then in all cases but
\PSL_{2}(29)
we could use the
ConjugateIntoTorus
function instead: the second output is an element that conjugates
s
into an element of the torus generated by the
h_{i}(\lambda)
, so we can use it to map the current Chevalley basis to one that makes
s
diagonal.

This is not always the case as we may be working with a matrix in
\GL_{d}(k)
whose word is unknown to
Magma
. In general, we do not need to know the words involved, so we may use either algorithm.

We have a Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}
with
\dim\mathcal{L}=d
,
\rk\mathcal{L}=l
. Let
n=\frac{d-l}{2}
and let
\Phi=\left\{\alpha_{\pm 1},\ldots,\alpha_{\pm n}\right\}
be the root system of
\mathcal{L}
, with
\Pi=\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l}\right\}
a base of
\Phi
. We have that a Chevalley basis of
\mathcal{L}
is
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{-n},\ldots,\varepsilon_{-1},h_{\varepsilon 1},\ldots,h_{\varepsilon l},\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})
, where
h_{\varepsilon}=(h_{\varepsilon 1},\ldots,h_{\varepsilon l})
is a basis of a Cartan subalgebra
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{L}
, and
\varepsilon_{i}
generates the root space corresponding to
\alpha_{i}
. The reason for this ordering is described in Appendix
A.1
.

Furthermore, we have the automorphism group
\mathrm{G}
of
\mathcal{L}
as a matrix group in
\GL_{d}(k)
, and a semisimple element
s
of
\mathrm{G}
lying in the subgroup
\langle n_{i}|i=1,\ldots,l\rangle
.

We start by looking for a Cartan subalgebra
\mathcal{H}_{s}
corresponding to
s
, so we look at the action of
s
on
\mathcal{L}
, as
\mathcal{H}_{s}
is a subspace of the fixed point space
\mathcal{L}^{s}
as shown in
Lemma

2.6.4
. After computing
\mathcal{L}^{s}
, we decompose it into a direct sum made of a maximal abelian ideal
\mathcal{A}
and simple ideals
\mathcal{I}_{i}
. Note that the dimension of
\mathcal{A}
and the exact structure of each
\mathcal{I}_{i}
depend on what type of algebra
\mathcal{L}^{s}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
is, which is determined by the root subsystem
\Phi_{s}\subseteq\Phi
of roots whose root space is fixed by
s
.

Then, by taking a basis of
\mathcal{A}
and a basis of the Cartan subalgebra lying in each simple ideal, we obtain a basis
h_{\varepsilon_{s}}=(h_{\varepsilon_{s}1},\ldots,h_{\varepsilon_{s}l})
of a Cartan subalgebra
\mathcal{H}_{s}
of
\mathcal{L}
.

This can be done in
Magma
using the
CartanSubalgebra
function. If such tool is not available, other options include the following:

(i)

A possible approach is to find some vector whose adjoint representation has nullspace with dimension equal to the rank of
\mathcal{I}_{i}
, so that it has a good chance of being the Cartan subalgebra we are looking for.

(ii)

[
30
, Computation 3.5]
uses a similar approach based on Meat-axe.

(iii)

One could also find the whole Chevalley basis of
\mathcal{L}^{s}
using the algorithm described in
[
47
]
.

We point out that in the cases we will study,
\mathcal{L}^{s}
is a subalgebra of type
\mathrm{T}_{l}
or
\mathrm{A}_{1}\mathrm{T}_{l-1}
, so either we obtain directly a Cartan subalgebra, or little work is required to find one.

Now that we have a basis of
\mathcal{H}_{s}
, we find the root spaces
\mathcal{L}_{r}
of a Cartan decomposition of
\mathcal{L}
with Cartan subalgebra
\mathcal{H}_{s}
. We can do so by intersecting the eigenspaces of the matrices
\mathrm{ad}h_{\varepsilon_{s}i}\in\GL_{d}(k)
representing the adjoint action of the
h_{\varepsilon_{s}i}
, thus resulting in a Cartan decomposition of
\mathcal{L}
as described in
Section

2.4.2
.

By taking a generator
\varepsilon_{s,r}\in\mathcal{L}_{r}
for each root space, together with
l
generators for
\mathcal{H}_{s}
, we now have a Chevalley basis
\varepsilon_{s}=(\varepsilon_{s,1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{s,2n},h_{\varepsilon_{s}1},\ldots,h_{\varepsilon_{s}l})
for
\mathcal{L}
made of eigenvectors for
s
. Note that at this point we can compute the root spaces, but we do not know what is the corresponding root, hence the different labelling of the basis (
1,\ldots,2n
instead of
\pm 1,\ldots,\pm n
).

We then rearrange the elements of
\varepsilon_{s}
in such a way that they match the order of
\varepsilon
, by which we mean that elements in the same position will correspond to the same root. While this is not strictly necessary, it will help us perform the next computations, since we can associate a root to a generator of the corresponding root space just from their position in the list.

The way we chose to do this is by constructing a graph
\Gamma
for
\varepsilon
whose vertices are the generators
\varepsilon_{i}
of the root spaces, and two vertices are connected by an edge if the corresponding generators have non-zero commutator. In the same way, we construct
\Gamma_{s}
for
\varepsilon_{s}
. By finding an isomorphism between the two graphs, we obtain a bijection between the sets of indices, which tells us how to reorder
\varepsilon_{s}
in order to match
\varepsilon
, say

\varepsilon_{s}=(\varepsilon_{s,-n},\ldots,\varepsilon_{s,-1},h_{\varepsilon_{s}1},\ldots,h_{\varepsilon_{s}l},\varepsilon_{s,1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{s,n}).

(3.4)

Remark
.

Magma
handles graph isomorphisms by using McKay’s
nauty
program, based on
[
49
]
. More recent work on the topic is described in
[
51
]
, and the program is available and kept up to date at
[
50
]
.

It is important
not
to include the generators of the Cartan subalgebras
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}_{s}
in this construction: different choices for their bases lead to different coefficients for the action on the root spaces, therefore there would be
l
vertices of
\Gamma_{s}
impossible to control.

We perform another modification to
\varepsilon_{s}
that will come in handy in
Section

3.4.2
, that is we rescale the generators
\varepsilon_{s,r}
of the root spaces
\mathcal{L}_{r}
so that
\varepsilon_{s}
has the same structure constants as
\varepsilon
; we also change the choice of
h_{\varepsilon_{s}}
in order to match the action of
h_{\varepsilon}
on the generators
\varepsilon_{r}
of the root spaces as well. A way of doing so is the following:

(i)

Compute a
\mathrm{G}\text{-invariant}
bilinear form
\langle-,-\rangle
for
\mathcal{L}
(for example, the Killing form).

(ii)

Rescale the vectors
\varepsilon_{s,r}
for
r=-1,\ldots,-l
(i.e.
\alpha_{r}\in-\Pi
) so that
\langle\varepsilon_{s,r},\varepsilon_{s,-r}\rangle=\langle\varepsilon_{r},\varepsilon_{-r}\rangle
for all such
r
, and define
R=\Pi\cup-\Pi
.

(iii)

Take
\alpha_{r}\in\Phi-R
and compute an extraspecial pair
(\alpha_{r_{1}},\alpha_{r_{2}})
such that
\alpha_{r_{1}}+\alpha_{r_{2}}=\alpha_{r}
. If
\alpha_{r_{1}},\alpha_{r_{2}}\in R
then rescale
\varepsilon_{s,r}
in such a way that
\frac{[\varepsilon_{s,r_{1}},\varepsilon_{s,r_{2}}]}{\varepsilon_{s,r}}=\frac{[\varepsilon_{r_{1}},\varepsilon_{r_{2}}]}{\varepsilon_{r}}
, and add
\alpha_{r}
to
R
. If either
\alpha_{r_{1}}\notin R
or
\alpha_{r_{2}}\notin R
, repeat this step by taking
\alpha_{r_{1}}
or
\alpha_{r_{2}}
instead of
\alpha_{r}
, respectively. Repeat this step until
R=\Phi
.

(iv)

Compute
h_{s,r}=[\varepsilon_{s,r},\varepsilon_{s,-r}]
for
r=1,\ldots,l
(i.e.
\alpha_{r}\in\Pi
), and use them to get a new basis
h^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{s}}=(h^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{s}1},\ldots,h^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{s}l})
of
\mathcal{H}_{s}
by taking linear combinations of the
h_{s,r}
. We want the new basis
h^{\prime}_{s}
to satisfy
\frac{[h_{\varepsilon r},\varepsilon_{i}]}{\varepsilon_{i}}=\frac{[h^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{s}r},\varepsilon_{s,i}]}{\varepsilon_{s,i}}

for all
1\leq r\leq l
,
i\in\left\{\pm 1,\ldots,\pm n\right\}
(i.e. for all
\alpha_{r}\in\Pi
,
\alpha_{i}\in\Phi
).

In our case, for (iv) we used
h^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{s}i}=\sum_{j=1}^{l}(C^{-1})_{i,j}h_{s,j}
,
i=1,\ldots,l
, where
C
is the Cartan matrix of
\mathrm{G}
, in order to match the standard basis for the Cartan subalgebra used by
Magma
; see Appendix
A.1
.

Now the basis
\varepsilon_{s}
has the same structure constants of
\varepsilon
, and its elements are ordered in the same way, which makes the result in
Section

3.4.2
straightforward to obtain.

3.4.2
An involution inverting a semisimple element

Given a semisimple element
s\in\mathrm{G}
and its corresponding Chevalley basis
\varepsilon_{s}
, constructed as in
Section

3.4.1
, it is straightforward to construct an involution of
\mathrm{G}
that inverts
s
, in particular we can consider the Chevalley involution
\iota
described in
Section

2.4.5
.

Lemma 3.4.1
.

Let
s_{c}\in\mathrm{G}
be the matrix in
\GL_{d}(k)
realising the change of basis from
\varepsilon
to
\varepsilon_{s}
, that is
s_{c}
diagonalises
s
. Let
A\in\mathrm{G}
be a matrix with entries
-1
on the anti-diagonal, except in the middle
l\times l
entries, where we take
-1
on the diagonal, and 0 everywhere else. Then an involution
e\in\mathrm{G}
that inverts
s
is given by
A^{s_{c}^{-1}}
.

Proof.

Since
s_{c}
maps
\varepsilon
to
\varepsilon_{s}
, a basis of eigenvectors of
s
, we have that
s_{c}ss_{c}^{-1}=s^{*}
is a diagonal matrix.

Because of the specific ordering we chose for
\varepsilon_{s}
, see (
3.4
), it is clear that
A
is a matrix realising the automorphism of
\mathcal{L}
given by the Chevalley involution on
\varepsilon_{s}
described by (
2.9
).

Let
e\coloneqq A^{s_{c}^{-1}}
, since
A
is an involution it is clear that
e
is an involution as well, and
e\in\mathrm{G}
since both
A,s_{c}\in\mathrm{G}
, as they are matrices realising a change of Chevalley bases.

The claim can be written as

\displaystyle s^{-1}

\displaystyle=s^{e}

\displaystyle=s_{c}^{-1}As_{c}ss_{c}^{-1}As_{c}

\displaystyle=s_{c}^{-1}As^{*}As_{c},

which is equivalent to

{s^{*}}^{-1}=As^{*}A.

(3.5)

Let
s^{*}=\text{diag}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{d})
, then a matrix multiplication shows that

As^{*}A=\text{diag}(s_{d},\ldots,s_{n+l+1},s_{n+1},\ldots,s_{n+l},s_{n},\ldots,s_{1}),

while we have

{s^{*}}^{-1}=\text{diag}(s_{1}^{-1},\ldots,s_{n}^{-1},s_{n+1}^{-1},\ldots,s_{n+l}^{-1},s_{n+l+1}^{-1},\ldots,s_{d}^{-1}).

In order to prove (
3.5
), we have to show that
s_{i}^{-1}=s_{d+1-i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, and
s_{i}^{-1}=s_{i}
for
i=n+1,\ldots,n+l
.

This follows because
\varepsilon_{s}
is a Chevalley basis for
s
, and
s^{*}
is the diagonal matrix representing the action of
s
on
\varepsilon_{s}
. In particular,
s^{*}
is the adjoint representation of some element
h_{\varepsilon_{s}}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{l})=h_{\varepsilon_{s}1}(\lambda_{1})\ldots h_{\varepsilon_{s}l}(\lambda_{l})
, for some
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{l}\in k^{\times}
, and the action of on element of the form
h_{\varepsilon_{s}j}(\lambda_{j})
is described by (
2.7
):

(i)

Let
1\leq i\leq n
, then
\varepsilon_{s,i}
is an eigenvector of
\mathrm{ad}h_{\varepsilon_{s}j}(\lambda_{j})
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}^{A_{j,i}}
. On the other hand,
\varepsilon_{s,-i}
is an eigenvector with eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}^{A_{j,-i}}=\lambda_{j}^{-A_{j,i}}=(\lambda_{j}^{A_{j,i}})^{-1}
, where the first equality follows by
Lemma

2.4.7
. This holds for all
1\leq j\leq l
, and by the ordering of
\varepsilon_{s}
we chose,
s_{n+l+i}
is the eigenvalue of
\varepsilon_{s,i}
and
s_{n+1-i}
is the eigenvaule of
\varepsilon_{s,-i}
. Therefore,
s_{i}^{-1}=s_{d+1-i}
.

(ii)

Let
i=n+j
,
1\leq j\leq l
, then
s_{i}
is the eigenvalue of the eigenvector
h_{\varepsilon_{s}j}
, so
s_{i}=1
and
s_{i}^{-1}=s_{i}
.

∎

3.4.3
How to deal with
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)

The issue now is to construct
c\in C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
, since we can work directly only in
\mathrm{G}
. The idea is to construct a subgroup
C(s)\leq C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
that is as “close” to
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
as possible, and work on the linear span
M(s)
of
C(s)
, by which we mean the matrix subalgebra of
M_{d}(k)
generated by
k\text{-linear}
combinations of elements of
C(s)
. Likewise, we denote by
\boldsymbol{M}(s)
the linear span of
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
, a matrix subalgebra of
M_{d}(\overline{k})
.

Clearly,
\dim\boldsymbol{M}(s)\geq\dim M(s)
; if we can show that the equality holds, then we have that
\boldsymbol{M}(s)=M(s)\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
and we can study
\boldsymbol{M}(s)
using a basis
\Set{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}}
of
M(s)
.

By
Theorem

2.6.1
, we know the structure of
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
, which depends on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{s}
. Observe that
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{s}=\mathcal{L}^{s}\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
, and we already studied
\mathcal{L}^{s}
in
Section

3.4.1
, where we obtained a direct sum of a maximal abelian ideal
\mathcal{A}
and some simple ideals
\mathcal{I}_{i}
. In particular, we have that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
contains
\dim\mathcal{A}
copies of
\overline{k}^{\times}
and the root subgroups corresponding to roots fixed by
s
, whose exact type depends on the
\mathcal{I}_{i}
. We define
C(s)
to be a group with the same structure, but defined over
k
, in such a way that
C(s)
stabilises the same spaces of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
that are stabilised by
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
.

This is not true in general, mostly depending on
k
, so we will have to prove it in each case.

Example 3.4.2
.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
is of type
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{8}
, and
s\in\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
semisimple stabilises the root spaces corresponding to the roots
\pm\alpha,\pm\beta
, with
\alpha\neq\pm\beta
and
\alpha+\beta\notin\Phi
. By
Corollary

2.6.3

C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}_{\pm\alpha},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}_{\pm\beta}\rangle
, i.e. it is generated by a maximal torus
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
and two
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
groups; then we take
C(s)
to be the group generated by
\mathrm{T}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}^{\sigma}
and two
\mathrm{A}_{1}(k)
groups, generated by
x_{\pm\alpha}(1)
and
x_{\pm\beta}(1)
.

Since we already have a Chevalley basis
\varepsilon_{s}
corresponding to
s
from
Section

3.4.1
, it is straightforward to obtain the generators of
C(s)
by taking the suitable
h_{i}(\lambda)
,
x_{\pm i}(1)
,
n_{i}
, as described in
Section

3.1
, using
\varepsilon_{s}
instead of
\varepsilon
.

We can now find
\dim M(s)
. One could proceed for example as in
[
30
, Computations 3.8 and 3.9]
and use a probabilistic method:

(i)

Initialise an empty list.

(ii)

Generate a random element of
C(s)
and verify whether it is linearly independent to the elements of the list. If it is, add it to the list.

(iii)

Repeat step (ii) until a linearly dependent element is generated
m
times in a row, and then stop.

The higher the value for
m
, the more likely is that the elements of the list form a basis for
M(s)
.

Remark
.

Working with
Magma
, this is straightforward as we can use built-in functions to construct directly the matrix algebra
M_{d}(k)
, then ask for a basis of the subalgebra generated by the generators of
C(s)
.

Instead of trying to get
\dim\boldsymbol{M}(s)
explicitly, we compute an upper bound for it, and if said bound coincides with
\dim M(s)
then
\boldsymbol{M}(s)=M(s)\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

Suppose that the
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)\text{-module}

\overline{\mathcal{L}}
has a direct sum decomposition

0^{l-\dim\mathcal{A}}\bigoplus_{j\in J}j^{n_{j}}

(3.6)

for a set
J
of dimensions, meaning that it decomposes as a sum of
l-\dim\mathcal{A}
copies of the trivial module, and
n_{j}
modules of dimension
j
, not necessarily isomorphic to one another, for
j\in J
. Then

\dim\boldsymbol{M}(s)\leq 1+\sum_{j\in J}n_{j}\cdot j^{2}.

(3.7)

Proof.

Let
V
be a non-trivial summand of (
3.6
), then the action of
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
on
V
is represented by a subgroup
E
of
\Endo(V)\simeq\GL(V)
, therefore the dimension of the linear span of
E
is at most
(\dim V)^{2}
, i.e. the dimension of the linear span of
\GL(V)
.

Since the action on the set of trivial modules contributes with a 1-dimensional linear span, the result follows by adding together the upper bounds of the contributions of all summands.
∎

Remark
.

The decomposition (
3.6
) satisfies
l-\dim\mathcal{A}+\sum_{j\in J}n_{j}\cdot j=d
. This is clear from the fact that it is the decomposition of a module of dimension
d
; each copy of the trivial module contributes with dimension 1, and for each
j\in J
there are
n_{j}
summands of dimension
j
.

In order to compute the bound, we want to use the decomposition of the
C(s)\text{-module}

\overline{\mathcal{L}}
instead, and this can be done only if
C(s)
and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
stabilise the same subspaces of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
.

This does not happen in general, but when studying the embedding listed in
Theorem

1.0.2
we only encountered a limited number of cases for which this is easy to verify.

Lemma 3.4.4
.

If
s
is a regular element of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, then
C(s)
and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
stabilise the same subspaces of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

If
s
is a regular element then it does not stabilise any root subspace hence by
Theorem

2.6.1
its centraliser is simply a maximal torus
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
; in particular,
C(s)
is
\mathrm{T}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}^{\sigma}
. It is clear then that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
and
\mathrm{T}
have the same action on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
, that is
0^{l}\oplus 1^{d-l}
.
∎

Lemma 3.4.5
.

Let
\mathrm{G}
be of type
\mathrm{E}_{7,\mathrm{ad}}
over
k
and
s
stabilise two root spaces, corresponding to some roots
\pm\alpha
of
\Phi(\mathrm{G})
. If
\lvert k\rvert>u(\mathrm{G})\cdot(2,p-1)
, where
p=\ch k
and
u(G)
is defined in
Theorem

2.10.3
, then
C(s)
and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
stabilise the same subspaces of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

2.6.1
we have that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}_{\pm\alpha},n_{w}\colon s^{w}=s\rangle
. Since
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
is a torus and
n_{w}
can be taken to lie in
\mathrm{G}
, we only need to understand the action on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}_{\pm\alpha}
and
\mathrm{X}_{\pm\alpha}
, i.e. an
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
group and an
\mathrm{A}_{1}(k)
group, respectively.

We can use
[
42
, Theorem 1]
: let
\mathrm{X}\coloneqq\langle X_{\pm\alpha}\rangle
be the
\mathrm{A}_{1}(k)
group, and assume that
\lvert k\rvert>u(\mathrm{G})\cdot(2,p-1)
; then there exists a closed connected subgroup
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
containing
\mathrm{X}
such that every
\mathrm{X}\text{-invariant}
subspace of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
is also
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}\text{-invariant}
.

Since
\mathrm{X}
centralises
s
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
stabilises the same spaces as
\mathrm{X}
, then
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
must lie in
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
. Since
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
is a closed connected subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, it is contained in
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
, and it contains an
\mathrm{A}_{1}(k)
subgroup, then
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
contains an
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
subgroup and is contained in a connected component of
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
, that is a subgroup of type
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
.

Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}_{\pm\alpha}
and
\mathrm{X}_{\pm\alpha}
stabilise the same subspaces of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
, hence the same holds for
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
and
C(s)
.
∎

Remark
.

In
[
30
, Lemma 4.2]
they use a different approach and compute the
n_{j}
in (
3.6
) directly. By taking
R
to be the roots of
\Phi
whose corresponding root subspace is fixed by
s
, they deduce the
n_{j}
by going through every root
\alpha\in\Phi
and counting how many element of
R

\alpha
is orthogonal to.

3.4.4
Constructing
t

Now that we have a linear space of matrices
\boldsymbol{M}(s)
where we know the element
c
described on page
3.4
lies, we can narrow down our options by using the relation
(ut)^{3}=1
.

Proposition 3.4.6
.

Let
u,e\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\leq\GL_{d}(\overline{k})
, and let
t=ce
be an involution, where
c\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. Let
c=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}c_{i}
, where
\left\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\right\}
is a basis of the linear span of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
, and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\overline{k}
. Then
(tu)^{3}=1
iff
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
is a solution of the system of equations given by

v\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n}X_{i}(euc_{i}eu-u^{-1}c_{i}e)=0,

(3.8)

where
v
ranges through a basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}}
.

Proof.

The condition
(tu)^{3}=1
is not linear, but we can rewrite it as

ceuceu=u^{-1}e^{-1}c^{-1}=u^{-1}(ce)^{-1}=u^{-1}ce,

where the last equality follows from the fact that
t=ce
is an involution.

Let
v
be an element of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
fixed by
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
, then we have
v\cdot c=v
and
v\cdot euceu=v\cdot u^{-1}ce
. We can then write the equation as

\displaystyle v\cdot euceu

\displaystyle=v\cdot u^{-1}ce,

\displaystyle v\cdot(euceu-u^{-1}ce)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle v\cdot\biggl(eu\biggl(\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}c_{i}\biggr)eu-u^{-1}\biggl(\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}c_{i}\biggr)e\biggr)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle v\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}(euc_{i}eu-u^{-1}c_{i}e)

\displaystyle=0,

where
a_{i}\in\overline{k}
.
∎

We solve this system of equations in the case where
