\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
is
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
, whose linear span is
\boldsymbol{M}(s)
.

By ranging
v
through a basis of the fixed point space of
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
, and by splitting each equation (
3.8
) into one for each component of
v
, we obtain a system of
\dim\mathcal{A}\cdot d
equations in the variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
. The number of equations is simply given by the dimension of the fixed point space (
\dim\mathcal{A}
, from
Section

3.4.3
) times the number of components of a vector of such space as an element of
\mathcal{L}
, that is the dimension
d
of
\mathcal{L}
. A solution to this homogeneous system determines a subspace
\boldsymbol{M}_{0}(s)\leq\boldsymbol{M}(s)
where
c
lies, so that
t\in(\boldsymbol{M}_{0}(s)\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})e
.

How to proceed now depends on
\dim\boldsymbol{M}_{0}(s)
: if it is small enough, then we may be able to find some other linear conditions to further reduce the space containing
c
. Ideally, we would want to find exactly
\dim\boldsymbol{M}_{0}(s)
choices for
c
, and show that they all lie in
\mathrm{G}
.

We will deal with each specific case individually, as we will use a few different approaches, depending mainly on
\dim\boldsymbol{M}_{0}(s)
, but also on
q
and
k
.

3.5
LDU decomposition

Definition 3.5.1
.

Let
M
be a
n\times n
matrix. An
LDU decomposition
of
M
is a factorisation
M=LDU
, where
L
is a lower unitriangular matrix,
D
is a diagonal matrix, and
U
is an upper unitriangular matrix.

Remark
.

If
M
is defined over a field, then
M
admits a LDU decomposition iff every principal minor of
M
, defined as
\det(M_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq k}
for
k=1,\ldots,n
, is nonzero.

Lemma 3.5.2
.

If
M
admits a
LDU
decomposition, then it is unique.

Proof.

Let
M=LDU
and
M=L^{\prime}D^{\prime}U^{\prime}
be two such decompositions, then
L^{\prime-1}L=D^{\prime}U^{\prime}U^{-1}D^{-1}
. The left hand side is a lower unitriangular matrix, while the right hand side is an upper triangular matrix, so they both must be the identity matrix, and
L=L^{\prime}
. Then we have
D^{\prime-1}D=U^{\prime}U^{-1}
; the left hand side is a diagonal matrix, while the right hand side is an upper unitriangular matrix, so they both must be the identity matrix, and
D=D^{\prime}
,
U=U^{\prime}
.
∎

Since we need it only for small matrices, we will compute the LDU decomposition of a matrix in a naive way.

Let
M=(m_{ij})
be an invertible
n\times n
matrix,
L=(l_{ij})
be a lower unitriangular matrix,
U=(u_{ij})
a upper unitriangular matrix, and
D
be a diagonal matrix with non-zero entries
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}
,
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

If
M=LDU
then it is straightforward to verify by computing the matrix multiplication that

m_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{\min(i,j)}d_{k}l_{ik}u_{kj}.

(3.9)

Therefore, if an LDU decomposition exists, we can compute the matrices
L,D,U
in the following way:

(i)

Set
d_{1}=m_{11}
, then
u_{1j}=m_{1j}/d_{1}
and
l_{i1}=m_{i1}/d_{1}
.

(ii)

Set
r=2
.

(iii)

Compute
d_{r}=m_{rr}-d_{1}l_{r1}u_{1r}-\ldots-d_{r-1}l_{r,r-1}u_{r-1,r}
. This is feasible since it only depends on
d_{k}
,
l_{kr}
,
u_{rk}
for
r<k
, which have already been computed.

(iv)

Compute the terms
u_{rj}
,
j>r
(resp.
l_{ir}

i>r
), using (
3.9
). This is feasible since they only depend on
d_{k}
,
u_{kj}
,
l_{rk}
, for
k<r
, and
d_{r}
(resp.
d_{k}
,
u_{kr}
,
l_{ik}
, for
k<r
, and
d_{r}
), which have been already computed.

(v)

If
r<n
, increase
r
by 1 and go to
(iii)
.

Remark
.

One can see that computing the terms
u_{ij}
and
l_{ij}
requires taking inverses of the
d_{i}
’s, therefore:

(i)

If
M
is defined over an integral domain
I
that is not a field, then
L,D,U
cannot be defined unless we allow them to have entries in the field of fractions of
I
.

(ii)

Even if
M
is defined over a field, if
d_{k}=0
then the LDU decomposition cannot be computed; this happens when the
k\text{-th}
principal minor of
M
is 0, as mentioned in a previous remark.

3.6
Elements of
\SL_{n+1}(q)
as words of
\mathrm{A}_{n}

Fix
n>0
and consider the simply connected group of Lie type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
over a field
k
, which has a standard representation
\rho\colon\mathrm{A}_{n}\rightarrow\SL_{n+1}(k)
. Given element
M\in\SL_{n+1}(k)
, our goal is to find its preimage in
\mathrm{A}_{n}
as a product of terms of the form
x_{r}(t)
and
h_{r}(t)
,
1\leq r\leq n
,
t\in k
.

Remark
.

We preface this section by mentioning that if we are working with
Magma
, this can be done automatically. For example, take
n=3
and
k=\mathbb{F}_{19}
. Then, using

⬇

>

G
:=
GroupOfLieType
(
"A3"
,19);

>

rho
:=
StandardRepresentation
(
G
);

>

H
:=
SL
(4,19);

>

M
:=
Random
(
H
);

>

w
:=
M@@rho
;

we obtain the preimage
w\in\mathrm{A}_{3}(19)
under
\rho
of a random element
M\in\SL_{4}(19)
.

In
Magma
, the standard representation is constructed using the algorithm in
[
17
]
, which gives a way to move between a finite group of Lie type and its standard representation.

The way
Magma
constructs the standard representation of
\mathrm{A}_{n}
is explained in
[
17
, Example 8.2]
, so we will briefly describe it, then show a way to construct
\rho^{-1}(M)
when
M\in\SL_{n+1}(k)
is a matrix that has a LDU decomposition.

We denote by
\mathbb{1}
the identity element of
\SL_{n+1}(k)
, and by
\mathbb{1}_{ij}
the elementary matrix with entries 1 in position
(i,j)
, and 0 everywhere else.

Recall also that the root system of type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
consists of a set
\Phi=\left\{\alpha_{\pm 1},\ldots,\alpha_{\pm\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}\right\}
of roots, with a fundamental set of
n
roots.

Let the indexing of the roots of
\Phi
be such that
\Pi=\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\right\}
is a base of
\Phi
, and let
\gamma
be the map

\left\{1,\ldots,n+1\right\}^{2}\rightarrow\Phi
given by

\gamma\colon(i,j)\mapsto\sum_{k=0}^{\lvert i-j\rvert-1}\alpha_{(\sgn(i-j))(\min(i,j)+k)},

for
1\leq i\neq j\leq n+1
, where
\sgn
denotes the sign function.

It may be easier to visualise
\gamma
as the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}-&\alpha_{-1}&\alpha_{-(n+1)}&\alpha_{-2n}&\cdots&\alpha_{-\left(\frac{n(n+1)}{2}\right)}\\
\alpha_{1}&-&\alpha_{-2}&\alpha_{-(n+2)}&\cdots&\alpha_{-\left(\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-1\right)}\\
\alpha_{n+1}&\alpha_{2}&-&\alpha_{-3}&\cdots&\alpha_{-\left(\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-3\right)}\\
\alpha_{2n}&\alpha_{n+2}&\alpha_{3}&-&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\alpha_{-n}\\
\alpha_{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}&\alpha_{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-1}&\alpha_{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-3}&\cdots&\alpha_{n}&-\end{pmatrix},

where the entry in position
(i,j)
is
\gamma(i,j)
. Here,
\alpha_{n+1}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
,
\alpha_{n+2}=\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}
,
\alpha_{2n}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}
, and so on, up to
\alpha_{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}=\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}
.

Recall from
Lemma

2.3.11
that in type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
the roots are all of the form
\sum_{i=0}^{j}\alpha_{\pm(k+i)}
, where the choice of sign is the same for all summands, so the image of
\gamma
is indeed
\Phi
.

Then for
r=1,\ldots,n(n+1)
we have that
\rho(x_{r}(t))=\mathbb{1}+(-1)^{i+j+1}t\mathbb{1}_{ij}
, where
\gamma(i,j)=\alpha_{r}
, while the image of
h_{r}(t)=\alpha_{r}^{\vee}\otimes t
is a diagonal matrix in
\SL_{n+1}(k)
with entries
t
and
t^{-1}
in positions
j
and
i
respectively, where
\gamma(i,j)=\alpha_{r}
, and 1 everywhere else.

Although we do not use the elements
n_{r}
, we have that
\rho(n_{r})=\mathbb{1}+(-1)^{i+j+1}\mathbb{1}_{ij}+(-1)^{i+j}\mathbb{1}_{ji}
, where
\gamma(i,j)=\alpha_{r}
.

Remark
.

Observe that this representation is consistent with the mapping
\phi_{r}:\SL_{2}(k)\rightarrow\langle X_{r},X_{-r}\rangle
described in
Section

2.4.4
, by taking
\rho(x_{r}(t))
,
\rho(h_{r}(t))
,
\rho(n_{r})
, and considering only the entries in position
(i,i)\,(i,j),(j,i),(j,j)
. It also matches the structure of the root system, as we can verify that
[x_{r}(1),x_{s}(1)]
is
x_{r+s}(\pm 1)
if
r+s
is a root, and is
\mathbb{1}
if
r\neq-s
and
r+s
is not a root.

Since the set of
x_{r}(t)
with
r
a positive (resp. negative) root generates the lower (resp. upper) unitriangular matrices of
\SL_{n+1}(k)
, and the set of
h_{r}(t)
generates the diagonal matrices, we have a way to write an element of
\SL_{n+1}(k)
as a word in unipotent and toral elements, assuming that it admits a LDU decomposition.

Indeed, let
M\in\SL_{n+1}(k)
have an LDU decomposition, we can write
L
using
x_{r}(t)
for
r
positive roots,
D
using
h_{r}(t)
, and
U
using
x_{r}(t)
for
r
negative roots.

Given a lower unitriangular matrix
L
, we can proceed as follows:

(i)

Let
\rho\colon\mathrm{A}_{n}\rightarrow\SL_{n+1}(k)
be the standard representation, which we described above for
x_{r}(t)
and
h_{r}(t)
. Initialise
w
to be the empty word, that is
\rho(w)=\mathbb{1}
.

(ii)

Set
c=1
.

(iii)

Take the entries
l_{ij}
with
i-j=c
, e.g. if
c=1
that would be the entries just below the diagonal, and post-multiply
w
by
x_{\gamma(i,j)}((-1)^{c+1}(l_{ij}-\rho(w)_{ij}))
for
j=1,\ldots,n-c
.

(iv)

If
c=n-1
then stop, otherwise increase
c
by 1 and return to the previous step.

Proof.

Observe that each time we repeat step (iii), we are post-multiplying
w
by
x_{r}(t)
, for some
1\leq r\leq n-1
,
t\in k
, and that
\rho(x_{r}(t))=\mathbb{1}+(-1)^{i+j+1}t\mathbb{1}_{ij}
is a matrix with at most one non-diagonal non-zero element
(-1)^{i+j+1}t
in position
(i,j)
, with
\gamma(i,j)=\alpha_{r}
. Right multiplication by
\rho(x_{r}(t))
can only affect the entries in position
(i,j),\ldots,(i,n)
, therefore since we traverse the set of indices by taking
c=1,\ldots,n-1
,
j=1,\ldots,n-c
, and
i=j+c
, we are never going to affect entries we already visited.

Since the entry
(i,j)
of
\rho(wx_{r}(t))
is
\rho(w)_{ij}+(-1)^{i+j+1}t
, we just need to take
t=(-1)^{i+j+1}(l_{ij}-\rho(w)_{ij})
.
∎

The same algorithm is used to compute the word for
U
, provided that in (iii) we consider entries
l_{ij}
with
i-j=-c
, and we let
j
range from
c+1
to
n
instead.

For
D
, the principle is the same: pick
t_{1}=d_{11}
so that
\rho(h_{1}(t_{1}))_{11}=d_{11}
, and then work
h_{i}(t_{i})
(
2\leq i\leq n-1
) by comparing
d_{ii}
and
\rho(h_{1}(t_{1})\cdots h_{i-1}(t_{i-1}))_{ii}
.

Proof.

Since
\rho(h_{r}(t))
has two entries that may be
\neq 1
in position
(r,r)
and
(r+1,r+1)
in the diagonal, we can consider just elements
h_{r}(t)
with
r=1,\ldots,n
, as they generate the whole group of diagonal matrices.

Since the entry in position (1,1) is fixed by the choice of
h_{1}(t_{1})
, then
t_{1}=d_{11}
. Assume we chose
h_{1}(t_{1}),\ldots,h_{c-1}(t_{c-1})
,
1\leq c\leq n
, then the entry in position
(c,c)
is fixed by the choice of
h_{c}(t_{c})
. In particular, we have that
\rho(h_{1}(t_{1})\ldots h_{c-1}(t_{c-1})_{cc}=t_{c-1}^{-1}
and
\rho(h_{c}(t_{c}))_{cc}=t_{c}
, so we take
t_{c}=l_{cc}t_{c-1}
.

Observe that the entry in position
(n+1,n+1)
cannot be decided, as it is only affected by the choice of
t_{n}
, which is fixed by the entry in position
(n,n)
. This is not a concern, as we are working with matrices in
\SL_{n+1}(k)
, so the last entry will automatically be correct as it makes the determinant 1. ∎

3.7
Construction of a
G\text{-invariant}
trilinear form

Given a group
G
and a
kG\text{-module}

V
, we can construct
G\text{-invariant}
trilinear forms defined over
V
, if any exists. In particular, if the fixed point space of
\sym^{3}(V)
is 1-dimensional, then we can construct the unique (up to scalars)
G\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear form on
V
(see
Lemma

2.11.13
and the following remarks).

This will be relevant when we will construct the
\Sp_{8}\!\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear form on the natural module for
\mathrm{E}_{6}
. As it is unique, and we work in characteristic
\neq 2,3
, the form has to be the
\mathrm{E}_{6}\text{-form}
described in
Definition

2.11.9
.

While we focus on the case where the symmetric trilinear form is unique, this strategy can be used in any other situation, with the final result being some space of solution of dimension equal to that of the fixed point space of
\sym^{3}(V)
.

Let
f
be a trilinear symmetric form defined over
V
; given a basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
of
V
, we need to find the structure constants
f(e_{i},e_{j},e_{k})
, which completely determine
f
. In order to do so, we solve a linear system of equations of the form

f(u,v,w)-f(gu,gv,gw)=0,

where
u,v,w\in V
and
g\in G\leq\GL_{n}(k)
. The variables are the structure constants, so once we obtain
\dim(\sym^{3}(V))-1=\binom{n+2}{3}-1
linearly independent equations, the resulting 1-dimensional space of solutions will be the structure constants of
f
, up to scalars.

Taking random elements
g
of
G
and acting on all the possible choices of
(e_{i},e_{j},e_{k})
is a viable strategy, but the computation time scales badly with
n
. This process can be sped up significantly by finding elements of
G
that are diagonal: let
g\in G\leq\GL_{n}(k)
be a diagonal matrix with entries
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
, then

0=f(e_{i},e_{j},e_{k})-f(ge_{i},ge_{j},ge_{k})=(1-d_{i}d_{j}d_{k})f(e_{i},e_{j},e_{k}),

hence if
d_{i}d_{j}d_{k}\neq 1
then
f(e_{i},e_{j},e_{k})=0
, which removes one variable from the linear system.

The computation time can be further reduced if the matrix for
g
is sparse, as having many zero entries makes computing the values of
f
faster, but the impact of this choice depends on
n
and the number of variables of the linear system.

3.8
Restriction of a trilinear form

Let
G
be a finite group,
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
a
kG\text{-module}
, and
f
a
G\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear form on
V
. As shown in
Lemma

2.11.15
,
f
can be decomposed in terms of
\left.f\right|_{V_{1}}
,
\left.f\right|_{V_{2}}
,
\left.f\right|_{V_{1}\times V_{2}\times V_{2}}
, and
\left.f\right|_{V_{2}\times V_{1}\times V_{1}}
, so we are interested in studying objects like
\hom_{kG}(V_{1},\sym^{2}(V_{2}^{*}))
and
\sym^{3}(V_{1})^{G}
.

In
Section

5.2
, we will find a situation where we do not know much about
f
, but we know its behaviour when taking
V\!\!\downarrow_{H}
,
H\leq G
. For example, if we know that
\left.f\right|_{V_{1}\!\downarrow_{H}\times V_{2}\!\downarrow_{H}\times V_{2}\!\downarrow_{H}}
is constantly 0, we may want to know whether that is the case for
\left.f\right|_{V_{1}\times V_{2}\times V_{2}}
.

In the case of
\left.f\right|_{V_{1}\times V_{2}\times V_{2}}
, we want to compare
A\coloneqq\hom_{kG}(V_{1},\sym^{2}(V_{2}^{*}))
with
B\coloneqq\hom_{kH}(V_{1}\!\!\downarrow_{H},\sym^{2}(V_{2}^{*}\!\!\downarrow_{H}))
. This is fairly straightforward, as both
A
and
B
can be represented as subspaces of the matrix algebra
M_{\dim V_{1},\dim\sym^{2}(V_{2}^{*})}(k)
, therefore we take a basis
a=\left\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\dim A}\right\}
of
A
and write each
a_{i}
as a linear combination of a basis
b=\left\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{\dim B}\right\}
of
B
. We then proceed differently depending on
\dim A
,
\dim B
, and the specific relations between
a
and
b
we obtain.

A similar analysis can be done for
\left.f\right|_{V_{1}}
, where we take
A\coloneqq\sym^{3}(V_{1})^{G}
and
B\coloneqq\sym^{3}(V_{1}\!\!\downarrow_{H})^{H}
, by working in
M_{\dim\sym^{3}(V_{1})}(k)
instead.

Chapter 4
Primitive subgroups

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
an exceptional algebraic group of Lie type and
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
, where
\sigma
is a Steinberg endomorphism of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

In this chapter we study the embedding of
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
in characteristic 0 or coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, and in
\mathrm{G}
, where
(H,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
is one of
(\PSL_{2}(25),\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
,
(\PSL_{2}(27),\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
,
(\PSL_{2}(37),\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
,
(\PSL_{2}(29),\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
.

Recall that we construct
H
by finding a triple of generators
(u,s,t)
satisfying the presentations described in
Section

2.7
.

4.1
General strategy

The general strategy we will use is divided in several steps as follows:

(i)

Find a copy of a Borel subgroup
B=\langle u,s\rangle
of
H
lying in
\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{G}(k)\leq\GL_{d}(k)
, where
k
is a suitable finite field and
d
is the degree of the adjoint representation of
\mathrm{G}
.

(ii)

Find all possible
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
such that
(u,s,t)
are the generators of the presentation of
H
described in
Section

2.7
; in particular
\langle u,s,t\rangle
is isomorphic to
H
. Furthermore, show that
t\in\mathrm{G}
.

(iii)

Check whether the different copies of
H
containing the same
B
are
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugate}
to each other or not.

(iv)

Show that
B
is unique up to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
, and deduce the number of embeddings of
B
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

(v)

Generalise the result of (iv) to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
in characteristic zero or coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, using
Theorem

2.9.11
.

(vi)

Find
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
.

(vii)

For any finite field
k_{1}
of characteristic coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, find whether
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(k_{1})
, and count the conjugacy classes of
H<\mathrm{G}(k_{1})
in such cases.

(viii)

If
H<\mathrm{G}(k_{1})
, deduce whether
N_{\mathrm{G}(k_{1})}(H)
is a maximal subgroup of
\mathrm{G}(k_{1})
, and what happens in the case of an almost simple group
\overline{\mathrm{G}}(k_{1})
with socle
\mathrm{G}(k_{1})
.

(ix)

Check whether we say anything when the characteristic of
k
divides
\lvert H\rvert
.

4.2
Embedding of
\PSL_{2}(25)
in
\mathrm{F}_{4}

In this section, we consider an embedding of a group
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(25)
into
\mathrm{F}_{4}
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,5
.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

If
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
contains a subgroup
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(25)
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,5
, then
H
is Lie primitive, acts irreducibly on the minimal module
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
, and acts as
26_{1}\oplus 26_{2}
on the adjoint module
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
.

Furthermore, if
\chi
is the character of an embedding of
H
into
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
in the adjoint representation, then
\chi
is the character in
Table

4.1
, where classes with the same character value have been merged for brevity.

Proof.

The fact that
H
may only embed primitively in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
is proved in
[
44
, Corollary 3]
, and the action on the minimal and adjoint module is described in
[
44
, Table 5.3.24]
.

The character values can be deduced using the character table of
\PSL_{2}(25)

[
21
]
together with Litterick’s program in
[
44
]
, which allows to compute the feasible characters of the embedding. A computed list of traces of semisimple elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
is available in
[
24
]
.

In particular, semisimple elements of order 2 can only have trace
-4
or
20
on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
, and semisimple elements of order 3 can only have trace
-2
or
7
. We then have that the only possible choice is for
\chi
to be the sum of the two characters of degree 26 that have value
-2
on involutions, and
-1
on elements of order 3.
∎

Order

1

2

3

4

5

6

12

13

\chi

52

-4

-2

0

2

2

0

0

Table 4.1:
The possible characters
\chi
of
\PSL_{2}(25)
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
in adjoint representation.

Theorem 4.2.2
.

Let
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(25)
be a subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
in characteristic
p
, and let
\sigma
be a Steinberg endomorphism of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
with
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
. Suppose
p\neq 2,3,5
, and that
H
acts irreducibly on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
and as
26_{1}\oplus 26_{2}
on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
. Then:

(i)

There is a unique
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
, which gives rise to two conjugacy classes of embeddings,
H
is Lie primitive, and
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H.2
, where the extension is given by the diagonal-field automorphism.

(ii)

H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
for any field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
q
a power of
p
, and is unique up to conjugacy.

(iii)

If
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
is an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
, then
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
is a maximal subgroup of
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
iff
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
and
q=p
, in which case
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H.2
.

The proof will be divided in several steps.

Presentation
.

Consider the following matrices, generating
\SL_{2}(25)
:

u\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad s\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{-1}&0\\
0&\omega\end{pmatrix},\qquad t\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},

where
\omega
is a primitive element of
\mathbb{F}_{25}^{\times}
with minimal polynomial
X^{2}+4X+2
over
\mathbb{F}_{5}
. We are going to consider a presentation for
\PSL_{2}(25)
whose generators are the images of
u,s,t
when taking the quotient by the group of scalar matrices, which we denote with the same notation, and whose relations are given by (
2.12
). We will also use (
2.13
), which stems from the fact that
X^{2}+3X+4
is the minimal polynomial of
\omega^{2}
over
\mathbb{F}_{5}
.

Furthermore,
B=\langle u,s\rangle\simeq 5^{2}\rtimes 12
is a Borel subgroup of
H
, and its derived subgroup is the socle
B^{\prime}=\langle u,uu^{s}\rangle\simeq 5^{2}
.

Construction 4.2.3
.

Construction of
u,s
in
\mathrm{F}_{4}(k)
for some finite field
k
.

Note that we are going to look for
u
in a maximal torus, and while not required to construct
B
, for further computations later on we also need
s
to lie in a different one. Therefore, we consider the field
k
of order
61=12\times 5+1
which has 5th and 12th roots of unity, and
u
and
s
lie in (distinct) tori of
\mathrm{G}\coloneqq\mathrm{F}_{4}(k)
.

By
Lemma

2.8.2
, a
5^{2}
subgroup is always toral, so we can look for
u
in a maximal torus
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathrm{G}
; furthermore, we can look for
s
in
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
because the
5^{2}
subgroup containing
u
is regular (this is discussed in the proof of
Lemma

4.2.9
).

We proceed as described in
Section

3.3
. We can compute
\widetilde{W}=\langle n_{i}:i=1,\ldots,4\rangle
, and verify that
\widetilde{W}=T_{2,1}.W
, where
T_{2,1}\coloneqq\langle h_{i}(-1):i=1,\ldots,4\rangle\leq\mathrm{T}
is an elementary abelian group of order
2^{4}
. Then we can take random elements of
\widetilde{W}
until we find an element
s
of order 12 such that its image under the quotient map
\widetilde{W}\rightarrow\widetilde{W}/T_{2,1}
has order 12.

We can check with
Magma
that the Weyl group of
\mathrm{F}_{4}
has only one conjugacy class of elements of order 12, so
s
must lie in the correct class. Likewise, we can compute
C_{\mathrm{T}}(s)=1
, meaning that there is a unique conjugacy class of elements in
\langle\mathrm{T},s\rangle
that we need to investigate.

Let
\zeta
be a primitive element of
k^{\times}
, so that
h_{i}\coloneqq h(\zeta^{\delta_{ij}})_{j=1}^{4}\in\GL_{52}(k)
with
1\leq i\leq 4
are generators of
\mathrm{T}
constructed according to
Section

3.1
, and
h^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq h_{i}^{12}
are generators of the Sylow
5\text{-subgroup}
of
\mathrm{T}
. By computing
{h^{\prime}_{i}}^{s}=\prod_{j=1}^{4}{h^{\prime}_{j}}^{a_{ij}}
we obtain a matrix
A=(a_{ij})
with coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{5}
describing the action of
s
, in particular
A
is decomposable into two irreducible
2\times 2
blocks, one of which has characteristic polynomial
X^{2}+3X+4
. If we take
z=(z_{i})_{i=1}^{4}
to be any non-zero vector of
k^{4}
acted on by said block, we can define
u\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{4}{h^{\prime}_{i}}^{z_{i}}\in\GL_{52}(k)
.

By construction,
u
and
s
satisfy the relations

u^{5}=s^{12}=u^{4}s^{-1}u^{3}s^{-1}us^{2}=1.

Construction 4.2.4
.

Construction of
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

We can compute that the fixed point space of
s
acting on
L(\mathrm{G})
is
4\text{-dimensional}
, hence it is a Cartan subalgebra of
L(\mathrm{G})
by
Corollary

2.6.5
.

By writing the corresponding Chevalley basis (
Section

3.4.1
), we can diagonalise
s
and find an involution
e\in\mathrm{G}
such that
s^{e}=s^{-1}
as described in
Section

3.4.2
.

Since
s
is semisimple in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
as well, and fixes no root space in
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
(
s
has a 4-dimensional eigenspace), it is a regular element, and by
Proposition

2.9.2
and
Corollary

2.6.3
we have that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
is a maximal torus
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
; therefore,
C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)
is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\cap\mathrm{G}=T
, the maximal torus corresponding to the Cartan subalgebra fixed by
s
.

Denote by
M(s)
(resp.
\boldsymbol{M}(s)
) the linear subspace of
M_{52}(k)
(resp.
M_{52}(\overline{k})
) spanned by elements of
C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)
(resp.
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
). As observed in
Lemma

3.4.4
, both
C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)
and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
are tori, so it is clear that both
M(s)
and
\boldsymbol{M}(s)
have dimension 49, given that their action on
L(\mathrm{G})
(resp.
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
) decomposes as the sum of 48 one-dimensional root spaces and the Cartan subalgebra (4 copies of the trivial module). Therefore, a basis
\Set{c_{i}}_{i=1}^{49}
of
M(s)
is also a basis of
\boldsymbol{M}(s)
.

Assume that
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
is such that
u,s,t
satisfy the relations in (
2.12
), then
t=ce
where
e
is the involution constructed earlier, and
c\in C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
.

By
Proposition

3.4.6
, the relation
(tu)^{3}=1
is satisfied iff
c=\sum_{i=0}^{49}a_{i}c_{i}
, where
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{49})\in{(\overline{k})^{49}}
is a solution of the system of equations given by

w_{j}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{49}X_{i}(euc_{i}eu-u^{-1}c_{i}e)=0,

(4.1)

where
w_{1},\ldots,w_{4}
form a basis of
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})^{C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)}
, i.e. a basis of the Cartan subalgebra fixed by
s
.

By solving the system of
4\times 52=208
equations in
49
variables, we obtain a one-dimensional
\overline{k}\text{-space}
of solutions
M_{0}=\langle m_{0}\rangle
.

Therefore, we established that
t\in(M_{0}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})e
.

Since
M_{0}
is one-dimensional, there can only be one element in
M_{0}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
: if there were two, say
am_{0}
and
bm_{0}
,
a\neq b\in\overline{k}
, then
(am_{0})(bm_{0})^{-1}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, i.e. the scalar matrix
ab^{-1}\mathbb{1}_{52}
would lie in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, giving
a=b
.

It is then straightforward to compute the unique
r\in\overline{k}
such that
c^{*}\coloneqq rm_{0}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, using the membership test described in
Section

3.2
. In particular, we obtain that
r\in k
hence
c^{*}\in\mathrm{G}
.

Lemma 4.2.5
.

If
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
is such that
(u,s,t)
satisfy (
2.12
), then
t=c^{*}e
, where
c^{*}
and
e
are computed elements of
\mathrm{G}
. In particular,
t\in\mathrm{G}
.

Proof.

This follows from computer calculations, doing
4.2.3
and
4.2.4
. The relations (
2.12
)((ii)-(iv),(viii)) were not used during the construction of
u,s,t
, so we verify them using the computed matrices
u,s,t
.
∎

Now we have a triple
(u,s,t)
of elements in
\mathrm{G}
that satisfy the presentation (
2.12
) of
H
.

We will first discuss
\aut H
, then count conjugacy classes of groups in
\mathrm{G}(k)
isomorphic to
H
and their embeddings in a finite or algebraic group.

Construction 4.2.6
.

Construction of
H.2<\mathrm{G}
.

We can construct the field-diagonal extension directly from the group obtained with
4.2.4
.

Let
\tau
be a representative of the class of non-trivial outer automorphisms of
H
; since
B
is a Borel subgroup of
H
, all subgroups of
H
isomorphic to
B
are
H\text{-conjugate}
, hence
B=\tau(B)^{g}
for some
g\in H
, therefore we may assume
B
is fixed by
\tau
.

Since
B^{\prime}=\langle u,uu^{s}\rangle
is a
5^{2}
group, which is supersoluble, we have that
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)\leq N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
by
Lemma

3.3.4
. Therefore, we can construct the matrix group
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})=\langle h_{i}(\lambda),n_{i}:i\in 1,\ldots,4\rangle
, with
\lambda
primitive element of
k^{\times}
, as described in
Section

3.1
, and let
Magma
compute
N_{N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})}(B)
directly, which outputs a
B.2
group, and any element of said group that does not lie in
B
can be taken to generate
H.2
together with
H
.

Proposition 4.2.7
.

Neither
\PGL_{2}(25)
nor
\PsL_{2}(25)
embeds in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, while
\PSL_{2}(25).2
does, where the extension is given by the diagonal-field automorphism.

Proof.

The fact that the first two groups do not embed follows for example from the argument used in
Lemma

4.2.1
. From the character table of
\PSL_{2}(25)
in
[
21
]
we see that both
\PGL_{2}(25)
and
\PsL_{2}(25)
have (at least) one additional class of involutions with trace 0 in the adjoint representation, which is not admissible as the only possibilities are
-4
and
20
.

The fact that the remaining extension exists follows directly from
4.2.6
, as we constructed a copy of it in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
in characteristic coprime to
\lvert\PSL_{2}(25).2\rvert
, and we can lift this embedding to any field of characteristic 0 or coprime to
\lvert\PSL_{2}(25).2\rvert
using
Theorem

2.9.11
.
∎

Remark
.

Observe that in
[
18
, Section 6.6]
, the fact that
H.2<\mathrm{F}_{4}(\mathbb{C})
is seemingly erroneously deduced from the existence of the embedding
H.2<\mathrm{F}_{4}(2)
. However,
\lvert H\rvert
is even so an embedding in characteristic 2 does not imply an embedding in characteristic zero in general.

Lemma 4.2.8
.

The ordered pairs
(u,s)
and
(uu^{s},s)
are not
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
.

Proof.

Recall that
B\simeq U\rtimes\langle s\rangle
, where
U
is a
5^{2}
subgroup containing
s
.
s
acts on
U
with two orbits of size
12
, and
u,uu^{s}
are a representative of each orbit. One could also verify that
u=(uu^{s})^{2}s(uu^{s})^{-2}s^{-1}
, implying that
u\in\langle uu^{s},s\rangle
and
\langle u,s\rangle=\langle uu^{s},s\rangle
.

u
and
uu^{s}
are representatives of the classes of elements of order 5 in
H
,
uu^{s}
being the image of
u
under the action of an outer automorphism of
H
. If there was an element of
g\in C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
that conjugates them, then
g
would lie in some extension of
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. By
Proposition

4.2.7
, the only possibility for
g
is to lie in the field-diagonal extension of
H
; however, we can verify with
Magma
that no element of
\PSL_{2}(25).2
swaps the two classes of elements of order 5 while centralising
s
.
∎

Lemma 4.2.9
.

Let
\overline{u}
and
\overline{s}
be elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
that satisfy the relations

\overline{u}^{5}=\overline{s}^{12}=[\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\overline{s}}]=\overline{u}^{4}\overline{s}^{-1}\overline{u}^{3}\overline{s}^{-1}\overline{u}\overline{s}^{2}=1.

(4.2)

Furthermore, suppose that all elements of
\langle\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\overline{s}}\rangle
of order 5 have trace 2 in the adjoint representation.

Then the ordered pair
(\overline{u},\overline{s})
is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
to either
(u,s)
or
(uu^{s},s)
. In particular,
B
is unique up to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
.

Proof.

We can verify with
Magma
that if
\overline{u},\overline{s}
satisfy (
4.2
), then
\overline{B}\coloneqq\langle\overline{u},\overline{s}\rangle\simeq\langle u,s\rangle
.

Since
\overline{U}\coloneqq\langle\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\overline{s}}\rangle\simeq 5^{2}
, by
Lemma

2.8.2
we have that
\overline{U}
lies in a maximal torus of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. Furthermore, the condition on the traces shows that
\overline{U}
fixes a 4-dimensional subspace of
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
.

This can be seen by taking the representation
\rho
of
\overline{U}
induced by the adjoint representation of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, then by
[
26
, Proposition 2.8]
we have that
\frac{1}{\lvert U\rvert}\sum_{x\in\overline{U}}\rho(x)
is a projection from
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
to
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})^{\overline{U}}
, hence by
[
26
, (2.9)]
we can compute:

\dim L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})^{\overline{U}}=\frac{1}{\lvert\overline{U}\rvert}\sum_{x\in\overline{U}}\tr\rho(x)=\frac{52+2\cdot 24}{25}=4.

Therefore
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})^{\overline{U}}
must be the Cartan subalgebra corresponding to
\overline{u}
(and to
\overline{u}^{\overline{s}}
) by
Corollary

2.6.5
.

In particular,
\overline{U}
is regular and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\overline{U})^{\circ}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
for some maximal torus
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, which we may assume it is a maximal torus containing
U
; therefore,
\overline{B}\leq N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
(see proof of
Proposition

2.9.4
).

Recall from
4.2.3
that there is a unique conjugacy class in
W(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
of elements of order 12, and the action of such an element
w
on a maximal torus has no fixed points, so by
Lemma

3.3.2
we have that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(w)
is finite. This means that
s
is unique up to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\text{-conjugacy}
, being a preimage in
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
of
w
.

Thus we can replace
(\overline{u},\overline{s})
by a conjugate pair such that
\overline{s}=s
and
\overline{u}
lies in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
.

We can compute that the action of
w
hence of
s
on
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}_{5,1}\simeq 5^{4}
has two blocks, with characteristic polynomial
X^{2}+2X+4
and
X^{2}+3X+4
, respectively, like in
4.2.3
. This means that
s
acts on
5^{4}
normalising a
5^{2}\times 5^{2}
decomposition; the condition
\overline{u}^{4}\overline{s}^{-1}\overline{u}^{3}\overline{s}^{-1}\overline{u}\overline{s}^{2}=1
means that
\overline{u}
lies in the same
5^{2}
as
u
, i.e.
\overline{u}\in\langle u,u^{s}\rangle
.

The result then follows by
Lemma

4.2.8
.
∎

Lemma 4.2.10
.

Suppose that
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(25)
is a subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and let
u,s
be the elements obtained from
4.2.3
. Then there are elements
g,\overline{t}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
such that
H^{g}
is generated by a triple
(u,s,\overline{t})
that satisfies presentation (
2.12
) for
H
.

Proof.

H
is generated by a triple
(u_{0},s_{0},t_{0})
that satisfies presentation (
2.12
). Let
\alpha
be an outer automorphism of
H
that fixes
s_{0}
and maps
u_{0}
to
u_{0}^{\phantom{\alpha}}u_{0}^{s_{0}}
, then
H
is generated by
(u_{0}^{\alpha},s_{0}^{\alpha},t_{0}^{\alpha})=(u_{0}^{\phantom{\alpha}}u_{0}^{s_{0}},s_{0}^{\phantom{\alpha}},t_{0}^{\alpha})
, and this triple satisfies presentation (
2.12
) as well.

By
Lemma

4.2.9
, one of
(u_{0},s_{0})
and
(u_{0}^{\alpha},s_{0}^{\phantom{\alpha}})
is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
to
(u,s)
, hence we may find
g\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
such that
H^{g}
is generated by a triple
(u,s,\overline{t})
that satisfies the presentation, where
\overline{t}
is either
t_{0}^{\alpha g}
or
t_{0}^{g}
.
∎

We can now deduce part (i) of
Theorem

4.2.2
when
p
is 0 or coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
.

By Lemmas
4.2.10
and
4.2.5
there is a unique conjugacy class of subgroups isomorphic to
\PSL_{2}(25)
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and by Lemma
4.2.9
we have that for each copy of
5^{2}\rtimes 12
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
we obtain two embeddings (depending on whether a chosen element of order 5 is mapped to
u
or to
uu^{s}
), thus we have two conjugacy classes of embeddings of
B
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. Since the characteristic of
k
does not divide
\lvert H\rvert
, we can use
Theorem

2.9.11
to generalise this result to characteristic 0 and any other characteristic coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, namely
p\neq 2,3,5,13
. Lie primitivity is proved in
[
44
, Corollary 3]
.

In order to complete part (ii), we need to find whether
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(q)
, and show that if it does then
H
is unique up to conjugacy; we proceed as discussed in
Section

2.9
.

Lemma 4.2.11
.

If
\mathrm{G}
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
B
, than it is unique up to
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
.

If
\mathrm{G}
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
H
, than it is unique up to
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
.

Proof.

Recall that there is a unique conjugacy class of elements of order 12 in
W(\mathrm{G})
.

If we show that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)=1
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\cap\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{T}
, then the result follows by
Lemma

2.9.5
.

We already computed the action of
s
on
\mathrm{T}
in
4.2.3
, and since it has no fixed points then
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
is finite by
Lemma

3.3.2
. Observe that we could also compute the image of
s
in
W(\mathrm{G})
and verify that its imagine under the reflection representation has no eigenvectors, in particular none of eigenvalue 1.

We can then use
Lemma

3.3.3
to verify that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)=1
. Observe that
\mathrm{T}
is small enough that we can compute
C_{\mathrm{T}}(s)
directly.

Recall from the proof of
Lemma

4.2.9
that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\langle u,u^{s}\rangle)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
, then

C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B)=C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\langle u,u^{s}\rangle)\cap C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\cap C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)=C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)=1.

Then, by
Corollary

2.9.9
we have that the unique
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
does not split in
\mathrm{G}(q)
meaning that for any field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(q)
there is exactly one
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
in
\mathrm{G}(q)
.
∎

Proposition 4.2.12
.

H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(q)
in characteristic not dividing
\lvert H\rvert
.

Proof.

We need to understand what is the action of outer automorphisms of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
on
H
; since we are in odd characteristic, we need to consider only the field automorphism of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

Take the Frobenius morphism
F_{p}
; since by
Lemma

4.2.5
there is only one conjugacy class of
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
then there is no choice for
F_{p}
but to permute the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugates}
of
H
. We then assume that
H
is normalised by
F_{p}
and we have to check whether
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(p)
.

By
[
48
, Theorem 25.14]
,
\mathrm{G}(p)
contains Sylow
5\text{-subgroups}
that are homocyclic with 4 cyclic factors of order
\lvert p\mp 1\rvert_{5}
if
p\equiv\pm 1\bmod 5
, or 2 cyclic factors of order
\lvert p^{2}+1\rvert_{5}
if
p\equiv\pm 2\bmod 5
. Here, we denote by
\lvert n\rvert_{5}
the
5\text{-part}
of the integer
n
.

From
[
12
, p. 57 and Table 3]
we have that a Sylow
5\text{-subgroup}
of
\mathrm{G}(p)
is normalised by the Weyl group of
\mathrm{G}
if
p\equiv\pm 1\bmod 5
, and by the complex reflection group
G_{8}
if
p\equiv\pm 2\bmod 5
. As
G_{8}
contains (a unique conjugacy class of) elements of order 12, in either case we have that
\mathrm{G}
contains a
5^{2}\rtimes 12
subgroup.

Therefore
B
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(p)
, and since there is only one conjugacy class of
H
containing a given
B
, we can conclude that
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(p)
for any
p\neq 2,3,5,13
.
∎

Remark
.

H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(p)
for any
p
(see for example
[
23
]
); we kept the restriction on the characteristic as those are the only cases we can verify with the technique we are using.

Proposition 4.2.13
.

H.2
is a maximal subgroup of
\mathrm{G}(q)
,
q
a power of
p
coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, iff
q=p
.

Proof.

Since
H
is primitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and
N_{\mathrm{G}}(H)=H.2
, as observed in
Lemma

2.10.5
we only need to verify that
H.2
does not lie in any other candidate maximal subgroup.

By
Proposition

4.2.12
we must have
q=p
, otherwise
H.2<\mathrm{G}(p)<\mathrm{G}(q)
. Since
\mathrm{G}
is
\mathrm{F}_{4}
in odd characteristic, this is the only possibility for
H.2
to lie in a group of the same type as
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

Furthermore,
H.2
cannot lie in an almost simple subgroup
M<\mathrm{G}
whose socle
K
is a primitive simple subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
not isomorphic to
H
. The candidates for
K
are the groups in
[
44
, Table 1.2-1.3]
; for
p\neq 2,3,5
they are:
\PSL_{2}(r)
for
r=7,8,9,13,17,27
,
\PSL_{3}(3)
,
\PSU_{3}(3)
, and
\prescript{3}{}{\mathrm{D}}_{4}(2)
. We can find their order using the Atlas
[
21
]
or
Magma
, and it is straightforward to verify that
H.2
cannot be a subgroup of
M
.
∎

Theorem

4.2.2
(iii) then follows from
Proposition

4.2.13
in characteristic coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, since we are in odd characteristic so
\mathrm{G}(p)
has a trivial outer automorphism group hence
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
.

Remark
.

In
[
23
]
it shown that this is actually true for any
p\neq 2
; for
p=2
, we have that
H.2
is contained in
\!\prescript{2}{}{\mathrm{F}}_{4}(2)
.

Finally, observe that using
Theorem

2.9.11
we could generalise the results we obtained to any characteristic coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, or zero, that is
p\neq 2,3,5,13
; however,
4.2.4
only requires
p\neq 2,3,5
. Indeed, we have the following:

Proposition 4.2.14
.

In characteristic 13, there is a unique
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
,
H
is Lie primitive, and
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H.2
.
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(k)
for any field
k
of characteristic 13, and is unique up to conjugacy; furthermore,
H.2
is maximal in
\mathrm{G}(13)
.

Proof.

Lie primitivity is proved in
[
44
, Corollary 3]
.

Since the only step that needed the characteristic not to divide
\lvert H\rvert
is the use of the lifting lemma, we only need to prove that
H<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

We can replicate
4.2.4
using a field of order
13^{4}
instead: all the computations can be performed in a similar fashion, leading to
Lemma

4.2.5
but in characteristic 13. Furthermore,
Lemma

4.2.9
,
Lemma

4.2.10
, and the arguments used to prove
Proposition

4.2.12
and
Proposition

4.2.13
still hold.
∎

4.3
Embedding of
\PSL_{2}(27)
in
\mathrm{F}_{4}

In this section, we consider the embedding of a group
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(27)
into
\mathrm{F}_{4}
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,7
.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

If
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
contains a subgroup
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(27)
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,7
, then
H
is Lie primitive, acts irreducibly on the minimal module
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
, and as
26_{1}\oplus 26_{2}
on the adjoint module
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
: there are three such representations, not isomorphic to each other but
\aut H\text{-conjugate}
.

Furthermore, if
\chi
is the character of an embedding of
H
into
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
in the adjoint representation, then
\chi
is one of the characters in
Table

4.2
, where classes with the same character value have been merged for brevity. Here,
z_{1}=1+y_{7}^{3}+y_{7}^{4}
,
z_{2}=1+y_{7}+y_{7}^{6}
,
z_{3}=1+y_{7}^{2}+y_{7}^{5}
, where
y_{7}
is a primitive 7th root of unity; the minimal polynomial of
z_{i}
is
X^{3}-X^{2}-2X+1
. The conjugacy classes of elements of order 7 square between each other in alphabetic order, and a 14-class squares into the 7-class denoted by the same letter.

Proof.

The fact that
H
may only embed primitively in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
is proved in
[
44
, Corollary 3]
, and the action on the minimal and adjoint module is described in
[
44
, Table 5.3.27]
.

The character values can be deduced using the character table of
\PSL_{2}(27)

[
21
]
together with Litterick’s program in
[
44
]
, which allows to compute the feasible characters of the embedding. A computed list of traces of semisimple elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
is available in
[
24
]
.

In particular, semisimple elements of order 2 can only have trace
-4
or
20
on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
, and semisimple elements of order 3 can only have trace
-2
or
7
. We then have that the only possible choice is for
\chi_{i}
to be the sum of two out of the three characters of degree 26 that have value
-2
on involutions, and
-1
on elements of order 3.
∎

7_{b}

7_{c}

\chi_{1}

52

52

Table 4.2:
The possible characters
\chi_{i}
of
\PSL_{2}(27)
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
in adjoint representation.

Theorem 4.3.2
.

Let
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(27)
be a subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
in characteristic
p
, and let
\sigma
be a Steinberg endomorphism of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
with
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
. Suppose
p\neq 2,3,7
, and that
H
acts irreducibly on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
and as
26_{1}\oplus 26_{2}
on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
. Then:

(i)

There is a unique
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
, which gives rise to two
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
classes of embeddings,
H
is Lie primitive, and
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
.

(ii)

H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
,
q
a power of
p
, iff
f(X)=X^{3}-X^{2}-2X+1
splits over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. In such cases,
H
is unique up to
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
.

(iii)

If
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
is an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{G}
, then
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
is maximal iff
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is the minimal splitting field for
f
, in which case either
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
and
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
, or
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}.3
and
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H.3
.

The proof will be divided in several steps.

Presentation
.

Consider the following matrices, generating
\SL_{2}(27)
:

u\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad s\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{-1}&0\\
0&\omega\end{pmatrix},\qquad t\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},

where
\omega
is a primitive element of
\mathbb{F}_{27}^{\times}
with minimal polynomial
X^{3}+2X+1
over
\mathbb{F}_{3}
.

We are going to consider a presentation for
\PSL_{2}(27)
whose generators are the images of
u,s,t
when taking the quotient by the group of scalar matrices, which we will denote with the same notation, and whose relations are given by (
2.14
). Observe that (
2.14
)(iii) stems from the fact that
X^{3}+X^{2}+X+2
is the minimal polynomial of
\omega^{2}
over
\mathbb{F}_{3}
.

Furthermore,
B=\langle u,s\rangle\simeq 3^{3}\rtimes 13
is a Borel subgroup of
H
, which acts as
13\oplus 13^{*}
on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
and as
13^{2}\oplus{13^{*}}^{2}
on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
and its derived subgroup is the socle
B^{\prime}=\langle u,u^{s},u^{s^{2}}\rangle\simeq 3^{3}
.

Construction 4.3.3
.

Construction of
u,s
in
\mathrm{F}_{4}(k)
for some finite field
k
.

Looking at the character values of our specific representation of
H
, by
Lemma

2.9.10
we need
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
with
q\equiv\pm 1\bmod 7
. Since we want
u
to lie in a maximal torus, we will take
\ch k\neq 3
.

While
B
can be constructed in any such characteristic, for the follow-up we will need
s
to lie in a maximal torus as well, so we take
\ch k\equiv 1\bmod 13
. We will deal with the case of
\ch k=13
separately later on, as that makes
s
unipotent.

Under such premises, the smallest field
k
we can consider has order
547=13\times 7\times 6+1
; define
\mathrm{G}\coloneqq\mathrm{F}_{4}(k)
.

By
Lemma

2.8.2
, a
3^{3}
subgroup containing
u
cannot be toral, and its normaliser
N
in
\mathrm{G}
is
3^{3}\rtimes\SL_{3}(3)
, which is unique up to
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
, so we can construct it and then find suitable
u,s
in there.

The first step is constructing
3^{3}\simeq\langle u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\rangle
: while it is non-toral, a
3^{2}
subgroup like
\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle
is (see
[
28
, Theorem 7.4]
), with
u_{3}
centralising it. Furthermore, it is known that there are three conjugacy classes of elements of order 3 in
\mathrm{G}
, with centraliser
3\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}
,
\mathrm{T}_{1}\mathrm{B}_{3}
, and
\mathrm{T}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{3}
, the first being the only one whose elements have trace
-2
in the adjoint representation (see for example
[
25
, Table 4.4]
). Therefore, we can take a maximal torus
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathrm{G}
, and by
Lemma

2.8.1
we can choose
u_{3}
to be an element of order 3 in
\widetilde{W}=\langle n_{i}:i=1,\ldots,4\rangle
such that
\tr u_{3}=-2
(by
Lemma

4.3.1
) and
u_{3}
maps to an element of order 3 of
W(\mathrm{F}_{4})
, as described in
Section

3.3
.

Let
\zeta
be a primitive element of
k^{\times}
, so that
h_{i}\coloneqq h(\zeta^{\delta_{ij}})_{j=1}^{4}\in\GL_{52}(k)
with
1\leq i\leq 4
are generators of
\mathrm{T}
, and
h^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq h_{i}^{182}
are generators of the Sylow
3\text{-subgroup}
of
\mathrm{T}
. By computing
{h^{\prime}_{i}}^{u_{3}}=\prod_{j=1}^{4}{h^{\prime}_{j}}^{a_{ij}}
we obtain a matrix
A=(a_{ij})
with coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{3}
describing the action of
u_{3}
on the Sylow
3\text{-subgroup}
of
\mathrm{T}
.

In order to find
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathrm{T}
centralised by
u_{3}
, we can look at the kernel of
A-\mathbb{1}_{4}
: we compute that it has dimension 2, so let
z_{i}=(z_{ij})_{j=1}^{4}
for
i=1,2
be a basis for it. We define
u_{i}\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{4}{h^{\prime}_{j}}^{z_{ij}}\in\GL_{52}(k)
, for
i=1,2
. By construction,
u_{3}
centralises both
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
, and they lie in the torus
\mathrm{T}
so they commute with each other; furthermore, the way we chose
z_{1},z_{2}
guarantees that
u_{2}\neq u_{1}^{2}
, so
E\coloneqq\langle u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\rangle\simeq 3^{3}
.

It is not obvious how to look for the
\SL_{3}(3)
normalising
E
. We start by constructing
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})=\langle h_{r}(\lambda),n_{r}:r\in\Pi\rangle
, for some
\lambda
primitive element of
k
, as described in
Section

2.4.4
, and we let
Magma
compute
N_{1}\coloneqq N_{N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})}(E)
, which has order
11664=3^{3}\times 2^{4}\times 3^{3}
.

As the order suggests, it can be checked that
N_{1}\simeq E\rtimes P
, where
P
is isomorphic to a maximal parabolic subgroup of
\SL_{3}(3)
, that is
3^{2}.\GL_{2}(3)
. In particular, the flag stabilised by
P
is
1<\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle<E
.

Therefore, we can consider a different flag to obtain another maximal parabolic and generate
N
. To do so, we can find a torus
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
containing
u_{3}
and repeat the above construction to obtain
N_{2}\coloneqq N_{N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T}^{\prime})}(E)\simeq N_{1}
. For instance, we can find a Chevalley basis corresponding to
u_{3}
as in
Section

3.4.1
in order to construct
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
.

We can check that
N=\langle N_{1},N_{2}\rangle\simeq 3^{3}\rtimes\SL_{3}(3)
by computing the composition factors: this is enough as
3^{3}.\SL_{3}(3)
is always a split extension, as we can check with
Magma
that
\dim H^{2}(\PSL_{3}(3),M)=0
, where
M
is any irreducible
\mathbb{F}_{3}\PSL_{3}(3)\text{-module}
.

The group
N
is not very large, so with a random search it is easy to find
s\in N
of order 13 and
1\neq u\in E\subset N
such that
s^{3}u^{2}s^{-1}us^{-1}us^{-1}u=1
.

Construction 4.3.4
.

Construction of
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

We can compute that the fixed point space of
s
acting on
L(\mathrm{G})
is 4-dimensional, hence it is a Cartan subalgebra of
L(G)
by
Corollary

2.6.5
and we can apply
Lemma

3.4.4
.

We can follow
4.2.4
verbatim up to solving the system of equations (
4.1
) obtained by using the relation
(ceu)^{3}=1
. We obtain again a system of
4\times 52=208
equations in
49
variables, but this time we compute a
\overline{k}\text{-space}
of solutions
M_{0}=\langle m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}\rangle
that is
3\text{-dimensional}
, so we look for additional linear conditions to use.

We can check with
Magma
that
tus^{2}
,
tus^{5}
,
tus^{6}
represent an element of
H
of order 7, a conjugate of its square, and a conjugate of its fourth power, respectively. Looking at
Table

4.2
, their traces are some cyclic rearrangement of
z_{1}=1+y^{3}+y^{4}
,
z_{2}=1+y+y^{6}
, and
z_{3}=1+y^{2}+y^{5}
, where
y
is a primitive 7th root of unity in
k
.

Since
c=a_{1}m_{1}+a_{2}m_{2}+a_{3}m_{3}
for
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in\overline{k}
, we have that

\tr(tus^{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\tr(m_{i}eus^{j}),

so let
S
be a matrix where the row
i
has elements
\tr(m_{i}eus^{j})
,
j=2,5,6
, let
C
be a matrix over
k
whose rows are the cyclic permutations of
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
, and let
A
be a matrix of indeterminates whose three rows are all
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
. Then the condition on the traces of elements of order 7 given by
Table

4.2
translates to

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle=\tr(tus^{2})=\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\tr(m_{i}eus^{2}),

\displaystyle z_{2}

\displaystyle=\tr(tus^{5})=\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\tr(m_{i}eus^{5}),

\displaystyle z_{3}

\displaystyle=\tr(tus^{6})=\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\tr(m_{i}eus^{6}),

and the same triple of relations but permuting
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
cyclically.

These three sets of three relations can be written as
AS=C
, so each row of
A=CS^{-1}
contains a triple
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
that defines the coefficients of
c
, thus resulting in a set
U_{0}
of three possible values of
c
.

As all the elements of
U_{0}
are matrices with coefficients in
k
, we then use the membership test in
Section

3.2
to check that all three choices of
c\in U_{0}
lie in
\mathrm{G}
.

Lemma 4.3.5
.

If
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
is such that
(u,s,t)
satisfy (
2.14
), then
t\in(U_{0}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})e
, where
e
is a computed element of
\mathrm{G}
and
U_{0}
is a computed set of three elements of
\mathrm{G}
. In particular,
t\in\mathrm{G}
.

Proof.

This follows from computer calculations, doing
4.3.3
and
4.3.4
. The relations (
2.14
)((ii),(vii)) were not used during the construction of
u,s,t
, so we verify them using the computed matrices
u,s,t
.
∎

We can verify with
Magma
that the three choices of
t
give rise to three distinct groups
H_{1},H_{2},H_{3}
isomorphic to
\PSL_{2}(27)
containing
B
, so the question is whether they are conjugate.

We now discuss
\aut H
, and show that the
H_{i}\text{'s}
are
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugate}
to each other.

Proposition 4.3.6
.

Neither
\PGL_{2}(27)
nor
\PsL_{2}(27)
embeds in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, hence
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
.

Proof.

This follows for example from the argument used in
Lemma

4.3.1
and the character table of
\PSL_{2}(27)

[
21
]
. Observe that:

(i)

\PGL_{2}(27)\simeq\PSL_{2}(27).2
has a class of involutions with trace 0 on both the minimal and the adjoint module, which are not admissible.

(ii)

\PsL_{2}(27)\simeq\PSL_{2}(27).3
has a class of elements of order 3 with trace 0 on both the minimal and the adjoint module, which are not admissible.∎

Proposition 4.3.7
.

The groups
H_{1},H_{2},H_{3}
obtained with
4.3.4
are all
\mathrm{G}
-conjugate to one other.

Proof.

Since they all intersect in
B
, and
N_{\mathrm{G}}(H)=H
by
Proposition

4.3.6
, we can look at
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)/N_{H}(B)
, as any element in
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)/N_{H}(B)
would not lie in
H
and therefore would permute the three
H_{i}
.

Being a Borel subgroup of
H
,
N_{H}(B)=B
by
Corollary

2.2.3
. Observe that

N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)\leq N_{\mathrm{G}}(B^{\prime})=N\simeq 3^{3}\rtimes\SL_{3}(3),

where
N
is the group obtained in
4.3.3
. Therefore, we can use
Magma
to compute
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)=N_{N}(B)=B.3
, and we obtain
\lvert N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)/N_{H}(B)\rvert=3
. A preimage of a generator of the quotient therefore normalises
B
and acts on the three copies of
H
without stabilising them; the only option is for it to conjugate them cyclically.
∎

Note that such an element of order 3 can be obtained easily with
Magma
by constructing the normaliser and quotient described in the proof, and it is straightforward to check that it does indeed conjugate the copies of
H
containing the given
B
that it normalises.

We proceed by counting conjugacy classes of
H
and its embeddings, in a finite or algebraic group.

Lemma 4.3.8
.

Let
\overline{u}
and
\overline{s}
be elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
that satisfy the relations

\overline{u}^{3}=\overline{s}^{13}=[\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\overline{s}}]=\overline{u}^{2}\overline{s}^{-1}\overline{u}\overline{s}^{-1}\overline{u}\overline{s}^{-1}\overline{u}\overline{s}^{3}=1.

Then the pair
(\overline{u},\overline{s})
is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
to either
(u,s)
or
(uu^{s},s)
.

In particular,
B
is unique up to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
.

Proof.

We can verify with
Magma
that such presentation gives a
3^{3}\rtimes 13
group, and as previously discussed, a
3^{3}
elementary abelian subgroup is non-toral: in characteristic not 2 or 3 its normaliser is
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\langle\overline{u},\overline{s}\rangle)\simeq 3^{3}\rtimes\SL_{3}(3)
, of which there is only one conjugacy class in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}

[
20
, Theorem 1]
; note also that
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\langle\overline{u},\overline{s}\rangle)
contains a unique conjugacy class of subgroups isomorphic to
3^{3}\rtimes 13
.

Therefore,
\langle\overline{u},\overline{s}\rangle
is conjugate to
\langle u,s\rangle
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, in particular we may replace
\overline{u},\overline{s}
by a conjugate pair such that
\overline{s}=s
and
\overline{u}
lies in
\langle u,u^{s},u^{s^{2}}\rangle\simeq 3^{3}
.

We can verify with
Magma
that the pairs
(u,s)
and
(uu^{s},s)
are not
\langle u,s,t\rangle\text{-conjugate}
, i.e.
\PSL_{2}(27)\text{-conjugate}
, but they are
\PGL_{2}(27)\text{-conjugate}
. If they were
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
then we would have an extension
\PGL_{2}(27)<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, which is not possible as shown in
Proposition

4.3.6
.
∎

Lemma 4.3.9
.

Suppose that
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(27)
is a subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and let
u,s
be the elements obtained from
4.3.3
. Then there are elements
g,\overline{t}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
such that
H^{g}
is generated by a triple
(u,s,\overline{t})
that satisfies presentation (
2.14
) for
H
.

Proof.

H
is generated by a triple
(u_{0},s_{0},t_{0})
that satisfies presentation (
2.14
). Let
\alpha
be an outer automorphism of
H
that fixes
s_{0}
and maps
u_{0}
to
u_{0}u_{0}^{s_{0}}
, then
H
is generated by
(u_{0}^{\alpha},s_{0}^{\alpha},t_{0}^{\alpha})=(u_{0}u_{0}^{s_{0}},s_{0}^{\phantom{\alpha}},t_{0}^{\alpha})
, and such triple satisfies presentation (
2.14
) as well.

By
Lemma

4.3.8
, one of
(u_{0},s_{0})
and
(u_{0}^{\alpha},s_{0}^{\phantom{\alpha}})
is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
to
(u,s)
, hence we may find
g\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
such that
H^{g}
is generated by a triple
(u,s,\overline{t})
that satisfies presentation (
2.14
), where
\overline{t}
is
t_{0}^{\alpha g}
or
t_{0}^{g}
.
∎

We can now deduce part (i) of
Theorem

4.3.2
when
p
is 0 or coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
.

By
Lemma

4.3.9
and
Lemma

4.3.5
we have that there are at most three conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and by
Proposition

4.3.7
we deduce there is exactly one. By
Lemma

4.3.8
we have that for each copy of
3^{3}\rtimes 13
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
we obtain two embeddings, thus we have two conjugacy classes of embeddings in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. Since the characteristic of
k
does not divide
\lvert H\rvert
, we can use
Theorem

2.9.11
to lift this result to characteristic 0 or coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, namely
p\neq 2,3,7,13
.
Proposition

4.3.6
shows that
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
. Lie primitivity is proved in
[
44
, Corollary 3]
.

In order to complete part (ii), we need to find whether
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(q)
, and show that if it does then
H
is unique up to conjugacy; we proceed as discussed in
Section

2.9
.

Lemma 4.3.10
.

If
\mathrm{G}
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
B
, than it is unique up to
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
.

If
\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{G}(p)
with
p\geq 5
, then
\mathrm{G}
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
B
.

If
\mathrm{G}
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
H
, than it is unique up to
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
.

Proof.

Like for the case of the algebraic group, we have that
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)
is unique up to conjugacy; see for example
[
20
]
or
[
3
]
.

Since
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)\simeq 3^{3}\rtimes\SL_{3}(3)
, the result follows as there is a unique conjugacy class of subgroups isomorphic to
B
in
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)
.

The second claim is proved in
[
20
, Theorem 1]
.

Since

C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B)\unlhd N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B)\leq N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B^{\prime})=N,

where the inclusion
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B)\leq N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B^{\prime})
follows from the fact that
B^{\prime}
is the derived subgroup of
B
, as observed in the proof of
Lemma

3.3.4
, we can see that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
is trivial. Therefore we can apply
Corollary

2.9.9
to
H
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
: let
q
be such that
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(q)
, then the unique
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
does not split in
\mathrm{G}(q)
, meaning that there is a exactly one
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
in
\mathrm{G}(q)
.
∎

Proposition 4.3.11
.

H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(q)
in characteristic not dividing
\lvert H\rvert
when
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a splitting field for
f(X)=X^{3}-X^{2}-2X+1
, i.e.
q\equiv\pm 1\bmod 7
.

Proof.

We need to understand what is the action of outer automorphisms of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
on
H
; since we are in odd characteristic, we need to consider only the field automorphism.

Take the Frobenius morphism
F_{p}
; since by
Lemma

4.3.10
there is only one conjugacy class of
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
then there is no choice for
F_{p}
but to permute the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugates}
of
H
. We then assume that
H
is normalised by
F_{p}
and we have to check whether
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(p)
or
\mathrm{G}(p^{2})
.

Since by
Lemma

4.3.10
the unique
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of
B
lies in
\mathrm{G}
,
F_{p}
centralises
B
. Now we look at the action of
F_{p}
on the three copies of
H
containing
B
:

(i)

If
F_{p}
normalises at least one of them, then
F_{p}
acts as an automorphism of
H
that centralises
B
, and the only such element of
\aut H
is trivial. Thus,
F_{p}
centralises
H
.

(ii)

If
F_{p}
permutes them, then
F_{p}
induces the field automorphism of
H
hence permutes the three representations in
Table

4.2
, and this can happen only if
f
does not split over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

In particular,
H<\mathrm{G}(p)
if
f
splits over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and
H<\mathrm{G}(p^{3})
if it does not.
∎

Proposition 4.3.12
.

H
is a maximal subgroup of
\mathrm{G}(q)
,
q
a power of
p
coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, iff
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is the minimal splitting field of characteristic
p
for
f(X)=X^{3}-X^{2}-2X+1
.

Proof.

Since
H
is primitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and
N_{\mathrm{G}}(H)=H
, as observed in
Lemma

2.10.5
we only need to verify that
H
does not lie in any other candidate maximal subgroup.

By
Proposition

4.3.11
we must have that
q=p
or
q=p^{3}
, depending on whether
f
splits over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
or not, otherwise
H<\mathrm{G}(p)<\mathrm{G}(q)
. Since
\mathrm{G}
is
\mathrm{F}_{4}
in odd characteristic, this is the only possibility for
H
to lie in a group of the same type as
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

Furthermore,
H
cannot lie in an almost simple subgroup
M<\mathrm{G}
whose socle
K
is a primitive simple subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
not isomorphic to
H
. The candidates for
K
are the groups in
[
44
, Table 1.2-1.3]
; for
p\neq 2,3,5
they are:
\PSL_{2}(r)
for
r=7,8,9,13,17,25
,
\PSL_{3}(3)
, and
\prescript{3}{}{\mathrm{D}}_{4}(2)
.

We can find their order using the Atlas
[
21
]
or
Magma
, and for almost all of them it is straightforward to verify that
H
cannot be a subgroup of
M
. The only non-obvious case is
K=\!\prescript{3}{}{\mathrm{D}}_{4}(2)
, as
\lvert H\rvert
divides
\lvert M\rvert
in that case. However, we can compute the subgroups of
K
with
Magma
to see that
H
cannot lie in
M
(we used the permutation representation of
K
on 819 points given in
[
1
]
); alternatively, this can be deduced from the list of maximal subgroups of
M
, which is included in the Atlas.
∎

Theorem

4.3.2
(iii) then follows from
Proposition

4.3.12
in characteristic coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
: if
q=p
then we are in odd characteristic so
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
, while if
q=p^{3}
then either
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
and
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
, or
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}.3
and as observed in the proof of
Proposition

4.3.11
we have that the field automorphism of
\mathrm{G}
induces the field automorphism of
H
, hence
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H.3
.

Can we say anything about the embedding in characteristic dividing
\lvert H\rvert
in this case?

As usual, we do not consider characteristic 2 or 3 as the technique we use cannot really work in such cases.

As for characteristic 7, we can still apply
4.3.3
, since the
3^{3}\rtimes\SL(3,3)
subgroup can be found in the same way. The issue is that when looking for
t
in
4.3.4
, we cannot use information about elements of order 7, since they all have trace 0 and do not add any additional condition, so we cannot reduce the linear system any more, leaving a 3-dimensional space of solutions. It is possible to reduce the problem to 2 dimensions, but the field size required to diagonalise
s
(
7^{12}
) is too large to find solutions in a reasonable time.

Remark
.

This case was solved in
[
23
]
.

The case of characteristic 13 is also interesting, as
s
is not semisimple but unipotent. We can still use
4.3.3
, but we cannot use
Theorem

2.6.1
to obtain
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
, nor
Section

3.4.2
to construct
e
, so
4.3.4
has to be changed accordingly.

Construction 4.3.13
.

Construction of
t\in\mathrm{G}(q)
,
q=13
.

As previously mentioned,
u
and
s
can be obtained as in the coprime characteristic case.

The first difference is in constructing a suitable
C(s)\leq C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
, so that we can use the general technique described in
Section

3.4.3
.

Observe that
s
is a regular unipotent element, hence its centraliser in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
is a 4-dimensional subgroup, and in
\mathrm{G}(q)
it is an elementary abelian subgroup of order
q^{4}
. Since
p=13
is coprime with 12, there is a unique conjugacy classes of centralisers of regular unipotent elements (see for example
[
39
, Tables 22.1.4 and 22.2.4]
).

If we have
s
as a product of root elements (that is, a product of terms of type
x_{\alpha}(t)
where
\alpha
is a root and
t\in k
), then one could use for example
[
45
]
to find generators of
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
. Note that, while we constructed
s
by a random search in a matrix group,
Magma
should be able to recover its decomposition as a product of root elements by taking the preimage under the adjoint representation map; this does not work in general for any element of
\GL_{52}(k)
, but it did for us in this specific case.

Regardless, we proceeded in the following way to find
C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)\simeq q^{4}
:

(i)

Compute the fixed point space of
s
on
\mathcal{L}
, which is
4\text{-dimensional}
.

(ii)

For each element of the fixed point space, compute its adjoint action on
\mathcal{L}
.

(iii)

Compute their exponentiation, discard any element that fails the membership test for
\mathrm{G}
; there are
13^{3}
elements left.

(iv)

Initialise
C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)
by taking
C\coloneqq\langle s\rangle
.

(v)

Select a random element among those computed: if it does not lie in
C
, add it to the set of generators.

(vi)

Repeat the process until
C
has 4 generators.

(vii)

Confirm that
C\simeq q^{4}
and centralises
s
, therefore it is
C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)
.

Now we need to find an involution
e
that inverts
s
.

We can find an element
w
of order 3 that normalises
C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)
in the
3^{3}\rtimes\SL_{3}(3)
subgroup that we already have; in particular
w
permutes the three copies of
H
that lie on the given
B
. Then we can locate
e
in
C_{\mathrm{G}}(w)
, in particular we can find exactly one: if there were more than one, then their product would lie in
C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)\cap C_{\mathrm{G}}(w)=1
, a contradiction.

Since
w
is semisimple, we can find a maximal torus containing it. We can also compute the fixed point space on its action on
\mathcal{L}
which is an
\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}
subalgebra and construct the corresponding centraliser which is of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}_{2}

[
25
, Table 4.4]
; here
\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
is simply used to distinguish the two copies of
\mathrm{A}_{2}
. The involution
e
either lies in one of the
\mathrm{A}_{2}
or is a product of an involution in
\mathrm{A}_{2}
and one in
\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
, and we can exclude at least one of the three cases since each of these groups has a unique such class of involutions and we know its trace is
-4
. The groups small enough that it is feasible to compute the required conjugacy class of involutions, and scan through it in order to find the one we need.

We can now proceed as usual, and solve the system of linear equations (
3.8
) to find three solutions corresponding to involutions that extend
B
to
H
. Similarly to
Proposition

4.3.7
, we have that they must be conjugate to each other by an element of order 3 that normalises
B
, like
w
.

Proposition 4.3.14
.

In characteristic 13, there is a unique
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
,
H
is Lie primitive, and
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
.
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(k)
for any field
k
of characteristic 13, and is unique up to conjugacy.

Proof.

Lie primitivity is proved in
[
44
, Corollary 3]
.

Since the only step that needed the characteristic of the field not to divide
\lvert H\rvert
is using the lifting lemma, we only need to prove that there is a unique conjugacy class of
H<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

We can replicate
4.2.4
using a field of order
13
, as shown in
4.3.13
.

We now need to replicate
Lemma

4.3.5
in this case. Observe that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
acts irreducibly on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
, so if we tried to estimate
\dim\boldsymbol{M}(s)
like in the other cases we would get
52^{2}
, while we can check that
\dim M(s)=49
.

However, since
s
is regular and we are not in characteristic 2 or 3, we can use the fact that
\mathcal{L}(C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s))=C_{\mathcal{\overline{L}}}(\mathcal{L}(s))
(see
[
54
, Corollary 5.2]
) to deduce that the dimension of
\mathcal{L}(C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s))
is
49=1+12\times 4
, since the centraliser is made of unipotent elements whose Jordan form has 4 blocks of size 13. Here,
\mathcal{L}(s)
denotes the element of
\mathcal{L}
corresponding to
s\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
; recall that the adjoint representation
\mathrm{ad}
of a Lie algebra and the adjoint representation
\mathrm{Ad}
of a group are related through the exponential map
\exp
(
2.2
).

Therefore
\dim M(s)=\dim\boldsymbol{M}(s)
, which means that the involutions that extend
B
to
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
lie in
\mathrm{G}
.

We can now proceed in a similar fashion as in the general case. In particular,
Lemma

4.2.9
,
Lemma

4.2.10
, and the argument used in
Proposition

4.2.12
still hold in characteristic 13.
∎

4.4
Embedding of
\PSL_{2}(37)
in
\mathrm{E}_{7}

In this section, we consider the embedding of a group
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(37)
into a group of type
\mathrm{E}_{7}
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,19,37
.

Lemma 4.4.1
.

If
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7}
contains a subgroup
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(37)
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,19,37
, then
H
is Lie primitive, acts as
18\oplus 38
on the minimal module
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
, and as
19\oplus 38_{1}\oplus 38_{2}\oplus 38_{3}
on the adjoint module
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
: there are two such representations, not isomorphic to each other but
\aut B\text{-conjugate}
, where
B
is a Borel subgroup of
H
, of the form
37\rtimes 18
.

Furthermore, if
\chi
is the character of an embedding of
H
into
\mathrm{E}_{7}
in the adjoint representation, then
\chi
is one of the characters in
Table

4.3
, where classes with the same character value have been merged for brevity. Here, we have that

\displaystyle z_{37}=1

\displaystyle+y^{2}+y^{5}+y^{6}+y^{8}+y^{13}+y^{14}+y^{15}+y^{17}+y^{18}+

\displaystyle+y^{19}+y^{20}+y^{22}+y^{23}+y^{24}+y^{29}+y^{31}+y^{32}+y^{35},

where
y
is a primitive 37th root of unity; the minimal polynomial of
z_{37}
is
X^{2}-X-9
.

Proof.

The fact that
H
may only embed primitively in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7}
is proved in
[
44
, Corollary 3]
, and the action on the minimal and adjoint module is described in
[
44
, Table 5.5.97]
.

The character values can be deduced using the character table of
\PSL_{2}(37)

[
21
]
together with Litterick’s program in
[
44
]
, which allows to compute feasible characters of the embedding. A computed list of traces of semisimple elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7,\mathrm{sc}}
is available in
[
24
]
. Observe that since we are working with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7,\mathrm{ad}}
, for the traces of involutions we also need to consider traces of elements of order 4 of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7,\mathrm{sc}}
.

In particular, since the character of degree 19 can be any of the two possible ones, the only feasible traces on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
of semisimple elements of order 2 and 3 we can obtain are
-7
and
-2
, respectively, by taking the three characters of degree 38 that have value
-2
on involutions and
-1
on elements of order 3.
∎
