Table 4.3:
The possible characters
\chi_{i}
of
\PSL_{2}(37)
in
\mathrm{E}_{7}
in adjoint representation.

Theorem 4.4.2
.

Let
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(37)
be a subgroup of the adjoint group
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7}
in characteristic
p
, and let
\sigma
be a Steinberg endomorphism of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
with
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
. Suppose
p\neq 2,3,19,37
, and that
H
acts as
18\oplus 38
on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
, and as
19\oplus 38_{1}\oplus 38_{2}\oplus 38_{3}
on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
. Then:

(i)

There is a unique
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
, which gives rise to two
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
classes of embeddings,
H
is Lie primitive, and
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
.

(ii)

H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{G}(q)
,
q
a power of
p
, iff
f(X)=X^{2}-X-9
splits over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. In such cases, there are two conjugacy classes of
H
. If
\mathrm{G}
is simple, there are twice as many classes.

(iii)

If
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
is an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{G}
, then
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
is maximal iff either
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
and
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is the minimal splitting field for
f
, in which case
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
, or
\mathbb{F}_{p}
is not a splitting field,
q=p^{2}
, and
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}.2
is an extension by a field automorphism, in which case
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H.2\simeq\PGL_{2}(37)
.

The proof will be divided in several steps.

Presentation
.

Consider the following matrices, generating
\SL_{2}(37)
:

u\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad s\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}19&0\\
0&2\end{pmatrix},\qquad t\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

We are going to consider a presentation for
\PSL_{2}(37)
whose generators are the images of
u,s,t
when taking the quotient over scalar matrices, which we will denote with the same notation, and whose relations are given by (
2.15
).

Furthermore,
B=\langle u,s\rangle\simeq 37\rtimes 18
is a Borel subgroup of
H
, and its derived subgroup is
B^{\prime}=\langle u\rangle\simeq 37
.

Construction 4.4.3
.

Construction of
u,s
in
\mathrm{E}_{7,\mathrm{ad}}(k)
for some finite field
k
.

Note that we want a field that contains the character values in
Table

4.3
,
u
to be semisimple, and while not required to construct
B
, for further computations later on we also need
s
to be diagonalisable, so we take
k
to contain 18th roots of unity. Therefore, the minimum field
k
we can use has order
5329=73^{2}
; observe that it is congruent to 1 modulo 4, 18, and 37. Define
\mathrm{G}\coloneqq\mathrm{E}_{7,\mathrm{ad}}(k)
.

By
Lemma

2.8.1
, since
B
is supersoluble, there exists a maximal torus
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathrm{G}
such that its normaliser
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
B
. In particular, we will look for
u
in
\mathrm{T}
, and for
s
in
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
.

We can proceed as described in
Section

3.3
, like we did in
4.2.3
. We compute
\widetilde{W}=\langle n_{i}:i=1,\ldots,7\rangle
, and verify that
\widetilde{W}=T_{2,1}.W
, then we take random elements of
\widetilde{W}
until we find an element of order 18 whose image in
W
has order 18.

We can check with
Magma
that the Weyl group of
\mathrm{E}_{7}
has only one class of elements of order 18, so
s
must lie in the correct class. We can compute that
\lvert C_{\mathrm{T}}(s)\rvert=2
, so there are two
\mathrm{T}\text{-conjugacy}
classes of
s
in
\langle\mathrm{T},s\rangle
. This will be addressed in
Lemma

4.4.10
.

Let
\zeta
be a primitive element of
k^{\times}
, so that
h_{i}\coloneqq h(\zeta^{\delta_{ij}})_{j=1}^{7}\in\GL_{133}(k)
with
1\leq i\leq 7
are generators of the torus, and
h^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq h_{i}^{144}
are generators of the Sylow
37\text{-subgroup}
of the torus. By computing
{h^{\prime}_{i}}^{s}=\prod_{j=1}^{7}{h^{\prime}_{j}}^{a_{ij}}
we obtain a matrix
A=(a_{ij})
with coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{37}
describing the action of
s
on
T
.

Since we have
u^{s}=u^{4}
, we can look at the kernel of
A-4\mathbb{1}_{7}
, which is generated by a vector, say
z=(z_{j})_{j=1}^{7}
. We define
u\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{7}{h^{\prime}_{j}}^{z_{j}}\in\GL_{133}(k)
, then by construction
u,s
satisfy
u^{37}=s^{18}=u^{s}u^{-4}=1
.

Construction 4.4.4
.

Construction of
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

We can compute that the fixed point space of
s
acting on
L(G)
is 7-dimensional, hence it is a Cartan subalgebra of
L(G)
by
Corollary

2.6.5
. Therefore, we can apply
Lemma

3.4.4
and follow
4.2.4
verbatim up to solving the system of equations (
3.8
) obtained by using the relation
(ceu)^{3}=1
. Naturally, this time we will work in
M_{133}(k)
instead, and we have
M(s)
and
\boldsymbol{M}(s)
of dimension 127, corresponding to the sum of 126 one-dimensional root spaces and a Cartan subalgebra.

We obtain a system of
7\times 133=931
equations in
127
variables, but the space of solutions is
2\text{-dimensional}
, meaning that we have not obtained enough independent equations.

Call
M,N
two matrices spanning the space of solutions, then we shall see that there are two pairs of coefficients
(a,b)
,
a,b\in\overline{k}
, such that
aM+bN
lies in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
and solves the linear system.

Since the space is only
2\text{-dimensional}
, it is possible to brute-force the problem. We use the fact that the determinant of matrices in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
is 1 to reduce the number of membership tests to perform; it is easy to verify that
a,b\neq 0
, so we have:

1=\det(aM+bN)=a^{133}\det\biggl(M+\frac{b}{a}N\biggr).

Initialise an empty set
U_{0}
of solutions. Then, for each
i\in k
:

(i)

Compute
M+iN
.

(ii)

If
d\coloneqq\det(M+iN)\neq 0
, find all the
c\in k
such that
dc^{133}=1
.

(iii)

For all such
c
, check whether
c(M+iN)\in\mathrm{G}
using the membership test described in
Section

3.2
.

(iv)

If
c(M+iN)\in\mathrm{G}
, add
cM+ciN
to
U_{0}
.

In particular,
U_{0}
contains precisely two matrices, both defined over
k
.

Lemma 4.4.5
.

If
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
is such that
(u,s,t)
satisfy (
2.15
), then
t\in(U_{0}\cap\boldsymbol{G})e
, where
e
is a computed element of
\mathrm{G}
and
U_{0}
is a computed set of two elements of
\mathrm{G}
. In particular,
t\in\mathrm{G}
.

Proof.

This follows from computer calculations, doing
4.4.3
and
4.4.4
. The relations (
2.15
)((ii),(vii)) were not used during the construction of
u,s,t
, so we verify them using the computed matrices
u,s,t
.
∎

We can verify with
Magma
that the two choices of
t
give rise to two distinct groups
H_{1},H_{2}
isomorphic to
\PSL_{2}(37)
containing
B
, so the question is whether they are conjugate.

We now discuss
\aut H
, and show that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugate}
to each other.

Proposition 4.4.6
.

\PGL_{2}(37)
does not embed in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, hence
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
.

Proof.

Looking at the character table of
\PGL_{2}(37)

[
21
]
, we can see that elements of order 37 have rational trace, so if
\PGL_{2}(37)
embedded into
\mathrm{E}_{7}(\mathbb{C})
this would contradict
Table

4.3
.

Alternatively: the diagonal automorphism of
H
does not stabilize the module of dimension 18 (in the action on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
) or 19 (in the action on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
), so the module cannot extend to
\PGL_{2}(37)
.
∎

Proposition 4.4.7
.

The groups
H_{1},H_{2}
constructed in
4.4.4
are
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugate}
to each other.

Proof.

Since they both contain
B
, and
N_{\mathrm{G}}(H)=H
by
Proposition

4.4.6
, we can look at the quotient
N_{G}(B)/N_{H}(B)
, as any element in
N_{G}(B)/N_{H}(B)
would not lie in
H
and therefore could only permute the
H_{i}
.

Since
B
is a Borel subgroup of
H
,
N_{H}(B)=B
by
Corollary

2.2.3
. Since
B^{\prime}=\langle u\rangle
is cyclic hence supersoluble, by
Lemma

3.3.4

N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)\leq N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T}),

therefore we can use
Magma
to compute
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)=N_{N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})}(B)=B.2
, and obtain
\lvert N_{\mathrm{G}}{B}/N_{H}(B)\rvert=2
. A preimage of a generator of the quotient then normalises
B
and acts on the two copies of
H
without stabilising them; the only option for it is to conjugate them.
∎

Note that such an element of order 2 can be easily constructed with
Magma
by constructing the normaliser and quotient described in the proof, and it is straightforward to check that it does indeed conjugate
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
.

Lemma 4.4.8
.

Let
\overline{u}
and
\overline{s}
be elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
that satisfy the relations

\overline{u}^{37}=\overline{s}^{18}=\overline{u}^{\overline{s}}\overline{u}^{-4}=1.

(4.3)

Suppose further that all elements of
\langle\overline{u}\rangle
have trace
3+z_{37}
or
4-z_{37}
in the adjoint representation. Then the ordered pair
(\overline{u},\overline{s})
is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
to either
(u,s)
or
(u^{2},s)
.

In particular,
B
is unique up to conjugacy in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

Proof.

Since
B
is supersoluble, by
Lemma

2.8.1
we can assume that any subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
isomorphic to it lies in
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
for some maximal torus
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

We compute that the action of
u
on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
has a 7-dimensional fixed point space, i.e. the Cartan subalgebra corresponding to
u
by
Corollary

2.6.5
, so
u
is a regular element; furthermore, we can use
Lemma

3.3.2
to show that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
is finite. Therefore, by
Proposition

2.9.4
we have that there is a single conjugacy class of subgroups isomorphic to
B
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, since there is a unique conjugacy class in
W(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
of elements of order
18
, as observed in
4.4.3
.

Observe that the pair
(u^{2},s)
satisfies (
4.3
); if
(u,s)
and
(u^{2},s)
were
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
, then
37\rtimes 36
would embed in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, hence in
\mathrm{E}_{7}(\mathbb{C})
by
[
28
, Corollary A2.7]
. But by
[
8
]
every supersoluble subgroup of
\mathrm{E}_{7}(\mathbb{C})
is contained in the normaliser of a torus. Since we are working with adjoint
\mathrm{E}_{7}
, its centre is trivial, so an element
s^{*}
of
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
of order 36 that centralises
s
and normalises but does not centralise
\langle u\rangle
must project to an element of
W
of order 36. Thus
W_{\mathrm{E}_{7}}
would have an element of order 36, contradiction.

Alternatively, one could proceed as in
Lemma

4.2.9
:
(u,s)
and
(u^{2},s)
are not
\PSL_{2}(37)
-conjugate but they are
\PGL_{2}(37)\text{-conjugate}
, therefore if there was an element of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
conjugating
(u,s)
to
(u^{2},s)
then we would find a subgroup
\PGL_{2}(37)
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, which is not possible by
Proposition

4.4.6
. This concludes the proof as we can verify with
Magma
that there are only two
B\text{-conjugacy}
classes of pairs of elements satisfying (
4.3
). ∎

Lemma 4.4.9
.

Suppose that
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(37)
is a subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and let
u,s
be the elements obtained from
4.4.3
. Then there are elements
g,\overline{t}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
such that
H^{g}
is generated by a triple
(u,s,\overline{t})
that satisfies presentation (
2.15
) for
H
.

Proof.

H
is generated by a triple
(u_{0},s_{0},t_{0})
that satisfies (
2.15
). Let
\alpha
be an outer automorphism of
H
that fixes
s_{0}
and maps
u_{0}
to
u_{0}^{2}
, then
H
is generated by
(u_{0}^{\alpha},s_{0}^{\alpha},t_{0}^{\alpha})=(u_{0}^{2},s_{0}^{\phantom{\alpha}},t_{0}^{\alpha})
, and such triple satisfies (
2.15
) as well.

By
Lemma

4.4.8
, one of
(u_{0},s_{0})
and
(u_{0}^{\alpha},s_{0}^{\phantom{\alpha}})
is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
to
(u,s)
, hence we may find
g\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
such that
H^{g}
is generated by a triple
(u,s,\overline{t})
that satisfies (
2.15
), where
\overline{t}
is
t_{0}^{\alpha g}
or
t_{0}^{g}
.
∎

We can now deduce part (i) of
Theorem

4.4.2
.

From
Lemma

4.4.9
and
Lemma

4.4.5
, we have that there are at most two conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and from
Proposition

4.4.7
we deduce there is exactly one. From
Lemma

4.4.8
we have that for each copy of
37\rtimes 18
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
we obtain two embeddings, thus we have two conjugacy classes of embeddings in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. Since the characteristic of
k
does not divide
\lvert H\rvert
, we can use
Theorem

2.9.11
to generalise this result to characteristic 0 or coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, namely
p\neq 2,3,19,37
.
Proposition

4.4.6
shows that
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
. Lie primitivity is proved in
[
44
, Corollary 3]
.

In order to complete part (ii), we need to understand what happens when going down to the finite group.

Lemma 4.4.10
.

If
B
embeds in
\mathrm{G}
, there are two conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to
B
in
\mathrm{G}
.

If
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}
, there are two conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to
H
in
\mathrm{G}
.

Proof.

Recall from
Lemma

4.4.8
that
u
is regular in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, so
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(u)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
and
s\in N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
; furthermore, there is a single conjugacy class of elements of order 18 in
W(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
. Then,

C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B)=C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(u)\cap C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\cap C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)=C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s).

In
Lemma

4.4.8
we checked that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
is finite, and using
Lemma

3.3.3
we can compute
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
and show that
\lvert C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)\rvert=2
.

Then by
Corollary

2.9.9
there are two
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
classes of subgroups isomorphic to
B
when
B<\mathrm{G}
.

Furthermore,
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B)
so each
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
splits into two
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
classes, one for each
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
class of the
B
subgroup.
∎

Remark
.

Observe that with
4.4.4
we can only construct one of the two classes. Indeed, given
s\in N_{\mathrm{G}(q)}(\mathrm{T})
a representative of an element of
W_{\mathrm{E}_{7}}
of order 18, we have that not all elements of
\mathrm{T}s
are diagonalisable in
\GL_{133}(q)
, some require a
q^{2}
extension, and those would be generators of elements of the other class. The two conjugacy classes of
B
in
\mathrm{G}(q)
are
\mathrm{G}(q^{2})\text{-conjugate}
via a diagonal automorphism, and a new conjugacy class of subgroups isomorphic to
B
arises in
\mathrm{G}(q^{2})
.

Remark
.

For small fields, we can check computationally that there are two conjugacy classes of subgroups of
\mathrm{G}
isomorphic to
B
, by constructing
B
as in
4.4.4
and
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
as in
Section

3.1
. If the field
k
is small enough, we can look directly for all the conjugacy classes of subgroups of
\langle\mathrm{T},s\rangle
isomorphic to
B
: this results in twelve classes of subgroups, which fuse into two classes inside
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
, as claimed. In the standard representation, we have that
C_{\mathrm{G}}(s)=Z(\mathrm{G})
, while in the adjoint representation the centraliser of
s
is a torus element that hints at the automorphism fusing the two classes in
\mathrm{G}(k^{2})
(where a new conjugacy class arises).

Proposition 4.4.11
.

H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(q)
in characteristic not dividing
\lvert H\rvert
when
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a splitting field for
f(X)=X^{2}-X-9
.

Proof.

We need to understand what is the action of outer automorphisms of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
on
H
; since we are in odd characteristic, we need to consider both the field and the diagonal automorphism.

The action of the diagonal automorphism is clear from
Proposition

4.4.6
: as it cannot normalise
H
, it must swap two classes. Therefore, when taking
\mathrm{G}
to be simple, we obtain twice as many classes.

Now take the Frobenius morphism
F_{p}
; since there is only one conjugacy class of
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, then the only choice for
F_{p}
is to permute the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugates}
of
H
, so we assume that
H
is normalised by
F_{p}
. As
\lvert\out H\rvert=2
, certainly
F_{p^{2}}
centralises
H
, up to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
, so
H<\mathrm{G}(p^{2})
.

Let
g\in H
be an element of order 37, and observe
Table

4.3
:

(i)

If
f
does not split over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, then
F_{p}
must send
\chi(g)
to
\chi(g^{2})
, in particular it cannot centralise
H
. This implies that in such case, in
\mathrm{G}(p^{2}).2
the normaliser of
H
is
H.2
.

(ii)

If
f
splits over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
(equiv.
p
is a square modulo 37 by
Lemma

2.9.10
) that cannot happen, thus
F_{p}
cannot induce an outer automorphism on
H
, hence
H<\mathrm{G}(p)
.

∎

Proposition 4.4.12
.

H
is a maximal subgroup of
\mathrm{G}(q)
,
q
coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, iff
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a minimal splitting field of
f(X)=X^{2}-X-9
.

Proof.

Since
H
is primitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and
N_{\mathrm{G}}(H)=H
, as observed in
Lemma

2.10.5
we only need to verify that
H
does not lie in any other candidate maximal subgroup.

By
Proposition

4.4.11
we have that
H<\mathrm{G}(q)
if
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a splitting field of
f
, so if
\mathbb{F}_{q}
was not minimal among such fields then
H
would not be maximal in
\mathrm{G}
as it would lie in a group with the same type as
\mathrm{G}
defined over some subfield of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

Furthermore,
H
cannot lie in an almost simple group
M<\mathrm{G}
whose socle
K
is a primitive simple subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
not isomorphic to
H
. The list of possible choice for
K
is contained in
[
44
, Table 1.2-1.3]
; for
p\neq 2,3,19,37
they are:
\alt_{n}
for
n=5,6,7,8
,
\PSL_{2}(r)
for
r=7,8,11,13,19,27,29
,
\PSL_{3}(4)
,
\PSU_{3}(3)
,
\PSU_{3}(8)
,
\Omega_{8}^{+}(2)
,
M_{12}
,
M_{22}
,
\Ru
, and
\HS
.

We can find their order using the Atlas
[
21
]
or
Magma
, and it is straightforward to check that none of them has elements of order 37, hence
H
cannot be a subgroup of
M
in any of such cases.
∎

Theorem

4.4.2
(iii) then follows from
Proposition

4.4.12
in characteristic coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
: if
q=p
then
N_{\mathrm{G}}(H)=H
is maximal in
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
, while if
q=p^{2}
then either
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
and
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
, or
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}.2
(extension by a field automorphism) and as observed in the proof of
Proposition

4.4.11
we have that
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H.2
.

Remark
.

We cannot draw any conclusion for the primes dividing
\lvert H\rvert
. Theoretically, we could work in characteristic 19, as the prime is not involved at any point in the construction (unlike for example
p=37
); however, the required field size would be too large to perform the computations, in particular
4.4.4
.

4.5
Embedding of
\PSL_{2}(29)
in
\mathrm{E}_{7}

In this section, we consider the embedding of a group
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(29)
into a group of type
\mathrm{E}_{7}
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,5,7,29
; this case is very similar to that of
\PSL_{2}(37)
, but with some additional complication in the computations.

Lemma 4.5.1
.

If
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7}
contains a subgroup
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(29)
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,5,7,29
, then
H
is Lie primitive, acts as
28_{1}\oplus 28_{2}
on the minimal module
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
, and as
15\oplus 28\oplus 30_{1}\oplus 30_{2}\oplus 30_{3}
on the adjoint module
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
: there are four such representations, not isomorphic to each other, but split into two pairs under the action of
\aut H
.

Furthermore, if
\chi
is the character of an embedding of
H
into
\mathrm{E}_{7}
in the adjoint representation, then
\chi
is one of the characters in
Table

4.4
, where classes with the same character value have been merged for brevity. Here,
y_{1}=1+\zeta_{5}^{2}+\zeta_{5}^{3}
and
y_{2}=1+\zeta_{5}+\zeta_{5}^{4}
, where
\zeta_{5}
is a primitive 5th root of unity; the minimal polynomial of
y_{i}
is
X^{2}-X-1
. Also,

z=1+\zeta_{29}^{2}+\zeta_{29}^{3}+\zeta_{29}^{8}+\zeta_{29}^{10}+\zeta_{29}^{11}+\zeta_{29}^{12}+\zeta_{29}^{14}+\zeta_{29}^{15}+\zeta_{29}^{17}+\zeta_{29}^{18}+\zeta_{29}^{19}+\zeta_{29}^{21}+\zeta_{29}^{26}+\zeta_{29}^{27},

where
\zeta_{29}
is a primitive 29th root of unity; the minimal polynomial of
z
is
X^{2}-X-7
.

Proof.

The fact that
H
embeds only primitively in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7}
is proved in
[
44
, Corollary 3]
, and the action on the minimal and adjoint module is described in
[
44
, Table 5.5.92]
.

The character values can be deduced using the character table of
\PSL_{2}(29)

[
21
]
together with Litterick’s program in
[
44
]
, which allows to compute feasible characters of the embedding. A computed list of traces of semisimple elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7,\mathrm{sc}}
is available in
[
24
]
. Observe that since we are working with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7,\mathrm{ad}}
, for the traces of involutions we also need to consider traces of elements of order 4 of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7,\mathrm{sc}}
.

In particular, since the character of degree 15 can be any of the two possible ones, the only feasible traces on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
of semisimple elements of order 2 and 3 we can obtain are
-7
and
-2
, respectively, by taking the three characters of degree 30 that have value
-2
on involutions, and one of the two characters of degree 28 that has value
-2
on elements of order 3.
∎

Order

1

2

3
