Table 4.4:
The possible characters
\chi_{i}
of
\PSL_{2}(29)
in
\mathrm{E}_{7}
in adjoint representation.

Theorem 4.5.2
.

Let
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(29)
be a subgroup of the adjoint group
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7}
in characteristic
p
and let
\sigma
be a Steinberg endomorphism of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
with
\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}^{\sigma}
. Suppose
p\neq 2,3,5,7,29
, and that
H
acts as
28_{1}\oplus 28_{2}
on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
, and as
15\oplus 28\oplus 30_{1}\oplus 30_{2}\oplus 30_{3}
on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{7})
. Then:

(i)

There are two
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
classes of subgroups isomorphic to
H
, which give rise to four
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
classes of embeddings,
H
is Lie primitive, and
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
.

(ii)

H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{G}(q)
,
q
a power of
p
, iff
f_{1}(X)=X^{2}-X-1
and
f_{2}(X)=X^{2}-X-7
split over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. In such cases, there are four conjugacy classes of
H
. If
\mathrm{G}
is simple, there are twice as many classes.

(iii)

If
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
is an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{G}(q)
, then
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)
is maximal in
\overline{\mathrm{G}}
iff one of the following holds:

(a)

\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
and either
q=p
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a splitting field for
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, or
q=p^{2}
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a not a splitting field for at least on of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
; in these cases,
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}=H
.

(b)

\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a splitting field for
f_{1}
but not for
f_{2}
,
q=p^{2}
, and
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}.2
is an extension by a field automorphism, in which case
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H.2\simeq\PGL_{2}(29)
.

The proof will be divided in several steps.

Presentation
.

Consider the following matrices, generating
\SL_{2}(29)
:

u\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad s\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}15&0\\
0&2\end{pmatrix},\qquad t\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

We are going to consider a presentation for
\PSL_{2}(29)
whose generators are the images of
u,s,t
when taking the quotient over scalar matrices, which we will denote with the same notation, and whose relations are given by (
2.16
).

Furthermore,
B=\langle u,s\rangle\simeq 29\rtimes 14
is a Borel subgroup of
H
, and its derived subgroup is
B^{\prime}=\langle u\rangle\simeq 29
.

Construction 4.5.3
.

Construction of
u,s
in
\mathrm{E}_{7,\mathrm{ad}}(k)
for some finite field
k
.

Note that we want a field that contains the character values in
Table

4.4
,
u
to be semisimple, and while not required to construct
B
, for further computations later on we also need
s
to be diagonalisable so we take
k
to contain 14th roots of unity. It looks like we could consider the field
k
of order
5279=29\times 14\times 13+1
; however, we could not diagonalise
s
without a field extension, so we ended up choosing
36541=29\times 14\times 90+1
; and defined
\mathrm{G}\coloneqq\mathrm{E}_{7,\mathrm{ad}}(k)
.

By
Lemma

2.8.1
, since
B
is supersoluble, there exists a maximal torus
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathrm{G}
such that its normaliser
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
B
. In particular, we will look for
u
in
\mathrm{T}
, and for
s
in
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})
.

Similarly to
4.4.3
, we compute
\widetilde{W}=\langle n_{i}:i=1,\ldots,7\rangle
, and take random elements of
\widetilde{W}
until we find an element
s
of order 14 whose image in
W
has order 14.

We can check with
Magma
that the Weyl group of
\mathrm{E}_{7}
has only one class of elements of order 14, so
s
must lie in the correct class. We can compute that
C_{\mathrm{T}}(s)=2
, so there are two
\mathrm{T}\text{-conjugacy}
classes of
s
in
\langle\mathrm{T},s\rangle
. This will be addressed in
Lemma

4.5.10
.

Let
\zeta
be a primitive element of
k^{\times}
, so that
h_{i}\coloneqq h(\zeta^{\delta_{ij}})_{j=1}^{7}\in\GL_{133}(k)
with
1\leq i\leq 7
are generators of the torus, and
h^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq h_{i}^{1260}
are generators of the Sylow
29\text{-subgroup}
of the torus. By computing
{h^{\prime}_{i}}^{s}=\prod_{j=1}^{7}{h^{\prime}_{j}}^{a_{ij}}
we obtain a matrix
A=(a_{ij})
with coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{29}
describing the action of
s
on
\mathrm{T}
.

Since we have
u^{s}=u^{4}
, we can look at the kernel of
A-4\mathbb{1}_{7}
, which is generated by a vector, say
z=(z_{j})_{j=1}^{7}
. We define
u\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{7}{h^{\prime}_{j}}^{z_{j}}\in\GL_{133}(k)
, then by construction
u,s
satisfy
u^{29}=s^{14}=u^{s}u^{-4}=1
.

Construction 4.5.4
.

Construction of
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

As in the other cases we studied, we can find an involution
e\in\mathrm{G}
that inverts
s
as described in
Section

3.4.2
.

This time, the computed fixed point space of the action of
s
on
L(\mathrm{G})
is
9\text{-dimensional}
and in particular it is of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}\mathrm{T}_{6}
, meaning that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)^{\circ}
is an
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}_{6}
group (see
Theorem

2.6.1
).

We construct
C(s)=\langle\mathrm{T},\mathrm{A}_{1}(k),n_{w}\colon s^{w}=s\rangle
, where
\mathrm{A}_{1}(k)
is the group generated by
x_{\pm r}(1)
, where
\pm r
are the roots corresponding to the subspaces fixed by
s
, and
n_{w}
are as defined in
Theorem

2.6.1
.

Now we can proceed in the usual fashion by using
C(s)
, and taking
M(s)
to be the linear subspace of
M_{133}(k)
spanned by elements of
C(s)
. In particular, we can compute with
Magma
that the dimension of
M(s)
is 198.

By
Lemma

3.4.5
,
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
and
C(s)
stabilise the same subspaces of
L(\mathrm{G})
, so we can compute the upper bound for
\dim\boldsymbol{M}(s)
established in
Lemma

3.4.3
by using the action of
C(s)
instead.

We can compute with
Magma
that
L(\mathrm{G})
decomposes as a direct sum of
3\oplus 1^{60}\oplus 2^{32}\oplus 0^{6}
, thus the matrices representing it span a space of dimension at most
198=1\times 3^{2}+60\times 1^{2}+32\times 2^{2}+1
.

Since the bounds on the dimensions coincide, we can conclude that a basis of
M(s)
is also a basis of
\boldsymbol{M}(s)
.

We can now use the relation
(ceu)^{3}=1
as in the other cases, and compute the system (
3.8
) of
6\times 133=798
equations in
198
variables. Note the 6 instead of 7: this is because unlike the other cases we studied the centraliser is not a maximal torus, but a
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}_{6}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
group; therefore, it does not fix a Cartan subalgebra, but only a 6-dimensional subspace, as the remaining 1-dimensional complement is not fixed by the action of the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
component.

The space of solutions
M_{0}
is
4\text{-dimensional}
, and given the field we are working with, it is unreasonable to attempt to brute-force this case by checking all possible 4-tuples of coefficients over
k
.

First, we can divide the problem into two smaller ones by considering traces of elements of order 5, similarly to how we did in
Lemma

4.3.5
. The possible traces are shown in
Table

4.4
, which means we obtain two different systems with a
2\text{-dimensional}
space of solutions each.

Now we proceed to solve each system separately; however, they are now nonhomogeneous, se we cannot do the same as in
4.4.4
. Let
A
be the nonhomogeneous part, and let
C,D
be generators of the corresponding homogeneous system, so that a solution is of the form
Z=A+XC+YD
, for some
X,Y\in\overline{k}
. Then:

(i)

Compute
P(X,Y)=\det Z-1\in k[X,Y]
.

(ii)

For each choice of
x\in k
, evaluate the polynomial to obtain
P_{x}(Y)=P(x,Y)\in k[Y]
.

(iii)

Factorize
P_{x}(Y)
and check whether linear terms exist.

(iv)

Each such term gives a choice
y\in k
such that the pair
(x,y)
gives a solution to the linear system.

(v)

Check whether
Z\in\mathrm{G}
by using the membership test described in
Section

3.2
.

In particular, each choice for the traces of elements of order 5 gives two solutions to the resulting linear system, therefore producing all the four solutions
U_{0}
of the original system.

Lemma 4.5.5
.

If
t\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
is such that
(u,s,t)
satisfy (
2.16
), then
t\in(U_{0}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})e
, where
e
is a computed element of
\mathrm{G}
and
U_{0}
is computed set of four elements of
\mathrm{G}
. In particular,
t\in\mathrm{G}
.

Proof.

This follows from computer calculations, doing
4.5.3
and
4.5.4
. The relations (
2.16
)((ii),(vii)) were not used during the construction of
u,s,t
, so we verify them using the computed matrices
u,s,t
.
∎

We can verify with
Magma
that the four choices of
t
give rise to four distinct groups
H_{1},\ldots,H_{4}
isomorphic to
\PSL_{2}(29)
containing
B
, so the question is whether they are conjugate.

Proposition 4.5.6
.

\PGL_{2}(29)
does not embed in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, so
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
.

Proof.

Looking at the character table for
\PGL_{2}(29)
, we can see that there are elements of order 29 with rational trace, so if
\PGL_{2}(29)
embedded into
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
this would contradict
Table

4.4
.
∎

Proposition 4.5.7
.

The four copies of
H
constructed in
4.5.4
are not
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugate}
. However, they form two pairs that are
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugate}
; each pair is made of the copies of
H
having the same traces on the corresponding conjugacy classes of elements of order 5.

Proof.

Since they all intersect in
B
, and
N_{\mathrm{G}}(H)=H
by
Proposition

4.5.6
, we can look at
N_{G}(B)/N_{H}(B)
, as any element in
N_{G}(B)/N_{H}(B)
would not lie in
H
and therefore could only permute the
H_{i}
.

Since
B
is a Borel subgroup of
H
,
N_{H}(B)=B
by
Corollary

2.2.3
. Since
B^{\prime}=\langle u\rangle
is cyclic hence supersoluble, by
Lemma

3.3.4

N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)\leq N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T}),

therefore we can use
Magma
to compute
N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)=N_{N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{T})}(B)=B.2
, hence
\lvert N_{\mathrm{G}}(B)/N_{H}(B)\rvert=2
. A preimage of a generator of the quotient then normalises
B
and acts on the copies of
H
without stabilising them; the only option for it is to conjugate them in pairs.

The last assertion can be verified with
Magma
.
∎

Lemma 4.5.8
.

Let
\overline{u}
and
\overline{s}
be elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
that satisfy the relations

\overline{u}^{29}=\overline{s}^{14}=\overline{u}^{\overline{s}}\overline{u}^{-4}=1.

(4.4)

Suppose further that all elements of
\langle\overline{u}\rangle
have trace
2+z
or
3-z
in the adjoint representation. Then the ordered pair
(\overline{u},\overline{s})
is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
to either
(u,s)
or
(u^{2},s)
.

In particular,
B
is unique up to conjugacy in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

Proof.

Since
B
is supersoluble, by
Lemma

2.8.1
we can assume that any subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
isomorphic to it lies in
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
for some maximal torus
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
.

We compute that the action of
u
on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
has a 7-dimensional fixed point space, i.e. the Cartan subalgebra corresponding to
u
by
Corollary

2.6.5
, so
u
is a regular element; furthermore, we can use
Lemma

3.3.2
to show that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
is finite. Therefore, by
Proposition

2.9.4
we have that there is a single conjugacy class of subgroups isomorphic to
B
is unique up to conjugacy in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
since there is a unique conjugacy class in
W(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
of elements of order
14
, as observed in
4.5.3
.

Observe that the pair
(u^{2},s)
satisfies (
4.4
); if
(u,s)
and
(u^{2},s)
were
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
, then
29\rtimes 28
would embed in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, hence in
\mathrm{E}_{7}(\mathbb{C})
by
[
28
, Corollary A2.7]
. But by
[
8
]
every supersoluble subgroup of
\mathrm{E}_{7}(\mathbb{C})
is contained in the normaliser of a torus. Since we are working with adjoint
\mathrm{E}_{7}
, its centre is trivial, so an element
s^{*}
of
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
of order 28 that centralises
s
and normalises but does not centralise
\langle u\rangle
must project to an element of
W
of order 28. Thus
W_{\mathrm{E}_{7}}
would have an element of order 28, contradiction.

Alternatively, one could proceed as in
Lemma

4.2.9
:
(u,s)
and
(u^{2},s)
are not
\PSL_{2}(29)
-conjugate but they are
\PGL_{2}(29)\text{-conjugate}
, therefore if there was an element of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
conjugating
(u,s)
to
(u^{2},s)
then we would find a subgroup
\PGL_{2}(29)
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, which is not possible by
Proposition

4.5.6
. This concludes the proof as we can verify with
Magma
that there are only two
B\text{-conjugacy}
classes of pairs of elements satisfying (
4.4
).
∎

Lemma 4.5.9
.

Suppose that
H\simeq\PSL_{2}(29)
is a subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and let
u,s
be the elements obtained from
4.5.3
. Then there are elements
g,\overline{t}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
such that
H^{g}
is generated by a triple
(u,s,\overline{t})
that satisfies presentation (
2.16
) for
H
.

Proof.

H
is generated by a triple
(u_{0},s_{0},t_{0})
that satisfies (
2.16
). Let
\alpha
be an outer automorphism of
H
that fixes
s_{0}
and maps
u_{0}
to
u_{0}^{2}
, then
H
is generated by
(u_{0}^{\alpha},s_{0}^{\alpha},t_{0}^{\alpha})=(u_{0}^{2},s_{0}^{\phantom{\alpha}},t_{0}^{\alpha})
, and such triple satisfies (
2.16
) as well.

By
Lemma

4.5.8
, one of
(u_{0},s_{0})
and
(u_{0}^{\alpha},s_{0}^{\phantom{\alpha}})
is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugate}
to
(u,s)
, hence we may find
g\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
such that
H^{g}
is generated by a triple
(u,s,\overline{t})
that satisfies (
2.16
), where
\overline{t}
is
t_{0}^{\alpha g}
or
t_{0}^{g}
.
∎

We can now deduce part (i) of
Theorem

4.5.2
.

From
Lemma

4.5.9
and
4.5.4
, we have that there are at most four conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and from
4.5.7
we deduce there are exactly two. From
Lemma

4.5.8
we have that for each copy of
29\rtimes 14
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
we obtain two embeddings, thus we have four conjugacy classes of embeddings in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
. Since the characteristic of
k
does not divide
\lvert H\rvert
, we can use
Theorem

2.9.11
to generalise this result to characteristic 0 or coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, namely
p\neq 2,3,5,7,29
.
Proposition

4.5.6
shows that
N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
. Lie primitivity is proved in
[
44
, Corollary 3]
.

In order to complete part (ii), we need to understand what happens when going down to the finite group.

Lemma 4.5.10
.

If
B
embeds in
\mathrm{G}
, there are two conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to
B
in
\mathrm{G}
.

If
H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}
, there are four conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to
H
in
\mathrm{G}
.

Proof.

Recall from
Lemma

4.5.8
that
u
is regular in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, so
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(u)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}
and
s\in N_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}})
; furthermore, there is a single conjugacy class of elements of order 14 in
W(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})
. Then,

C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B)=C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(u)\cap C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}\cap C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)=C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s).

In
Lemma

4.5.8
we checked that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
is finite, and using
Lemma

3.3.3
we can compute
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)
and show that
\lvert C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}}(s)\rvert=2
.

Then by
Corollary

2.9.9
there are two
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
classes of subgroups isomorphic to
B
when
B<\mathrm{G}
.

Furthermore,
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(L)\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(B)
so each
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}\text{-conjugacy}
class of subgroups isomorphic to
H
splits into two
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
classes, one for each
\mathrm{G}\text{-conjugacy}
class of the
B
subgroup.
∎

Remark
.

Observe that with
4.5.4
we can only construct one of the two classes of subgroups of
\mathrm{G}
isomorphic to
B
. Indeed, given
s\in N_{\mathrm{G}(q)}(\mathrm{T})
a representative of an element of
W_{\mathrm{E}_{7}}
of order 14, we have that not all elements of
\mathrm{T}s
are diagonalisable in
\GL_{133}(q)
, some require a
q^{2}
extension, and those would be generators of elements of the other class. The two conjugacy classes of
B
in
\mathrm{G}(q)
are
\mathrm{G}(q^{2})\text{-conjugate}
, and a new conjugacy class of subgroups isomorphic to
B
arises in
\mathrm{G}(q^{2})
.

Remark
.

Like for
Lemma

4.4.10
, for small fields we can check computationally that there are two conjugacy classes of subgroups of
\mathrm{G}
isomorphic to
B
, using the same algorithm.

Proposition 4.5.11
.

H
embeds in
\mathrm{G}(q)
in characteristic not dividing
\lvert H\rvert
when
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a splitting field for
f_{1}(x)=X^{2}-X-1
and
f_{2}(X)=X^{2}-X-7
.

Proof.

We need to understand what is the action of outer automorphisms of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
on
H
; since we are in odd characteristic, we need to consider both the field and the diagonal automorphism.

The action of the diagonal automorphism is clear from
Proposition

4.5.6
: as it cannot normalise
H
, it must swap two classes. Therefore, when taking
\mathrm{G}
to be simple, we obtain twice as many classes.

Now take the Frobenius morphism
F_{p}
; since there are two conjugacy classes of
H
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, then
F_{p}
can either normalise
H
or fuse classes. As
\lvert\out H\rvert=2
, certainly
F_{p^{2}}
centralises
H
, up to conjugacy, so
H<\mathrm{G}(p^{2})
.

Observe from
Table

4.4
that the behaviour of
F_{p}
depends on the irrationalities in the character values, i.e. whether
f_{1}
splits over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
(equiv.
p
is a square modulo 5) and whether
f_{2}
does (equiv.
p
is a square modulo 29):

(i)

If both
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
split, then all the irrationalities are realised in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and
H<\mathrm{G}(p)
.

(ii)

If
f_{1}
splits but
f_{2}
does not, let
g\in H
be an element of order 29 and
\chi
a character in
Table

4.4
, then
F_{p}
sends
\chi(g)
to
\chi(g^{2})
, in particular it cannot centralise
H
. Therefore
F_{p}
acts as the outer automorphism of
H
, which swaps the 15-dimensional modules, so
H<\mathrm{G}(p^{2})
and
H.2<\mathrm{G}(p^{2}).2
.

(iii)

If
f_{1}
does not split, then the character values of elements of order 5 do not lie in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Since character values of such elements are determined only by
28\text{-dimensional}
modules for
H
, and those are not swapped by the outer automorphism of
H
, then
F_{p}
swaps the two conjugacy classes of
H
. Therefore, regardless of whether
f_{2}
splits over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
we have that
H<\mathrm{G}(p^{2})
and
H.2\not<\mathrm{G}(p^{2}).2
.∎

Proposition 4.5.12
.

H
is a maximal subgroup of
\mathrm{G}(q)
,
q
coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
, iff
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a minimal splitting field of
f_{1}(X)=X^{2}-X-1
and
f_{2}(X)=X^{2}-X-7
.

Proof.

Since
H
is primitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
, and
N_{\mathrm{G}}(H)=H
, as observed in
Lemma

2.10.5
we only need to verify that
H
does not lie in any other candidate maximal subgroup.

By
Proposition

4.5.11
we have that
H<\mathrm{G}(q)
if
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a splitting field of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, so if
\mathbb{F}_{q}
was not minimal among such fields then
H
would not be maximal in
\mathrm{G}
as it would lie in a group with the same type as
\mathrm{G}
defined over some subfield of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

Furthermore,
H
cannot lie in an almost simple group
M<\mathrm{G}
whose socle
K
is a primitive simple subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}
not isomorphic to
H
. The list of possible choices for
K
is contained in
[
44
, Table 1.2-1.3]
; for
p\neq 2,3,19,37
they are:
\PSL_{2}(r)
for
r=5,7,9,11,13,19,27,37
,
\PSL_{3}(4)
,
\PSU_{3}(3)
,
\PSU_{3}(8)
,
\Omega_{8}^{+}(2)
.

We can find their order using the Atlas
[
21
]
or
Magma
, and it is straightforward to check that none of them has elements of order 29, hence
H
cannot be a subgroup of
M
in any of such cases.
∎

Theorem

4.5.2
(iii) then follows from
Proposition

4.5.12
in characteristic coprime to
\lvert H\rvert
: if
q=p
then
N_{\overline{G}}(H)=H
is maximal in
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
, while if
q=p^{2}
then either
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}
and
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
, or
\overline{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{G}.2
and as observed in the proof of
Proposition

4.5.11
we have that either
X^{2}-X-1
split and
X^{2}-X-7
does not, which gives
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H.2
, or
X^{2}-X-1
does not split, in which case
N_{\overline{\mathrm{G}}}(H)=H
.

Remark
.

We cannot draw any conclusion for the primes dividing
\lvert H\rvert
. Theoretically, we could work in characteristic 5, as the prime is not involved at any point in the construction (unlike for example
p=7
or 29); however, the required field size would be too large to perform the computations, in particular
4.5.4
.

Chapter 5
Imprimitive subgroups

Another subgroup that is a candidate for being primitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
is
\alt_{6}
, which belongs to the type of group we are trying to study thanks to the isomorphism
\alt_{6}\simeq\PSL_{2}(9)
.

However, it is not possible to replicate the construction that we used in
Chapter

4
to obtain a copy of
\alt_{6}
lying in
\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
, because
\alt_{6}
is “too small”. Using the same notation as in
Section

3.4.3
, that is because the centraliser of
s
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{3}
which is too large – recall that in
Chapter

4

C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
was either a maximal torus or a product of a torus and a
\mathrm{A}_{1}
group; this has the dual effect of making the linear span of
C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)
too large and its fixed point space
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}})^{C_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}}(s)}
too small, so we cannot obtain enough linearly independent equations from the relation
(tu)^{3}=1
, so the resulting space of solutions
M_{0}
is too large to compute candidate elements
t
such that
\langle u,s,t\rangle\simeq\PSL_{2}(9)
.

We will first see a different approach that allows us to construct copies of
\alt_{6}
in
\mathrm{F}_{4}(19)
, that works for any finite field for which certain
3.\alt_{6}\text{-modules}
exist, but it is not enough to deduce how many conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to
\alt_{6}
there are in the algebraic group.

Then, we will completely change the approach and exploit the trilinear form described in
Definition

2.11.9
. We deduce that if
\alt_{6}
embeds in a group of type
\mathrm{F}_{4}
with module structure
8\oplus 9\oplus 9
, or in a group of type
\mathrm{E}_{6}
with module structure
5\oplus 5^{\prime}\oplus 8\oplus 9
, then it is imprimitive as it is contained in a subgroup of type
\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}
or
\mathrm{C}_{4}
, respectively, and proceed to show it is strongly imprimitive.

5.1
Constructing copies of
\alt_{6}
in
\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)

The process is divided into two parts: constructing a copy of
\alt_{6}
in
\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
, and finding all other possible
\alt_{6}<\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
containing one of its
\alt_{5}
subgroups. However, we could not determine whether the computed copies
\alt_{6}
are conjugate to each other.

The code we used to perform this construction using
Magma
appears in the supplementary material.

Construction 5.1.1
.

Construction of a copy of
\alt_{6}<\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
.

From
[
21
]
, we have that
3.\!\alt_{6}
has four 3-dimensional complex irreducible representations. In order to perform this construction, we need a field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
of odd characteristic such that all such representations exist; this means that
\mathbb{F}_{q}
has primitive cubic roots of unity, and is a splitting field of
X^{2}-X-1
and
X^{4}+X^{3}+2X^{2}-X+1
.

We can verify with
Magma
that
\mathbb{F}_{19}
is a splitting field for the two polynomials, so it is the one we use.

Remark
.

Although we did not prove it, we suspect that suitable fields would be
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with
q\equiv 1,19\bmod 30
.

It is known that a copy of
\alt_{6}
can be found in an
\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}
subgroup of
\mathrm{F}_{4}
, which can be realised for example as described in
[
18
, Theorem 4.1]
. To construct it, we proceed in the following way:

(i)

There are four
3\text{-dimensional}
irreducible modules for
3.\!\alt_{6}
. Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be two of them such that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are not the dual of each other, and such that
M_{1}\!\!\downarrow_{Z(3.\!\alt_{6})}
and
M_{2}\!\!\downarrow_{Z(3.\!\alt_{6})}
are not isomorphic.

(ii)

Take generators for the action of
3.\!\alt_{6}
on
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, say
a_{1},a_{2}
and
b_{1},b_{2}
, such that
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}
admit a LDU decomposition. Being matrices of rank 3 and determinant 1,
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}
are elements of
\mathrm{A}_{2}(q)
in its standard representation.

(iii)

By performing a LDU decomposition of
a_{i},b_{i}
,
i=1,2
(see
Section

3.5
), obtain a way to write the matrices as a product of unipotent terms and torus elements of an
\mathrm{A}_{2}(q)
group.

(iv)

Locate roots corresponding to an
\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}
subroot system of
\mathrm{F}_{4}
. An example of it is given in
Corollary

2.3.13
, which is the one we will use. In particular,
\left\{-\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\right\}
and
\left\{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}\right\}
are bases of the two
\mathrm{A}_{2}
subroot systems.

(v)

Let
a_{1}=l_{a_{1}}d_{a_{1}}u_{a_{1}}
be an LDU decomposition of
a_{1}
, corresponding to an element

a_{1}^{*}=l^{*}_{a_{1}}d^{*}_{a_{1}}u^{*}_{a_{1}}=x_{r_{1}}(t^{a_{1}}_{1})x_{r_{2}}(t^{a_{1}}_{2})x_{r_{3}}(t^{a_{1}}_{3})h_{r_{1}}(t^{a_{1}}_{11})h_{r_{2}}(t^{a_{1}}_{22})x_{-r_{1}}(t^{a_{1}}_{-1})x_{-r_{2}}(t^{a_{1}}_{-2})x_{-r_{3}}(t^{a_{1}}_{-3}),

of
\mathrm{A}_{2}(q)
, where
\left\{r_{1},r_{2}\right\}
is a base of an
\mathrm{A}_{2}
root system, and all the
t^{a_{1}}_{i}
lie in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. The same holds for
a_{2}^{*},b_{1}^{*},b_{2}^{*}\in\mathrm{A}_{2}(q)
.

(vi)

Write
a_{1}^{*},a_{2}^{*}
as elements of the
\mathrm{A}_{2}(q)
subgroup of
\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
relative to the
\mathrm{A}_{2}
root subsystem of
\mathrm{F}_{4}
generated by
\left\{-\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\right\}
; for example,

a_{1}^{*}=x_{-\alpha_{0}}(t_{1}^{a_{1}})x_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{2}^{a_{1}})x_{\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{0}}(t_{3}^{a_{1}})h_{-\alpha_{0}}(t_{11}^{a_{1}})h_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{22}^{a_{1}})x_{\alpha_{0}}(t_{-1}^{a_{1}})x_{-\alpha_{1}}(t_{-2}^{a_{1}})x_{\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}}(t_{-3}^{a_{1}}).

In a similar fashion, we can write
b_{1}^{*}
and
b_{2}^{*}
as elements of the
\mathrm{A}_{2}(q)
subgroup of
\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
relative to the
\mathrm{A}_{2}
root subsystem of
\mathrm{F}_{4}
generated by
\left\{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}\right\}
.

(vii)

Let
g_{i}^{*}=a_{i}^{*}b_{i}^{*}
, for
i=1,2
. Then we have that
\langle g_{1}^{*},g_{2}^{*}\rangle
is a group isomorphic to
\alt_{6}
contained in an
\mathrm{A}_{2}(q)\mathrm{A}_{2}(q)
subgroup of
\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
. We will work with the adjoint representation of
\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
, so that the corresponding elements
g_{1},g_{2}
are matrices in
\GL_{52}(q)
.

Observe that we obtain
\alt_{6}
instead of
3.\!\alt_{6}
because of the choice in (i), which makes
\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle
have trivial centre.

Construction 5.1.2
.

Construction of all the copies of
\alt_{5}<\alt_{6}<\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
for a given
\alt_{5}<\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
.

Now that we have a copy
\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle
of
\alt_{6}
in
\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)\leq\GL_{52}(q)
, call
a=(1,2)(3,4)
,
b=(1,2)(3,5)
,
d=(1,2,3)
,
y=(1,2)(5,6)
; our goal is to find all the possible
x=(1,2)(5,6^{\prime})\in\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)
that extend
\alt_{5}\simeq\langle a,b,d\rangle
to an
\alt_{6}
.

Observe that
\langle a,a^{d}\rangle\simeq V_{4}
is toral by
Lemma

2.8.2
, and
xy\in C_{\mathrm{F}_{4}}(a,d)
, so that
x\in C_{\mathrm{F}_{4}}(a,d)y
.

Since the
V_{4}
subgroup is toral, we can simultaneously diagonalise both its generators to deduce its centraliser in
\mathrm{F}_{4}
. As previously mentioned, we could try doing the same with
d
, and then intersect the linear spans of the two centralisers, but that would not be enough to obtain information about the algebraic groups.

However, we can at least study what happens in the finite group, by taking generators of
C_{\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)}(V_{4})
, computing
C_{C_{\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)}(V_{4})}(d)=C_{\mathrm{F}_{4}(q)}(a,d)
, multiplying it by
y
and searching the resulting coset to obtain all the possible
x
that extend
\alt_{5}
to
\alt_{6}
.

We obtained two choices for the involution, say
x
and
y
(clearly, one of them must be the
y
we started with), hence two copies of
\alt_{6}
containing the same
\alt_{5}
.

Unfortunately, we did not find a way to check whether they are conjugate to each other.

Observe that with
Magma
we could write
x
as an element of the same
\mathrm{A}_{2}(19)\mathrm{A}_{2}(19)
group where
g_{1},g_{2}
were constructed, suggesting that both
\langle a,b,d,y\rangle
and
\langle a,b,d,x\rangle
are not maximal subgroups of
\mathrm{F}_{4}(19)
. This is further corroborated by comparing the distribution of the order of the elements of
\langle a,b,d,x,y\rangle
with those of an
\mathrm{A}_{2}(19)\mathrm{A}_{2}(19)
subgroup of
\mathrm{F}_{4}(19)
.

Indeed, we will prove in
Section

5.2
that
\alt_{6}
cannot be a maximal subgroup of
\mathrm{F}_{4}
.

5.2
Embedding of
\alt_{6}
in
\mathrm{F}_{4}

In this section, we will prove the following:

Theorem 5.2.1
.

\alt_{6}
is strongly imprimitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3
.

Most possible embeddings of
\alt_{6}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
are ruled out by existing results:

Lemma 5.2.2
.

If
\alt_{6}
embeds in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3
, then if it acts on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
as
8\oplus 9^{2}
, and on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
as
8_{1}^{\phantom{3}}\oplus 8_{2}^{3}\oplus 10^{2}
, it may be primitive; otherwise, it is strongly imprimitive.

Proof.

This follows directly from
[
44
, Table 6.5 and 6.6]
.
∎

Remark
.

Observe that from
[
44
, Table 6.6]
it looks like
\alt_{6}
may embed primitively in characteristic 5 if it acts as
8^{4}\oplus 10^{2}
on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
, but this is likely a typo as the action on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
stabilises a line. This is confirmed by
[
44
, Proposition 4.1]
, where it is established that
\alt_{6}
is always strongly imprimitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
in characteristic 5.

An important role in the proof is played by the
\alt_{5}
subgroup(s), because of the following:

Lemma 5.2.3
.

In
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}(\mathbb{C})
there is a single conjugacy class
\mathcal{C}
of groups isomorphic to
\alt_{5}
such that for any
H<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
with
H\simeq\alt_{6}
acting on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
as
8\oplus 9^{2}
, any
\alt_{5}\simeq K<H
lies in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

We can verify with
Magma
that if
\alt_{6}
acts as
8\oplus 9^{2}
, then an
\alt_{5}
subgroup must act as
3\oplus 4^{2}\oplus 5^{3}
. By
[
25
, Table 4.6]
and
[
25
, Theorem 4.17]
we have that there is a single conjugacy class in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
(and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
) of
\alt_{5}
subgroups with the said action.
∎

Remark
.

We can verify this behaviour in the groups constructed in
Section

5.1
, by computing the multiplicities of the eigenvalues of the elements of the
\alt_{5}
subgroups of the constructed
\alt_{6}
, and using
[
25
, Table 1.2 and 4.4]
.

This means that all of the possibly primitive
\alt_{6}
contain the same
\alt_{5}
subgroup, up to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}\text{-}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}\text{-conjugacy}
.

By
[
43
, Theorem 1]
there is a unique
\aut\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}\text{-conjugacy}
class of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
, and a similar result holds for finite
\mathrm{F}_{4}
and
\mathrm{E}_{6}
(see
[
43
, Corollary 5]
). In particular,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
is a line stabiliser on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
, i.e.
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})\!\!\downarrow_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}}
decomposes as
1\oplus 26
; see for example
[
43
, Table 10.1]
. We can study
\alt_{6}
inside
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
; this allows us to exploit the 3-form described in
Definition

2.11.9
, which is unique and whose isometry group is
\mathrm{E}_{6}
(
Theorem

2.11.10
), to obtain information about an
\alt_{6}
group containing an
\alt_{5}
subgroup acting on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
as
1\oplus 3\oplus 4^{2}\oplus 5^{3}
, and on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
as
1\oplus 3\oplus 4^{2}\oplus 5^{3}
.

In particular, we have the following chain of subgroups and corresponding modules:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}

\displaystyle 27

(5.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}

\displaystyle 9_{S}\oplus 9_{U}\oplus 9_{W}

\displaystyle\alt_{6}

\displaystyle 1_{S}\oplus 8_{S}\oplus 9_{U}\oplus 9_{W}

\displaystyle\alt_{5}

\displaystyle 1_{S}\oplus 3_{S}\oplus 5_{S}\oplus 4_{U}\oplus 5_{U}\oplus 4_{W}\oplus 5_{W},

where the labels
S,U,W
are used to distinguish the modules and their restrictions.

The restriction to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
is described in
[
59
, Theorem 3.1]
, and is a 3-decomposition in the sense of
Definition

2.11.11
by
Lemma

2.11.12
. It is known that an
\alt_{6}
subgroup with the required action arises as the group acting on the module for a
3.\!\alt_{6}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
, see for example
[
18
,
\nopp
6.13]
, which gives the restriction to
\alt_{6}
. As mentioned in
Lemma

5.2.3
, the restriction to
\alt_{5}
can be easily verified.

Since
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})\!\!\downarrow_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}}
is a 3-decomposition, and since there is a unique conjugacy class of
\alt_{5}
subgroups of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
with the given action on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
, then
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})\!\!\downarrow_{\alt_{5}}
is also a 3-decomposition, by taking the three 9-dimensional submodules
1_{S}\oplus 3_{S}\oplus 5_{S}
,
4_{U}\oplus 5_{U}
, and
4_{W}\oplus 5_{W}
.

Construction 5.2.4
.

Construction of
9_{S}\oplus 9_{U}\oplus 9_{W}
over a finite field.

We can construct a copy of
M(\mathrm{E}_{6}(q))\!\!\downarrow_{\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}}
as described for example in
[
57
, Theorem 3.1]
or in
[
18
, Theorem 4.1]
. This results in a direct sum of three 9-dimensional modules, and as expected it is a 3-decomposition, which we can verify using
Lemma

2.11.16
by computing that
\hom_{\mathbb{F}_{q}\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}}(M,\sym^{2}(N^{*}))
is zero for any pair
M,N
of distinct modules. Furthermore, we can also construct a copy of
3.\alt_{6}<\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}
and identify
9_{U}
and
9_{W}
by verifying that two said modules are irreducible, while the remaining one is
9_{S}
and decomposes as
1\oplus 8
.

While it is clear that the restriction of
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
to an
\alt_{6}
subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
is a 3-decomposition, we cannot say the same for any
\alt_{6}
subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
with the required action on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
, as they may not all arise in the same way.

Our goal is to prove the following:

Proposition 5.2.5
.

Let
\alt_{5}\simeq K<\mathrm{G}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
act on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
as a 3-decomposition
1\oplus 3\oplus 4^{2}\oplus 5^{3}
as described above. Then for any
K<H\simeq\alt_{6}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
,
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})\!\!\downarrow_{H}
is also a 3-decomposition.

Proof.

We denote by
f
the trilinear form described in
Definition

2.11.9
.

By definition of 3-decomposition, we must show that
9_{U}\subseteq(1_{S}\oplus 8_{S})\Theta
and
1_{S},8_{S},9_{U}\subseteq 9_{W}\Theta
; the modules
9_{U}
and
9_{W}
are indistinguishable as
\alt_{6}\text{-modules}
, so the same holds when interchanging
U
and
W
.

By
Lemma

2.11.16
,
M\subseteq N\Theta
is equivalent to
\hom_{kG}(M,\sym^{2}(N^{*}))=0
. Since we do not have a copy of
\alt_{6}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
, we cannot simply construct
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})\!\!\downarrow_{\alt_{6}}
. Instead, we study the restriction to
\alt_{5}
of
\alt_{6}\text{-modules}
, that is we compare
\hom_{k\alt_{6}}(M,\sym^{2}(N^{*}))
and
\hom_{k\alt_{5}}(M\!\!\downarrow_{\alt_{5}},\sym^{2}(N^{*}\!\!\downarrow_{\alt_{5}}))
as explained in
Section

3.8
.

We choose to work over the field
k=\mathbb{F}_{11}
as it is the smallest for which we can construct the required modules. Since
k
has coprime order with
\lvert\alt_{6}\rvert
, we can then deduce that the result can be lifted to characteristic coprime or zero.

Let
H\simeq\alt_{6}
and
\alt_{5}\simeq K<H
, we can either use the groups obtained in
5.2.4
or construct them as abstract groups. Either way, will use the notation of (
5.1
) to denote the modules we are working with, for clarity.

All the following computations are done in
Magma
.

We start with proving that
1_{S}\subset 9_{W}\Theta
, i.e.
\hom_{kH}(1_{S},\sym^{2}(9_{W}^{*}))=0
, or equivalently
f(1_{S},9_{W},9_{W})=0
by
Lemma

2.11.16
.

We compute
A\coloneqq\hom_{kH}(1_{S},\sym^{2}(9_{W}^{*}))
, which is 1-dimensional and restricts to a linear combination of
B\coloneqq\hom_{kK}(1_{S},\sym^{2}(4_{W}^{*}))
and
C\coloneqq\hom_{kK}(1_{S},\sym^{2}(5_{W}^{*}))
, which are both 1-dimensional as well. Let
b,c
be a generator of
B,C
, respectively; then for all
a\in A
,
a\!\!\downarrow_{K}=a_{b}b+a_{c}c
for some
a_{b},a_{c}\in k
.

If we have
K<H<\mathrm{E}_{6}
such that
M(\mathrm{E}_{6})\!\!\downarrow_{K}
is a 3-decomposition, then
f(1_{S},5_{W},5_{W})
and
f(1_{S},4_{W},4_{W})
are both zero. If
a\neq 0
, then
a\!\!\downarrow_{K}\neq 0
as well, i.e. at least one of
a_{b},a_{c}
is nonzero. However,
a=f\!\!\downarrow_{H}
and
a\!\!\downarrow_{K}=f\!\!\downarrow_{K}
, so if
a\!\!\downarrow_{K}\neq 0
then at least one of
f(1_{S},5_{W},5_{W})
and
f(1_{S},4_{W},4_{W})
would not be zero; therefore,
a
must be the trivial linear combination of
b
and
c
, i.e.
f(1_{S},9_{W},9_{W})=0
.

We can compute that both
\hom_{kH}(8_{S},\sym^{2}(9_{W}^{*}))
and
\hom_{kH}(9_{U},\sym^{2}((1_{S}\oplus 8_{S})^{*}))
are 1-dimensional, so we can deduce that
8_{S}\subset 9_{W}\Theta
and
9_{U}\subset(1_{S}\oplus 8_{S})\Theta
in the same fashion.

The only remaining case is
9_{U}\subset 9_{W}\Theta
, for which we compute that
A\coloneqq\hom_{kH}(9_{U},\sym^{2}(9_{W}^{*}))
is 2-dimensional. Therefore it could happen that given two generators
a_{1},a_{2}
of
A
, some linear combination
a=\alpha_{1}a_{1}+\alpha_{2}a_{2}
of them is such that
a\!\!\downarrow_{K}=0
, while still having
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\neq 0
. This is not the case, as we can find
a_{1},a_{2}
such that their restrictions are
a_{i}\!\!\downarrow_{K}=\sum_{j}\beta_{i,j}b_{i,j}
where the
\beta_{i,j}
are nonzero and the
b_{i,j}
lie in a set of generators for, respectively:

(i)

\hom_{kK}(4_{U},4_{W}^{*}\otimes 5_{W}^{*})
,
\hom_{kK}(4_{U},\sym^{2}(5_{W}^{*}))
,
\hom_{kK}(5_{U},\sym^{2}(4_{W}^{*}))
, which are 1-dimensional, and
\hom_{kK}(5_{U},4_{W}^{*}\otimes 5_{W}^{*})
,
\hom_{kK}(5_{U},\sym^{2}(5_{W}^{*}))
, which are 2-dimensional.

(ii)

\hom_{kK}(4_{U},\sym^{2}(4_{W}^{*}))
,
\hom_{kK}(4_{U},\sym^{2}(5_{W}^{*}))
,
\hom_{kK}(5_{U},\sym^{2}(4_{W}^{*}))
, which are 1-dimensional, and
\hom_{kK}(5_{U},4_{W}^{*}\otimes 5_{W}^{*})
,
\hom_{kK}(5_{U},\sym^{2}(5_{W}^{*}))
, which are 2-dimensional.

If we have that
K<H<\mathrm{E}_{6}
is such that
M(\mathrm{E}_{6})\!\!\downarrow_{K}
is a 3-decomposition, then
f(4_{U},4_{W},5_{W})=0
. Observe that the first component of (i) does not appear in (ii); therefore, in order to have
a\!\!\downarrow_{K}=f\!\!\downarrow_{K}=0
we must have
\alpha_{1}=0
.

The same argument can be used to obtain
\alpha_{2}=0
, since the first component of (ii) does not appear in (i).

Since the analysis we have done does not depend on the choice of the field, then the module
(1_{S}\oplus 8_{S})\oplus 9_{U}\oplus 9_{W}
is a 3-decomposition, as long as it exists.

This means that we must be in characteristic 0 or coprime to
\lvert\alt_{6}\rvert
, i.e.
p\neq 2,3,5
; for a construction over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, from the character table of
\alt_{6}

[
21
]
we deduce that we also need 5th roots of unity, i.e.
q\equiv\pm 1\bmod 5
(see
Lemma

2.9.10
).
∎

Therefore, in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,5
, if
\alt_{6}
acts as
1\oplus 8\oplus 9^{2}
on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
then it stabilises a 3-decomposition hence it is contained in a stabiliser of a 3-decomposition, which is a positive-dimensional subgroup of type
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
(
Lemma

2.11.12
).

From
[
58
, Table 3]
we have that there are two classes of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
subgroups of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
, one of which stabilises a line on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
, i.e. acts as
1\oplus 8\oplus 9^{2}
, and contains an
\alt_{6}
subgroup with the same action.

Remark
.

Depending on which pair of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
factors we consider, the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
subgroup stabilises a line lying on a different composition factor of
9_{S}\oplus 9_{U}\oplus 9_{W}
.

Furthermore, as observed in
[
58
,
\nopp
7.4]
, the maximal connected subgroup
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
is conjugate in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
to the aforementioned
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
; in particular,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
stabilise the same line on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
. Therefore, any
\alt_{6}
subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
acting on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
as
1\oplus 8\oplus 9^{2}
lies in an
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
, hence it is imprimitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
.

In order to complete the proof of
Theorem

5.2.1
, we need to show that such an
\alt_{6}
is strongly imprimitive. We can use
[
24
, Theorem 4.3]
: using the notation of (
5.1
), let
8_{S}
be the 8-dimensional subspace of
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4})
stabilised by
\alt_{6}
. The stabiliser
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
of
8_{S}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
is clearly not the whole
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
, and it contains the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
containing
\alt_{6}
. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{X}}
is positive dimensional and
\alt_{6}
is strongly imprimitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
.

5.3
Embedding of
\alt_{6}
in
\mathrm{E}_{6}

We will prove the following:

Theorem 5.3.1
.

\alt_{6}
is strongly imprimitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,5
.

Similarly to
Section

5.2
, we will study this case using the uniqueness of the 3-form described in
Definition

2.11.9
, and the fact that the
\alt_{5}
subgroup of the
\alt_{6}
we are interested in is unique up to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}\text{-conjugacy}
.

Lemma 5.3.2
.

If
\alt_{6}
embeds in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,5
, then if it acts on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
as
5\oplus 5^{\prime}\oplus 8\oplus 9
, and on
L(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
as
8^{2}\oplus 8^{\prime 3}\oplus 9^{2}\oplus 10^{2}
, it may be primitive; otherwise, it is strongly imprimitive.

Proof.

This follows directly from
[
44
, Table 6.50]
.
∎

Lemma 5.3.3
.

In
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}(\mathbb{C})
there is a single conjugacy class
\mathcal{C}
of groups isomorphic to
\alt_{5}
such that for any
H<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
with
H\simeq\alt_{6}
acting on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
as
5\oplus 5^{\prime}\oplus 8\oplus 9
, any
\alt_{5}\simeq K<H
lies in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

We can verify with
Magma
that if
\alt_{6}
acts as
5\oplus 5^{\prime}\oplus 8\oplus 9
, then an
\alt_{5}
subgroup must act as
3\oplus 4^{2}\oplus 5^{3}
, so the result follows from
[
25
]
using the same argument as in
Lemma

5.2.3
.
∎

We stress the fact that the modules called 5 and
5^{\prime}
in
Lemma

5.3.2
are not isomorphic. Recall that
\alt_{6}
has two classes of subgroups isomorphic to
\alt_{5}
: one class fixes a point on 5 while
5^{\prime}
remains irreducible, the other class fixes a point on
5^{\prime}
while 5 remains irreducible.

By
[
43
, Theorem 1]
there is a unique
\aut\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}\text{-conjugacy}
class of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
, and a similar result holds for finite
\mathrm{C}_{4}
and
\mathrm{E}_{6}
(see
[
43
, Corollary 5]
; in particular,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
acts irreducibly on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
(see for example
[
43
, Table 10.2]
).

It is known that an
\alt_{6}
with the required action arises as a subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
, see for example
[
18
,
\nopp
6.14]
, and such an
\alt_{6}
contains
\alt_{5}
subgroups with the action described in the proof of
Lemma

5.3.3
.

Therefore, we have the following chain of subgroups and corresponding actions:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}

\displaystyle 27

(5.2)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}

\displaystyle 27

\displaystyle\alt_{6}

\displaystyle 5_{A}\oplus 5_{B}\oplus 8_{C}\oplus 9_{D}

\displaystyle\alt_{5}

\displaystyle 1_{A}\oplus 4_{A}\oplus 5_{B}\oplus 3_{C}\oplus 5_{C}\oplus 4_{D}\oplus 5_{D},

where the labels
A,B,C,D
are used to distinguish the modules and their restrictions.

Therefore, the task is to check whether there are other
\alt_{6}
that contain such
\alt_{5}
but do not lie inside
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
.

Note that in
Section

5.2
we could exploit the action of the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
overgroup to obtain a criterion for
\alt_{6}
not to be maximal (stabilising a 3-decomposition); this time the action of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
is irreducible, so we will use a different approach.

Similarly to
Section

5.2
, we will work using the field
k=\mathbb{F}_{11}
, as it is the smallest field such that all the required modules exist, and has characteristic coprime to
\lvert\alt_{6}\rvert
.

We start by taking a copy
\mathrm{G}
of
\Sp_{8}(11)<\GL_{8}(11)
, a
2.\!\alt_{6}
maximal subgroup
H
of
\mathrm{G}
, and a
2.\!\alt_{5}
subgroup
K
of
H
;
Magma
has a function that returns a representative of each conjugacy class of maximal subgroups of classical Lie groups in small dimension, which we use to get
H
.

We construct the 27-dimensional module
V
for
\mathrm{G}
by taking the exterior square of the natural module for
\Sp_{8}(11)
and then taking the quotient over the centre, as described for example in
[
18
, Theorem 4.1]
.

We can compute that the fixed point space of
\sym^{3}(V)
is 1-dimensional: this is equivalent to say that it has a unique
\mathrm{G}\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear form, up to scalars, which implies it must be inherited from the unique trilinear form of
\mathrm{E}_{6}
. Therefore, we can work with a
\mathrm{G}\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear form
f
.

We proceed with the construction of
f
as described in
Section

3.7
. In this case, since we are working with a group
\mathrm{G}
of Lie type, we constructed a set of generators of a maximal torus of
\mathrm{G}
to obtain all possible diagonal elements of
\mathrm{G}
, and we used them to reduce the complexity of the problem from
\binom{27+2}{3}=3564
to just 78 structure constants that could be non-zero.

Equipped with the trilinear form
f
, we can now study
V\!\!\downarrow_{K}
, which decomposes as (
5.2
).

1_{A}
and
3_{C}
can be identified immediately as they are the unique submodules of the respective dimension.

Observe now that since
\alt_{5}
is unique up to conjugacy in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
, then from
Section

5.2
it stabilises not only
V\!\!\downarrow_{K}=1_{A}\oplus 4_{A}\oplus 5_{B}\oplus 3_{C}\oplus 5_{C}\oplus 4_{D}\oplus 5_{D}
, but also
1_{S}\oplus 3_{S}\oplus 5_{S}\oplus 4_{U}\oplus 5_{U}\oplus 4_{W}\oplus 5_{W}
(from (
5.1
)), where
1_{S}=1_{A}
,
3_{S}=3_{C}
,
4_{A}+4_{D}=4_{U}+4_{W}
,
5_{A}+5_{C}+5_{D}=5_{S}+5_{U}+5_{W}
, and
(1_{S}\oplus 3_{S}\oplus 5_{S})\oplus(4_{U}\oplus 5_{U})\oplus(4_{W}\oplus 5_{W})
is a 3-decomposition, i.e.
9_{S}\Theta=9_{U}\oplus 9_{W}
,
9_{U}\Theta=9_{S}\oplus 9_{W}
, and
9_{W}\Theta=9_{S}\oplus 9_{U}
, therefore we can use
Lemma

2.11.16
to identify the various summands.

Coming from
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
and not from
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{2}
, we do not already know
4_{U}
and
4_{W}
; however, we can use the
\Theta
relations to do so.

For example, we can use the fact that
1_{A}\subset 4_{U}\Theta
and
1_{A}\subset 4_{W}\Theta
, so we can take the
q+1=12
4-dimensional irreducible submodules of
V\!\!\downarrow_{K}
, i.e. all non-trivial proper submodules of
4_{A}+4_{D}
, and find those such that
f(1,4,4)=0
. Since this results in only two possibilities, they must be
4_{U}
and
4_{W}
.

Likewise, we can also take the
q^{2}+q+1=133
5-dimensional submodules of
5_{A}+5_{B}+5_{C}
, and locate
5_{S},5_{U},5_{W}
:

(i)

5_{S}
is the unique 5-dimensional submodule lying in
4_{U}\Theta\cap 4_{W}\Theta
, i.e. it satisfies
f(5,4_{U},4_{U})=0
and
f(5,4_{W},4_{W})=0
.

(ii)

5_{U}
is the unique 5-dimensional submodule lying in
4_{W}\Theta\cap 5_{S}\Theta
, i.e it satisfies
f(5,4_{W},4_{W})=0
and
f(5,5_{S},5_{S})=0
.

(iii)

5_{W}
is the unique 5-dimensional submodule lying in
4_{U}\Theta\cap 5_{S}\Theta
.

Now we try to find which of the diagonal 4-dimensional modules are possible choices for
4_{A}
; we also want to obtain a result that holds true when taking field extensions, in order to able to say something about
\alt_{6}
as a subgroup of the algebraic group
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
in characteristic 11.

In order to do so, we proceed as follows.

Start by constructing a “reference copy”
H^{*}
of
\alt_{6}
as an abstract group, and an
\alt_{5}
subgroup
K^{*}
of it, together with a 5-dimensional irreducible
H^{*}\text{-module}

M
such that
M\!\!\downarrow_{K^{*}}
decomposes as
1_{M}\oplus 4_{M}
. We can compute that
\sym^{3}(M)
has a 1-dimensional fixed point space, therefore there is a unique
H^{*}\text{-stable}
symmetric trilinear form on
M
up to scalars, and we fix a choice
f^{\prime}
for it. We will use
f^{\prime}
to understand what 4-dimensional modules are suitable to be
4_{A}
.

First we work on the trivial submodule: find
e\in 1_{A}=1_{S}
such that
f(e,e,e)=1
, and
e^{\prime}\in 1_{M}
such that
f^{\prime}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime},e^{\prime})=1
.

Recall that there is a unique irreducible 4-dimensional
\alt_{5}\text{-module}
, so
4_{M}\simeq 4_{U},4_{W}
as an
\alt_{5}\text{-module}
. Let
\alpha
be the composition of an isomorphism
4_{M}\rightarrow 4_{U}
(unique up to scalars) with the embedding of
4_{U}
in
V\!\!\downarrow_{K}
, and let
\beta
be the composition of an isomorphism
4_{M}\rightarrow 4_{W}
with the embedding of
4_{W}
in
V\!\!\downarrow_{K}
. Fix
v\in 4_{M}
and choose
\alpha,\beta
so that
f(e,\alpha(v),\beta(v))=1
.

Therefore, we have that for every choice of
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{11^{r}}
,
\alpha(v)+\lambda\beta(v)
lies in a distinct diagonal
\mathbb{F}_{11}^{r}\alt_{5}\text{-submodule}
of
4_{U}+4_{W}
, call it
D_{\lambda}
. Note that since
4_{U}
is orthogonal to
1_{A}
, it can never be that
4_{A}=4_{U}
, so we do not have to consider that case.

Now we have an isomorphism
\phi_{\lambda}:4_{M}\rightarrow D_{\lambda}
, so in order to make
f^{\prime}
and
f
coincide on
M
and
\phi_{\lambda}(M)=D_{\lambda}
we need to adjust
f^{\prime}
by some constant factor
\mu=\mu(\lambda)
.

The only thing left is to find for which
\lambda,\mu
the two forms coincide everywhere.

One condition is given by matching the forms on
(1,4,4)
, using the
v\in 4_{M}
chosen earlier. We want to have:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(e^{\prime},v,v)

\displaystyle=f(e,\mu(\alpha(v)+\lambda\beta(v)),\mu(\alpha(v)+\lambda\beta(v)))

\displaystyle=\mu^{2}f(e,\alpha(v),\alpha(v))+2\lambda\mu^{2}f(1,\alpha(v),\beta(v))+\lambda^{2}\mu^{2}f(e,\beta(v),\beta(v))

\displaystyle=2\lambda\mu^{2},

where we used that
f(e,\alpha(v),\beta(v))=1
and
1_{A}\subset 4_{U}\Theta,4_{W}\Theta
.
An explicit computation of
f^{\prime}
on
M
gives one of the relations, in our case we got
\lambda\mu^{2}=1
.

Now we need to match the form on
(4,4,4)
. We proceed in a similar way as before, this time taking
f^{\prime}(u,v,w)
where
u,v,w
are (not all equal) elements of
4_{M}
. We checked all possible triples of basis elements for
4_{M}
, and obtained only one independent relation, of the form
\mu^{3}\lambda^{3}-\mu^{3}+1=0
.

By combining the two relations, we obtained
\lambda=\mu^{-2}
, where
\mu^{3}=X
and
X
is a root of
X^{2}-X-1
.

Remark
.

Observe that
X^{2}-X-1
is the minimal polynomial of the non-rational character value appearing in the character table of
\alt_{6}
.

The polynomial
X^{2}-X-1
splits in characteristic 11, meaning that there are two or six possible choices of
(\lambda,\mu)
depending on whether
X=\mu^{3}
has one or three solutions in
\mathbb{F}_{11^{r}}
. In particular, for
r
odd there are two choices, while for
r
even there are six.

Remark
.

(i)

When there are two solutions, they give rise to two
\alt_{6}
groups containing the given
\alt_{5}
that either are conjugate to each other in
\Sp_{8}
or lie in the two different conjugacy classes of
\alt_{6}
in
\Sp_{8}
, depending on the field (see
[
11
, Table 8.49]
). The supplementary material includes the construction of an element of
\Sp_{8}
conjugating the two copies of
\alt_{6}
when working over
\mathbb{F}_{11}
.

(ii)

When there are six solutions, they form three pairs, one for each solution of
X=\mu^{3}
. This is because
(3,11^{2n}-1)=1
, so we have the diagonal automorphism
\delta
of
\mathrm{E}_{6}(q)
permuting the three classes. This is evident from the fact that there is a single conjugacy class of
\alt_{5}
in
\mathrm{E}_{6}
,
\delta
has order 3, and
\out\alt_{6}=2^{2}
, which implies that
\delta
cannot be an automorphism for any of the
\alt_{6}
overgroups. Furthermore, suppose
\delta
centralises at least one of them, then
\alt_{6}
would lie in the centraliser in
\mathrm{E}_{6}
of
\delta
, i.e. a group of type
\mathrm{A}_{5}\mathrm{T}_{1}
,
\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}\mathrm{A}_{2}
, or
\mathrm{D}_{4}\mathrm{T}_{2}
, whose action on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
is
15\oplus 6^{2}
,
9^{3}
, and
9^{3}
respectively (see for example
[
18
, Table 2]
); this is impossible as they cannot afford a subgroup acting as
5^{2}\oplus 8\oplus 9
. Therefore,
\delta
can only permute the three pairs cyclically. Furthermore,
\delta
permutes not only the three pairs, but also the
\mathrm{C}_{4}
overgroups containing each pair.

Since the construction is independent on the field extension we take, this is true for the algebraic group as well, i.e.
\alt_{6}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
in characteristic 11. Since 11 is coprime to
\lvert\alt_{6}\rvert
, we can lift the result to coprime characteristic and characteristic zero, therefore
\alt_{6}
is an imprimitive subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
in such cases.

We can now conclude the proof of
Theorem

5.3.1
by discussing
N_{\mathrm{E}_{6}}(\alt_{6})
, in characteristic
\neq 2,3,5
, and showing that
\alt_{6}
is strongly imprimitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
. Since
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
acts irreducibly on
M(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6})
, we cannot proceed as in
Section

5.2
to do so.

It is clear from the character table of
\alt_{6}

[
21
]
that the only feasible extension lying in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
is
\alt_{6}.2_{2}\simeq\PGL(2,9)
, acting as
(5+5^{\prime})\oplus 8\oplus 9^{-}
, where the parentheses denote that the two characters of
\alt_{6}
of degree 5 are fused in the extension. This is because the other two possible extensions,
2_{1}
and
2_{3}
, fuse the two characters of
\alt_{6}
of degree 8.

Since
\alt_{6}<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
, we can use the results in
[
11
, Table 8.49]
to deduce that
\alt_{6}.2_{2}
is realised in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
in characteristic
p\neq 2,3,5
, hence it embeds in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
.

Recall from
[
43
, Theorem 1]
that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
is a maximal subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
, and it is unique up to
\aut\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}\text{-conjugacy}
. In particular, a field automorphism of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
acts on
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
as the induced field automorphism, while a graph automorphism centralises it; therefore, a field-graph automorphism normalises
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
, hence
\mathrm{C}_{4}<\!\prescript{2}{}{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
.

As
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{C}}_{4}
is
\aut\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}\text{-stable}
,
\alt_{6}
is strongly imprimitive in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}_{6}
.

Remark
.

When looking at finite groups, from the character table of
\alt_{6}

[
21
]
we deduce that it embeds in
\mathrm{E}_{6}(p)
for
p\neq 2,3,5
iff
\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a splitting field for
X^{2}-X-1
, i.e.
p\equiv\pm 1\bmod 5
by
Lemma

2.9.10
. If
p\equiv\pm 2\bmod 5
, it embeds in
\mathrm{E}_{6}(p^{2})
instead.

Furthermore, from
[
11
, Table 8.49]
we have that:

(i)

If
p\equiv\pm 9\bmod 20
then
2.\alt_{6}
is maximal in
\Sp_{8}(p)
, with a diagonal automorphism of
\Sp_{8}(p)
acting as
2_{2}
.

(ii)

If
p\equiv\pm 1\bmod 20
then
2.\alt_{6}.2_{2}
is maximal in
\Sp_{8}(p)
.

(iii)

If
p\equiv\pm 2\bmod 5
then
2.\alt_{6}
is maximal in
\Sp_{8}(p^{2})
, with a diagonal automorphism of
\Sp_{8}(p^{2})
acting as
2_{2}
, and a field automorphism acting as
2_{1}
.

In all cases we have that
\alt_{6}.2_{2}<\mathrm{C}_{4}(p)<\mathrm{E}_{6}(p)
or
\alt_{6}.2_{2}<\mathrm{C}_{4}(p^{2})<\!\prescript{2}{}{\mathrm{E}}_{6}(p^{2})
, depending on the congruence of
p
modulo 5.

Appendix A
Lie structures in
Magma

A.1

Lie algebras

We take a look at how Magma constructs Lie algebras by default. Let
\mathcal{L}
be a Lie algebra of dimension
dim
and rank
rk
, so that the number of positive (resp. negative) roots is
\texttt{nr}\coloneqq(\texttt{dim}-\texttt{rk})/2
. Then the default Chevalley basis of
\mathcal{L}
is stored with the following order:

(e_{-\texttt{nr}},e_{-\texttt{nr}+1},\ldots,e_{-2},e_{-1},h_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,h_{\texttt{rk}}^{\prime},e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{\texttt{nr}-1},e_{\texttt{nr}}),

where
e_{1},\ldots,e_{\texttt{rk}}
form a set of fundamental (positive) roots, while

h_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j=1}^{\texttt{rk}}(C^{-1})_{i,j}[e_{j},e_{-j}],

where
C
is the Cartan matrix of
\mathcal{L}
.

Magma orders the roots by taking positive roots first, then negative roots in the same order, that is
1,\ldots,\texttt{nr},-1,\ldots,-\texttt{nr}
. Therefore, the indexing used in a default Chevalley basis can be converted to the indexing of the roots by the map

\begin{gathered}(1,2,\ldots,\texttt{nr}-1,\texttt{nr},\texttt{nr}+\texttt{rk}+1,\texttt{nr}+\texttt{rk}+2,\ldots,\texttt{dim}-1,\texttt{dim})\\
\updownarrow\\
(2\texttt{nr},2\texttt{nr}-1\ldots,\texttt{nr}+2,\texttt{nr}+1,1,2,\ldots,\texttt{nr}-1,\texttt{nr}).\end{gathered}

The following code generates said map and its inverse:

⬇

m
:=
map
<({1..
nr
}

join

{
nr
+
rk
+1..
dim
})->{1..
dim
-
rk
}|
x
:->
x

le

nr

select

dim
+1-
rk
-
x

else

x
-
nr
-
rk
,
y
:->
y

le

nr

select

nr
+
rk
+
y

else

dim
-
rk
+1-
y
>;

A.2

Groups of Lie type

As for Lie groups, the default construction is with the
GroupOfLieType
function.

Let
\mathrm{G}
be such a group over the field
k
, and let
i
denote a root, ranging from
1
to
2*\texttt{nr}
; its elements are products of:

(i)

Root elements, like
x_{i}(t)
, called with
elt<G|<i,t>>
,
t\in k
.

(ii)

Torus elements, like
h_{i}(t)
, called with
TorusTerm(G,i,t)
,
t\in k^{\times}
;

(iii)

Weyl group representatives, like
n_{i}
, called with
elt<G|i>
.

Torus elements can also be called using
elt<G|v>
, where
v
is vector of
{(k^{\times})}^{\rk\mathrm{G}}
. However, observe that for example
elt<G|Vector([t,1,...,1])>
is not the same element as
TorusTerm(G,1,t)
.

The elements of a group of Lie type can be represented as matrices using the functions
StandardRepresentation
and
AdjointRepresentation
, which map
\mathrm{G}
to
\GL_{n}(k)
for the appropriate
n
.

In the supplementary material, we provide the code for a function that constructs
x_{\pm i}(t)
,
h_{i}(\lambda)
,
n_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,\rk\mathrm{G}
, in the adjoint representation, for an arbitrary Chevalley basis of the Lie algebra of
\mathrm{G}
.

We use the actions described in (
2.4
), (
2.7
), (
2.8
), for
x_{\pm i}(t)
,
h_{i}(\lambda)
,
n_{i}
, respectively, by going through all vectors of the basis, and computing their image under said actions.

We can check that using the default basis for a Lie algebra we obtain the same elements as the built-in constructors in Magma. For example:

⬇

>

load

"GHN.mg"
;

>

G
:=
GroupOfLieType
(
"F4"
,13);

>

L
:=
LieAlgebra
(
G
);

>

ad
:=
AdjointRepresentation
(
G
);

>

x
,
h
,
n
:=
GHN
(
L
,[1..4],
Basis
(
L
));

>

xx
:=[
ad
(
elt
<
G
|<
i
,1>>):
i

in

[1..4]

cat

[25..28]];

>

hh
:=[
ad
(
TorusTerm
(
G
,
i
,2)):
i

in

[1..4]];

>

nn
:=[
ad
(
elt
<
G
|
i
>):
i

in

[1..4]];

>

print

xx

eq

x
,

hh

eq

h
,

nn

eq

n
;

true

true

true

Appendix B
Supplementary material

We include the code used to perform all the computations previously presented. All the files are in plain text format, and can be opened with any text editing software; they come with two extensions:

(i)

Files with a
.mg
extension are meant to be run with
Magma
.

(ii)

Files with a
.bash
extension are Bash scripts that automatically load and run other files with
Magma
; they are used when we prefer to parallelise certain computations instead of running a single instance of
Magma
.

All the files include comments that outline what the corresponding
Magma
code or Bash script does.

All the
.mg
files and all other
.mg
files produced by Bash scripts were run on
Magma
V2.25-4.

The files that directly perform the computations are the following:

(i)

25_F4.mg
constructs copies of
\PSL_{2}(25)<\mathrm{F}_{4}(61)
.

(ii)

25_F4_var.mg
does the same but showcasing a different way to construct objects.

(iii)

27_F4.mg
constructs copies of
\PSL_{2}(27)<\mathrm{F}_{4}(547)
.

(iv)

27_F4_13_main.bash
constructs copies of
\PSL_{2}(27)<\mathrm{F}_{4}(13)
. It will automatically load, in order:

(i)

27_F4_13_p1.mg
.

(ii)

27_F4_13_loadtest.mg
, once for each subdivision of the computation; the number of parallel processes is defined by the parameter
part
in the file
27_F4_13_main.bash
.

(iii)

27_F4_13_p2.mg
.

(v)

37_E7.mg
constructs copies of
\PSL_{2}(37)<\mathrm{E}_{7}(5329)
.

(vi)

37_E7_Borel_conjugacy.mg
constructs copies of
37\rtimes 18<\mathrm{E}_{7}(149)
.

(vii)

29_E7_main.bash
constructs copies of
\PSL_{2}(29)<\mathrm{E}_{7}(36541)
. It will automatically load, in order:

(i)

29_E7.mg
.

(ii)

29_E7_loaddet.mg
, twice, once for each possible determinant.

(iii)

29_E7_loadtest.mg
, twice, once for each determinant.

(viii)

41_E8.mg
constructs copies of
\PSL_{2}(41)<\mathrm{E}_{8}(5741)
.

(ix)

49_E8.mg
constructs copies of
\PSL_{2}(49)<\mathrm{E}_{8}(1009)
.

(x)

61_E8.mg
constructs copies of
\PSL_{2}(61)<\mathrm{E}_{8}(1831)
.

(xi)

Alt6_F4.mg
constructs copies of
\alt_{6}<\mathrm{F}_{4}(19)
.

(xii)

Alt6_A2A2A2_3-decomposition.mg
is used for
5.2.4
and the subsequent comparison between
M(\mathrm{E}_{6})\!\!\downarrow_{\alt_{6}}
and
M(\mathrm{E}_{6})\!\!\downarrow_{\alt_{5}}
.

(xiii)

Sp8_trilinear_form.mg
constructs the unique (up to scalars)
\mathrm{E}_{6}\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear form on
M(\mathrm{E}_{6})\!\!\downarrow_{\mathrm{C}_{4}}
.

(xiv)

Alt6_C4.mg
is used for
Section

5.3
.

Additionally, the following files define functions used in the above computations:

(i)

CompareAHom.mg
is used to study the restriction of a symmetric trilinear form as described in
Section

3.8
.

(ii)

exp.mg
computes
\exp
and
\log
of a given matrix, assuming that the power series required has finitely many terms.

(iii)

GenLinearComb.mg
is a naive implementation of
[
30
, Computation 3.9]
.

(iv)

GHN.mg
constructs elements of a Lie group as described in
Section

3.1
.

(v)

LDU.mg
performs LDU decompositions (
Section

3.5
), and writes matrices as elements of a group of type
\mathrm{A}_{n}
(
Section

3.6
).

(vi)

MapToMatrix.mg
allows to obtain the matrix corresponding to a change of basis of a given object that has an underlying vector space.

(vii)

Membership.mg
is an implementation of the membership test described in
Section

3.2
.

(viii)

RescaleChevalley.mg
rescales a given Chevalley basis to have standard structure constants, see
Section

3.4.1
.

(ix)

TrilinearForm.mg
contains various functions to construct
G\text{-invariant}
symmetric trilinear forms on
kG\text{-modules}
as described in
Section

3.7
, and operate with them.

(x)

WeakChevalleyBasis.mg
constructs a Chevalley basis that diagonalises a given semisimple element, as described in
Section

3.4.1
.
